Rajasthan History — Master Notebook

Political, administrative and modern history of Rajasthan from prehistory to integration (1956), written for RAS Prelims and Mains and mapped line by line to the 2026 syllabus. Art, culture and heritage are a separate notebook.

How to use each unit: read 1–4 for understanding, use 5 for Prelims revision, 6–7 for Mains practice, and 8–9 the day before the exam. Greyed units in the contents are planned but not written yet.

Unit R01 · All periods

Sources and Historians of Rajasthan

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR2 Sources of Rajasthan history: archaeological, archival, literary and numismatic
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH1 Prehistoric cultures, ancient sites; political and cultural achievements of rulers (to 18th century)

Topics this unit covers

  • Sources of Rajasthan history: archaeological, archival, literary and numismatic

Inscriptions, coins and chroniclers are asked almost every year in Prelims, and 2-mark Mains questions often name a single inscription (Ghosundi was asked in 2016).

2The big picture

Everything we know about Rajasthan's past comes from four kinds of evidence: what is dug up or carved (archaeology and inscriptions), what was minted (coins), what was written down by governments (archival records) and what was composed by poets, bards and chroniclers (literature). Each kind has strengths and biases. A good answer always asks: who produced this source, when, and for whom?

Rajasthan is unusually rich in two kinds: prashastis (eulogy-inscriptions of rulers) and khyats (prose chronicles in Rajasthani), and its state archives at Bikaner preserve huge quantities of court records.

This unit is a toolkit. Every later unit uses these sources; when you meet an inscription there, come back here to see where it fits.

  1. 3rd c. BCEAshoka's Bhabru (Bairat) edict — earliest datable royal inscription found in Rajasthan
  2. 2nd c. BCEGhosundi inscription — Bhagavata (Vaishnava) cult
  3. 646 CESamoli inscription of Guhil Shiladitya
  4. 837 / 861 CEMandore and Ghatiyala inscriptions of the Pratiharas
  5. 1170 CEBijolia inscription of Chauhan Someshwar
  6. 1460 CEKumbhalgarh and Kirti Stambha prashastis of Rana Kumbha
  7. c. 1660sMuhnot Nainsi writes his Khyat and the Marwar ra Pargana ri Vigat
  8. 1676 CERaj Prashasti at Rajsamand
  9. 1829–1832James Tod's Annals and Antiquities of Rajasthan
  10. 1886 onwardsKaviraj Shyamaldas's Vir Vinod (Mewar)
  11. 1955Rajasthan State Archives set up (moved to Bikaner in 1960)

3Core notes

R01.1 Archaeological sources: sites and inscriptions

Excavated sites (Kalibangan, Ahar, Ganeshwar, Bairat and others) tell us about societies with no written records; they are covered in units R02–R04.

Inscriptions are the backbone of early and medieval history. They are usually precisely dated (in the Vikram Samvat, which runs about 57 years ahead of CE), name kings and genealogies, and record grants, temples and wars. Their bias: most were composed for rulers and praise them.

InscriptionPlace / dateWhy it is important
Bhabru (Bairat–Calcutta) edictBairat (Viratnagar), 3rd c. BCEAshoka declares faith in the Buddha, Dhamma and Sangha; found by Captain Burt (1837), now in Kolkata
Barli inscriptionAjmer districtOften called the oldest inscription of Rajasthan (Prakrit, Brahmi); its early date is debated
Ghosundi inscriptionNagari, Chittorgarh, 2nd c. BCESanskrit in Brahmi; earliest evidence of the Bhagavata cult (Sankarshana–Vasudeva) in Rajasthan; records an Ashvamedha by Sarvatata
Badva yupa inscriptionsKota, 238 CEMaukhari rulers' sacrificial pillars — evidence of Vedic sacrifices in the 3rd century
SamoliUdaipur, 646 CEGuhil king Shiladitya; earliest dated Guhil record; mining activity at Jawar
AparajitaNagda, 661 CEGuhil king Aparajita
MandoreJodhpur, 837 CEPratihara Bauk; genealogy of the Mandore Pratiharas
GhatiyalaJodhpur, 861 CEPratihara Kakkuk; trade and settlement
BijoliaBhilwara, 1170 CEChauhan Someshwar; Chauhan genealogy from Vasudeva; calls the Chauhans "vatsagotra" Brahmins
ChirwaUdaipur, 1273 CEGuhil Samarsimha; officials and administration of Mewar
RanakpurPali, 1439 CEKumbha's reign; genealogy of Mewar; Jain temple building
Kirti Stambha prashastiChittor, 1460 CEKumbha's titles, victories and works
Kumbhalgarh prashastiKumbhalgarh, 1460 CEHistory of Mewar; describes Bappa Rawal as a Brahmin (vipra)
Raj PrashastiRajsamand, 1676 CERanchhod Bhatt Tailang; 25 slabs; Mewar history and the 1615 treaty

Persian inscriptions on mosques and tombs (for example at the Adhai Din ka Jhonpra, Ajmer) record the Sultanate and Mughal presence.

R01.2 Numismatic sources: coins

Coins reveal rulers unknown from other sources, the extent of trade, and the economy (metal purity, hoards).

  • Punch-marked coins (silver, c. 6th–2nd c. BCE) at Rairh (Tonk) and Bairat.
  • Indo-Greek coins, including those of Menander, in a hoard at Bairat.
  • Tribal republic coins: the Malavas ("Malavanam Jayah", "victory of the Malavas") at Nagar and Rairh (Tonk); the Yaudheyas in the north.
  • Gupta gold coins: the Bayana hoard (found 1946, Bharatpur), about 1,800 coins — the largest Gupta hoard known.
  • Gadhaiya coins (Indo-Sassanian type) of the early medieval period.
  • Chauhan coins, including those issued in the name of Somalladevi, queen of Ajayaraja.
  • Princely-state coins of the 18th–19th centuries: e.g. Vijayshahi (Jodhpur), Jharshahi (Jaipur), Swaroopshahi (Mewar). British-minted rupees (kaldar) gradually replaced them.

R01.3 Archival sources: state records

From the 16th century onwards, Rajput states kept large bureaucratic records — the most objective source for administration, revenue and society. Most are held by the Rajasthan State Archives, established in 1955 and moved to Bikaner in 1960.

RecordWhat it is
BahiRegister (e.g. Hakikat bahi of daily events, Byav bahi of marriages, Kotwali bahi of town administration)
PattaDeed granting land or jagir, with its terms
ParwanaAdministrative order issued by the ruler or officials
KharitaFormal letter between rulers (sent in a cloth bag)
ArzdashtPetition or report sent to a superior, e.g. to the Mughal court
Vakil reportLetters from a state's agent (vakil) at the Mughal court or another state
AkhbaratMughal court newsletters, copies of which reached Rajput states

Archival records answer the questions that inscriptions and poems ignore: who paid what tax, how jagirs were granted, how towns were policed. RAS Mains questions on revenue and administration (asked in 2023 and 2024) rely on this material — see unit R14.

R01.4 Literary sources

Sanskrit works, usually court poems:

  • Prithviraj Vijay (Jayanaka) — the Chauhans up to Prithviraj III.
  • Hammir Mahakavya (Nayachandra Suri) — Hammir of Ranthambhor.
  • Amarkavya (Ranchhod Bhatt) — Mewar, especially Amar Singh.

Rajasthani works: raso (heroic poems), khyat (prose chronicles), vat (tales), vachanika.

  • Prithviraj Raso (attributed to Chand Bardai) — its present text is much later and historically unreliable in detail.
  • Bisaldev Raso (Narpati Nalha) — Chauhan Vigraharaja IV.
  • Achaldas Khichi ri Vachanika (Shivdas Gadan) — the 1423 siege of Gagron.
  • Kanhadde Prabandh (Padmanabh) — Kanhaddev of Jalore against Alauddin.
  • Nainsi ri Khyat and Marwar ra Pargana ri Vigat (Muhnot Nainsi) — the Vigat is a pargana-by-pargana survey of Marwar's villages, revenue and castes, almost a gazetteer.
  • Bankidas ri Khyat; Dayaldas ri Khyat (Bikaner).
  • Vansh Bhaskar (Suryamal Mishran, Bundi) — history of the Hadas.

Persian works: Tabaqat-i-Nasiri (Minhaj), Khazain-ul-Futuh (Amir Khusrau), Baburnama, Akbarnama and Ain-i-Akbari (Abul Fazl), Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh (Badayuni), Jahangir's memoirs. They give the imperial view of Rajput states.

R01.5 The historians of Rajasthan

HistorianWorkNote
Muhnot Nainsi (1610–1670)Nainsi ri Khyat; Marwar ra Pargana ri VigatDewan of Jaswant Singh of Marwar; called the "Abul Fazl of Rajputana" by Munshi Devi Prasad
Suryamal Mishran (19th c.)Vansh Bhaskar; Veer SatsaiCourt poet of Bundi
Col. James Tod (1782–1835)Annals and Antiquities of Rajasthan (1829, 1832); Travels in Western IndiaPolitical Agent for the western Rajput states (1818–22); first connected history in English; romantic and pro-Mewar
Kaviraj ShyamaldasVir VinodOfficial history of Mewar written under Maharana Sajjan Singh; uses documents critically
Gaurishankar Hirachand Ojha (1863–1947)Histories of Rajputana states; Bharatiya Prachin LipimalaFounded modern, source-based Rajasthan history; expert in ancient scripts
Dasharath SharmaEarly Chauhan DynastiesAuthority on the Chauhans
G.N. SharmaSocial Life in Medieval Rajasthan; Mewar and the Mughal EmperorsSocial and political history

How reliable is Tod? Tod gathered bardic accounts and documents and gave Rajasthan its first full history, but he romanticised Rajput chivalry, compared Rajput society to European feudalism, and relied on bards for dates. Use him for tradition, and verify dates from inscriptions and archives.

4What historians say

Using sources critically. Prashastis and court poems were written to praise patrons; bardic literature mixes memory with legend (the Prithviraj Raso is the classic example); Persian chronicles see Rajputs as subordinate chiefs; Tod saw them through European romantic eyes. Modern historians — Ojha, Dasharath Sharma, G.N. Sharma — compare these with inscriptions, coins and archival records. When sources disagree (on the outcome of Haldighati, for instance), a good Mains answer names both sides.

Feudalism debate. Tod described Rajput states as "feudal" on the European model. Later historians argue that Rajput polity rested on kinship and clan brotherhood (bhai-bant) more than on contracts between lord and vassal — a useful point for questions on administration (unit R14).

5Prelims fact bank

  • Bhabru (Bairat) edict of Ashoka was found by Captain Burt in 1837 and is kept in Kolkata.
  • Ghosundi inscription: Nagari (Chittorgarh), 2nd c. BCE, Sanskrit in Brahmi; Bhagavata cult and Ashvamedha by Sarvatata.
  • Barli inscription (Ajmer) is traditionally called the oldest inscription of Rajasthan; its date is debated.
  • Badva yupa inscriptions (Kota, 238 CE) belong to the Maukharis.
  • Samoli inscription (646 CE) is the earliest dated record of the Guhils (Shiladitya).
  • Mandore inscription (837 CE) of Bauk and Ghatiyala inscription (861 CE) of Kakkuk record the Mandore Pratiharas.
  • Bijolia inscription (1170 CE) of Someshwar calls the Chauhans vatsagotra Brahmins.
  • Chirwa inscription (1273 CE) belongs to Guhil Samarsimha.
  • Kumbhalgarh prashasti (1460) calls Bappa Rawal a vipra (Brahmin).
  • Raj Prashasti (1676) at Rajsamand: Ranchhod Bhatt Tailang, 25 slabs.
  • Bayana hoard of Gupta gold coins was found in 1946.
  • Malava coins with "Malavanam Jayah" come from Nagar and Rairh (Tonk).
  • Vijayshahi = Jodhpur coin; Jharshahi = Jaipur coin; Swaroopshahi = Mewar coin.
  • Rajasthan State Archives: set up in 1955, shifted to Bikaner in 1960.
  • Kharita = formal letter between rulers; arzdasht = petition or report to a superior; patta = grant deed.
  • Muhnot Nainsi wrote Nainsi ri Khyat and Marwar ra Pargana ri Vigat; called the "Abul Fazl of Rajputana".
  • Hammir Mahakavya was written by Nayachandra Suri; Prithviraj Vijay by Jayanaka.
  • Kanhadde Prabandh by Padmanabh; Achaldas Khichi ri Vachanika by Shivdas Gadan; Bisaldev Raso by Narpati Nalha.
  • Vansh Bhaskar by Suryamal Mishran of Bundi.
  • Vir Vinod by Kaviraj Shyamaldas (Mewar, under Sajjan Singh).
  • James Tod published the Annals and Antiquities of Rajasthan in two volumes (1829, 1832).
  • G.H. Ojha wrote Bharatiya Prachin Lipimala.

Exam traps

  • Ghosundi is about Vaishnavism (Bhagavata); Bijolia is about the Chauhans. Do not swap.
  • Bhabru edict was found at Bairat (Viratnagar); "Bhabru" is the nearby village it is named after.
  • Vir Vinod (Shyamaldas, Mewar) is not Vansh Bhaskar (Suryamal Mishran, Bundi).
  • Nainsi's Vigat is a statistical survey; his Khyat is a historical chronicle.
  • Rajasthan State Archives are at Bikaner, not Jaipur (it began at Jaipur in 1955).
  • Vikram Samvat dates are about 57 years ahead of CE; Shaka dates are 78 years behind CE.

7Mains answer writing

2 marks · 15 words · RAS Mains 2016 (Special) What is the significance of the Ghosundi inscription?

How to frame it

  • Place and date
  • One-line significance
Model answer (15 words)

2nd-century BCE Sanskrit inscription from Nagari (Chittorgarh): earliest evidence of Bhagavata (Vaishnava) worship in Rajasthan.

2 marks · 15 words What was the Marwar ra Pargana ri Vigat?

How to frame it

  • Author
  • Nature of the work
Model answer (15 words)

Muhnot Nainsi's 17th-century pargana-wise survey of Marwar's villages, revenue, castes and produce — a gazetteer.

5 marks · 50 words Assess the importance of archival sources for the history of medieval Rajasthan.

How to frame it

  • Kinds of records
  • What they reveal
  • Where held
Model answer (48 words)

Bahis, pattas, parwanas, kharitas, arzdashts and vakil reports record daily administration, jagir grants, revenue, prices and diplomacy. Unlike prashastis and bardic poems, they were working documents, so they are more objective. Preserved mainly in the Rajasthan State Archives, Bikaner, they are the basis for studying administration and society.

10 marks · 100 words Critically evaluate the contribution of Col. James Tod to the historiography of Rajasthan.

How to frame it

  • Who he was and what he wrote
  • Contribution
  • Limitations
  • Balanced conclusion
Model answer (85 words)

As Political Agent of the western Rajput states (1818–22), Tod collected bardic chronicles, inscriptions and documents and published the Annals and Antiquities of Rajasthan (1829–32), the first connected history of the region. He made Rajput history known worldwide and preserved many traditions. But he relied heavily on bards, romanticised Rajput chivalry, favoured Mewar, misdated events and forced Rajput polity into a European "feudal" model. Later historians such as Ojha and Dasharath Sharma corrected him from inscriptions and archives. Tod remains a valuable but uncritical pioneer.

8One-screen revision card

  • Four kinds of sources: archaeological (sites and inscriptions), numismatic, archival, literary.
  • Key inscriptions: Bhabru, Ghosundi (Bhagavata), Samoli 646, Mandore 837, Ghatiyala 861, Bijolia 1170, Chirwa 1273, Kumbhalgarh 1460, Raj Prashasti 1676.
  • Coins: punch-marked (Rairh), Indo-Greek (Bairat), Malava (Nagar), Bayana Gupta hoard (1946), Vijayshahi/Jharshahi/Swaroopshahi.
  • Archives at Bikaner: bahi, patta, parwana, kharita, arzdasht, vakil report.
  • Literature: Prithviraj Vijay, Hammir Mahakavya, Kanhadde Prabandh, Nainsi's Khyat and Vigat, Vansh Bhaskar.
  • Historians: Nainsi, Tod, Shyamaldas, Ojha, Dasharath Sharma, G.N. Sharma.

9Self-test

  1. Which inscription is the earliest evidence of the Bhagavata cult in Rajasthan?

    Answer

    Ghosundi (Nagari, Chittorgarh), 2nd century BCE.

  2. Which inscription is the earliest dated record of the Guhils?

    Answer

    Samoli inscription of Shiladitya, 646 CE.

  3. Which inscription describes the Chauhans as vatsagotra Brahmins, and of which ruler is it?

    Answer

    Bijolia inscription (1170 CE) of Someshwar.

  4. Where are the Rajasthan State Archives, and when were they set up?

    Answer

    Bikaner; set up in 1955 (at Jaipur) and moved to Bikaner in 1960.

  5. What is a kharita?

    Answer

    A formal letter exchanged between rulers.

  6. Who wrote the Marwar ra Pargana ri Vigat?

    Answer

    Muhnot Nainsi.

  7. Which historian wrote Vansh Bhaskar, and for which state?

    Answer

    Suryamal Mishran, Bundi.

  8. In which years were the two volumes of Tod's Annals published?

    Answer

    1829 and 1832.

  9. What legend appears on Malava coins?

    Answer

    "Malavanam Jayah" — victory of the Malavas.

  10. Name the coin of Jodhpur state.

    Answer

    Vijayshahi.

Unit R02 · Palaeolithic to Mesolithic (c. 5 lakh – 3000 BCE)

Prehistoric Rajasthan

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR1 Pre-historical sites of Rajasthan — Palaeolithic to Chalcolithic and Bronze Age; society and culture of ancient Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH1 Prehistoric cultures, ancient sites; political and cultural achievements of rulers (to 18th century)

Topics this unit covers

  • Pre-historical sites of Rajasthan — from Palaeolithic to Chalcolithic and Bronze Age

Site–river–district matching is a Prelims favourite. RAS Mains 2018 asked a 10-mark question on Bagor, Kalibangan and Ahar (answered in unit R03).

2The big picture

Long before cities or kings, Rajasthan was home to hunter-gatherers. Their story is told by stone tools, not writing. Archaeologists divide this long period by the tools people made: large hand-axes in the Palaeolithic (Old Stone Age), tiny blades called microliths in the Mesolithic (Middle Stone Age), and polished tools with farming in the Neolithic.

Rajasthan's river valleys — the Banas, Berach, Chambal and Luni — and the edges of the Thar desert are rich in such sites. The key idea: Rajasthan's climate has changed. The Thar was wetter at times, with lakes and rivers that attracted early humans; dry phases emptied it.

Bagor (Bhilwara) is the site to know best. It shows the slow change from hunting to herding, one of the biggest transitions in human history, and it is regularly asked in exams.

  1. 1870C.A. Hacket reports Palaeolithic tools at Jaipur and Indragarh (Bundi)
  2. Lower PalaeolithicHand-axes and cleavers along the Banas, Berach, Chambal; Didwana dune sites
  3. Middle PalaeolithicFlake tools, especially in the Luni valley
  4. Upper PalaeolithicBlade tools; the Thar alternates between wet and dry phases
  5. c. 5000 BCEBagor: microliths and early evidence of animal domestication
  6. 1967–1970V.N. Misra excavates Bagor (and works at Tilwara)

3Core notes

R02.1 The Palaeolithic: first toolmakers

The first stone tools from Rajasthan were reported by C.A. Hacket in 1870, from Jaipur and Indragarh (Bundi). Since then hundreds of sites have been found.

PhaseTypical toolsRajasthan evidence
Lower PalaeolithicHand-axes, cleavers, choppers (Acheulian)Banas, Berach, Chambal and Gambhiri valleys; Didwana (Nagaur) dune and lake-edge sites, among the oldest stratified sites in India
Middle PalaeolithicScrapers and points made on flakesLuni valley (western Rajasthan); Banas basin
Upper PalaeolithicBlades and burinsScattered sites; desert edges

These people lived by hunting and gathering, moved with the seasons, and camped near water. Their tools were made mainly of quartzite.

R02.2 The Mesolithic: microliths and the first herders

After the last Ice Age, warmer conditions brought smaller animals and new resources. People made microliths — tiny geometric blades, often set in wooden or bone handles to make arrows and sickles.

Bagor (Bhilwara district, on the Kothari river, at the mound called Mahasatiyon ka Tila) was excavated by V.N. Misra (1967–70). It is one of the largest Mesolithic sites in India. It has three phases:

  1. Microliths with bones of both wild and domesticated animals (sheep and goat) — around 5000 BCE, among the earliest evidence of animal domestication in India.
  2. Copper tools appear alongside microliths, linking Bagor to the Chalcolithic cultures (unit R03).
  3. Iron and pottery in the latest phase.

Burials, stone-paved floors and hut traces show that people stayed for long periods.

Tilwara (Barmer, on the Luni) is another important Mesolithic site with microliths and animal bones. Other Mesolithic sites lie around Sojat, Didwana and the Banas valley.

Bagor shows that the move from hunting to food production was gradual: people hunted, herded and later used metal at the same site over thousands of years.

R02.3 Rock art and the Neolithic

Rock paintings in shelters along the Chambal and its tributaries — in Kota, Bundi, Jhalawar and Bharatpur districts — show animals, hunting scenes and human figures, comparable to the better-known Bhimbetka paintings in Madhya Pradesh.

A fully separate Neolithic phase is not well marked in Rajasthan: microlithic herders like those at Bagor lead fairly directly into the copper-using farming cultures (Ahar, Ganeshwar), described in unit R03.

4What historians say

Systematic prehistoric research in Rajasthan owes most to V.N. Misra and the Deccan College, Pune, who excavated Bagor and Tilwara and studied the Didwana sites (with Indian and foreign colleagues). Their work showed that the Thar desert was not always a barrier: wet phases made it habitable, so human history here is closely tied to climate history.

Dating is approximate. Absolute dates come from scientific methods (radiocarbon, luminescence); textbooks often round them, so in exams prefer the relative order (Lower → Middle → Upper Palaeolithic → Mesolithic) over exact numbers.

5Prelims fact bank

  • C.A. Hacket first reported Palaeolithic tools in Rajasthan (1870) at Jaipur and Indragarh (Bundi).
  • Lower Palaeolithic tools in Rajasthan: hand-axes and cleavers along the Banas, Berach and Chambal.
  • Didwana (Nagaur) has some of the oldest stratified Palaeolithic sites in India.
  • Singi Talav, a lake near Didwana, has Acheulian (Lower Palaeolithic) hand-axe levels.
  • Middle Palaeolithic flake tools are typical of the Luni valley.
  • Bagor is in Bhilwara district on the Kothari river.
  • Bagor was excavated by V.N. Misra (1967–70); the mound is Mahasatiyon ka Tila.
  • Bagor is among the largest Mesolithic sites in India and gives early evidence of animal domestication (c. 5000 BCE).
  • Tilwara (Barmer) is a Mesolithic site on the Luni river.
  • Microliths are tiny geometric stone blades typical of the Mesolithic.
  • Rock paintings are found in shelters in Kota, Bundi, Jhalawar and Bharatpur districts.

Exam traps

  • Bagor is on the Kothari river (Bhilwara), not the Banas or Berach.
  • Bagor is Mesolithic (with later copper and iron phases); Ahar and Ganeshwar are Chalcolithic.
  • Tilwara is in Barmer (Luni); Didwana is in Nagaur.
  • V.N. Misra excavated Bagor; R.C. Agrawal excavated Ganeshwar and Ahar (unit R03).

Unit R03 · c. 3000 – 1500 BCE

Chalcolithic and Bronze Age Cultures

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR1 Pre-historical sites of Rajasthan — Palaeolithic to Chalcolithic and Bronze Age; society and culture of ancient Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH1 Prehistoric cultures, ancient sites; political and cultural achievements of rulers (to 18th century)

Topics this unit covers

  • Pre-historical sites of Rajasthan — Chalcolithic and Bronze Age
  • Sources: archaeological

Tier A. Kalibangan, Ahar and Ganeshwar are among the most-asked topics in all of Rajasthan history. RAS Mains 2018 asked a 10-mark question on Bagor, Kalibangan and Ahar; RAS Prelims asked on Ahar (2021), copper hoards (2023) and Ojiyana (2024).

2The big picture

Between about 3000 and 1500 BCE, Rajasthan's people learned to use copper alongside stone, and two very different kinds of society grew up in different parts of the state. In the north, on the now-dry Ghaggar (identified by many with the ancient Sarasvati), Kalibangan became a town of the Harappan (Indus) civilisation, with planned streets, seals, standard bricks and long-distance trade. In the south-east, in the Banas valley of Mewar, the Ahar–Banas culture (Ahar, Gilund, Balathal, Ojiyana) was a culture of farming villages with its own pottery, stone-founded houses and copper smelting. Between them, on the Khetri copper belt of the Aravallis, Ganeshwar made copper tools on a large scale and probably supplied the Harappans. Geography explains the pattern: water and flat land in the north, fertile river valleys and copper ores in the Aravalli south.

The contrast is the key to any Mains answer: Kalibangan is urban and Harappan; Ahar is rural and regional; Ganeshwar is a copper-producing centre that linked both to resources. For Prelims, match each site to its district, river, excavator and signature find; the traps below are built on mixing these up.

  1. c. 3000–2600 BCEEarly (pre-) Harappan phase at Kalibangan; Sothi-type pottery
  2. c. 2600 BCEEarthquake evidence at Kalibangan; the early phase ends
  3. c. 2600–1900 BCEMature Harappan Kalibangan
  4. 3rd millennium BCEGaneshwar copper industry flourishes
  5. c. 2800/2500–1500 BCEAhar–Banas culture: Ahar, Gilund, Balathal, Ojiyana
  6. c. 2000 BCEBalathal skeleton with the oldest skeletal evidence of leprosy
  7. c. 1917–19L.P. Tessitori notices the Kalibangan mounds
  8. 1952A. Ghosh identifies Kalibangan as Harappan
  9. 1953 onwardsAhar excavated (A.K. Vyas, R.C. Agrawal, H.D. Sankalia)
  10. 1959–60B.B. Lal excavates Gilund
  11. 1961–69B.B. Lal and B.K. Thapar excavate Kalibangan
  12. 1977R.C. Agrawal excavates Ganeshwar
  13. 1993 onwardsV.N. Misra excavates Balathal
  14. c. 2000B.R. Meena and Alok Tripathi excavate Ojiyana

3Core notes

R03.1 What changed: from stone to copper

The Mesolithic people of Bagor (unit R02) hunted, gathered and began to herd sheep and goats, using tiny stone tools (microliths). By the 4th and 3rd millennia BCE, people in Rajasthan were settling in villages, growing crops, making pottery on a wheel and, above all, using copper. Copper changed what people could make: sharper axes for clearing land, fish-hooks, arrowheads and ornaments. It also changed how societies were organised, because ore had to be mined, smelted and traded, which needed skilled workers and exchange between regions.

Chalcolithic and Bronze Age. Chalcolithic means "copper–stone age" (Greek chalkos, copper, and lithos, stone): people used copper tools while still using stone ones, and lived mostly in farming villages. Bronze is copper alloyed with tin (or arsenic), which is harder than copper. In Indian usage the Bronze Age usually means the urban Harappan civilisation, which used bronze, writing and cities. So in Rajasthan, Kalibangan is "Bronze Age" (Harappan and urban), while Ahar and Ganeshwar are "Chalcolithic" (copper-using, non-Harappan and village-based), though they lived at the same time.

Rajasthan was well placed for this change. The Aravalli hills hold copper ores, especially in the Khetri belt of the north-east (Jhunjhunu and Neem ka Thana area) and around Udaipur in the south. The Ghaggar in the north gave water and flat, soft soil for farming, while the Banas and its tributaries gave fertile valleys in Mewar. Where the copper, water and land were, settlements grew.

R03.2 Kalibangan: the Harappan town on the Ghaggar

Location and discovery. Kalibangan is in Hanumangarh district, on the left (south) bank of the Ghaggar. The name means "black bangles", after the bangle fragments that cover the mounds. The Italian scholar L.P. Tessitori noticed the mounds around 1917–19 without recognising them as Harappan. A. Ghosh identified the site as Harappan in 1952, and B.B. Lal and B.K. Thapar excavated it from 1961 to 1969.

The early (pre-Harappan) settlement, from about 3000 BCE, was a walled village of mud-brick with its own pottery, named Sothi ware after a site in the same district. Just outside it the excavators found a ploughed field with furrows crossing each other at right angles, among the earliest ploughed fields known anywhere. Farmers in the area still plough this way to grow two crops (such as mustard and gram) in the same field. Around 2600 BCE this phase ended with damage the excavators read as an earthquake.

The mature Harappan town (c. 2600–1900 BCE) was rebuilt on a plan. It had a citadel on the west and a lower town on the east, and both were walled, which is unusual, since most Harappan cities walled only the citadel. Streets ran on a grid. Houses were mostly of mud-brick, with baked brick used only where water mattered: drains, wells and bathing floors. On the citadel, rows of fire altars (pits with ash and a central clay stele) point to rituals involving fire, which some link with later Vedic practice. Other finds include Harappan seals, a cylinder seal of Mesopotamian type, terracotta, copper tools, burials, and a skull reported to show surgical holes (trepanation).

Why Kalibangan matters for the origin debate. Scholars once thought the Harappan cities appeared suddenly, perhaps through ideas from Mesopotamia. At Kalibangan the excavators found a local early-Harappan village directly below the Harappan town, with planning, walls and a ploughed field already in place. This supports the view that the civilisation grew out of local cultures over centuries. Kalibangan also proves that the Harappan world extended deep into Rajasthan along the Ghaggar.

Decline. Kalibangan was abandoned around 1900 BCE. The most likely cause is that the Ghaggar, which had supported dense Harappan settlement, lost its water as rivers shifted and the climate became drier; the town could not survive without it. The many other Harappan-related sites along the river in Hanumangarh and Sri Ganganagar districts (Sothi among them) share this story.

R03.3 Ganeshwar: the copper workshop of the Aravallis

Ganeshwar is in the Neem ka Thana area (formerly Sikar district), on the seasonal Kantli river, at the edge of the Khetri copper belt. R.C. Agrawal and Vijay Kumar excavated it in 1977. They found more than a thousand copper objects: arrowheads, spearheads, fish-hooks, axes, chisels and bangles, made of nearly pure copper, together with ochre-coloured pottery, sometimes painted. Many sites with similar finds are grouped as the Ganeshwar–Jodhpura cultural complex.

Why does Ganeshwar matter? Copper objects of the Ganeshwar type turn up at Harappan sites, so Ganeshwar probably supplied copper to the Harappan cities. That makes it a link between the raw materials of the Aravallis and the urban economy of the Indus. It is often called the "mother of the copper-age cultures" (ताम्रयुगीन सभ्यताओं की जननी). The many fish-hooks show that the streams of the area were then perennial, in a wetter climate than today.

Copper also turns up in hoards: caches of copper objects found together, often by chance, rather than in a settlement. Such hoards have been reported from several places in Rajasthan, including Kurada (Nagaur region), Jharol (Udaipur) and Sabania; RAS Prelims 2023 asked what these three have in common (copper hoards). Hoards show how valued copper was, and that it moved across the region.

R03.4 The Ahar–Banas culture: the farming villages of Mewar

In south-eastern Rajasthan (Udaipur, Rajsamand, Chittorgarh and Bhilwara districts) a separate culture flourished from roughly 2800/2500 to 1500 BCE. It is named after its type-site, Ahar, and the Banas basin, and more than a hundred of its sites are known. It was not Harappan: it had no cities, no writing and no standard bricks. But it was not simply backward either. It was a well-organised society of farmers, herders and copper workers that lasted longer than the Harappan towns.

Black-and-red ware. The signature of the Ahar culture is pottery that is black inside and on the rim, red outside, painted with white lines and dots. The two colours come from the way it was fired: the pots were placed upside down, so the inside and rim, cut off from air, turned black while the outside, exposed to air, turned red. Black-and-red ware was later made in many parts of India, so the white painted designs are what mark the Ahar variety. Pottery like this is how archaeologists recognise a culture and date its sites when there is no writing.

Ahar (Udaipur, on the Ahar river, a tributary of the Banas) was known in later times as Tambavati ("city of copper") and Aghatpur; its mound is called Dhulkot. It was excavated by A.K. Vyas (1953), R.C. Agrawal (1956) and H.D. Sankalia (1961–62). Houses stood on stone foundations with mud walls; several hearths (chulhas) in a single house suggest large joint families. People smelted copper from local ores, grew crops including rice (the RPSC key to the 2021 Prelims question confirms rice was known) and made terracotta bulls. Metal-working was a regular part of the economy.

Gilund (Rajsamand, on the Banas) was excavated by B.B. Lal (1959–60) and later by Indian and American teams (V. Shinde and G.L. Possehl). Large mud-brick structures and a hoard of clay seal impressions point to organised storage and some form of administration, more than a simple village. Balathal (Vallabhnagar, Udaipur), excavated by V.N. Misra from 1993, was a fortified settlement with a large mud-brick enclosure and pieces of woven cloth; a skeleton of about 2000 BCE shows leprosy, the oldest skeletal evidence of the disease in the world. Ojiyana (Bhilwara district), excavated around 2000 by B.R. Meena and Alok Tripathi (RAS Prelims 2024), extended the culture into the hilly Aravalli zone; it is known for terracotta bull figurines painted in white.

KalibanganAharGaneshwar
CultureHarappan (urban)Ahar–Banas Chalcolithic (rural)Ganeshwar–Jodhpura Chalcolithic

Signature findsPloughed field, earthquake, fire altars, sealsCopper smelting, many hearthsOver a thousand copper objects

What these cultures left. The Chalcolithic villages faded by about 1500 BCE, probably as the climate dried, but their way of life did not vanish. Farming in the Banas valley, copper mining in the Aravallis (Khetri is still a copper centre) and the skill of local metalworkers continued into the iron-using cultures and the historic towns of Rajasthan, such as Bairath and Noh (unit R04).

How this unit connects.

  • Before: the hunters and herders of Bagor and the Mesolithic (R02); prehistory in India (I02).
  • The wider Harappan world: Harappan town planning, trade, seals and decline (I03); Harappan cities in architecture (I48) and the bronze Dancing Girl, made by the lost-wax method (I49).
  • After: iron, the Painted Grey Ware and the historic sites of Bairath, Noh and Nagari (R04).
  • The Sarasvati debate: the Ghaggar's identity links to Vedic geography (I04).
  • Sources: how archaeology is used when there is no writing (R01, I01).
  • Geography: the Aravalli copper belt and the Ghaggar and Banas river systems.

4What historians say

The Sarasvati question. Many archaeologists identify the Ghaggar–Hakra with the Rigvedic Sarasvati and note the dense cluster of Harappan sites along it, including Kalibangan; the identification and the river's history are still debated, so state it as a view, not a fact.

Origins of urbanism. Kalibangan's early phase shows a planned, fortified settlement before the mature Harappan town, supporting the view (B.B. Lal, B.K. Thapar, M.R. Mughal) that the civilisation grew out of local early Harappan cultures rather than arriving from outside.

Regional cultures. The Ahar–Banas culture shows that Chalcolithic India was not just a fringe of the Harappans; Gilund's seal impressions suggest considerable local organisation (Possehl and Shinde).

5Prelims fact bank

  • Kalibangan: Hanumangarh district, left bank of the Ghaggar; name means "black bangles".
  • Tessitori noticed the Kalibangan mounds (c. 1917–19); A. Ghosh identified the site as Harappan (1952).
  • B.B. Lal and B.K. Thapar excavated Kalibangan in 1961–69.
  • Kalibangan's early Harappan phase has a ploughed field with criss-cross furrows.
  • Kalibangan has evidence interpreted as an earthquake around 2600 BCE.
  • Kalibangan has fire altars and both a fortified citadel and a fortified lower town.
  • Sothi (Hanumangarh) gives its name to early Harappan pottery.
  • Ganeshwar: Neem ka Thana (formerly Sikar), Kantli river, Khetri copper belt.
  • Ganeshwar was excavated by R.C. Agrawal and Vijay Kumar (1977).
  • Ganeshwar is called the "mother of the copper-age cultures" and probably supplied copper to Harappan sites.
  • Copper hoards have been found at Kurada, Jharol and Sabania.
  • Ahar: Udaipur, on the Ahar river; ancient names Tambavati and Aghatpur; mound called Dhulkot.
  • Ahar was excavated by A.K. Vyas (1953), R.C. Agrawal (1956) and H.D. Sankalia (1961–62).
  • Ahar culture pottery: black-and-red ware with white painted designs.
  • Ahar houses had stone foundations and several hearths; rice was known.
  • Gilund (Rajsamand, Banas) was excavated by B.B. Lal (1959–60); it has a hoard of seal impressions.
  • Balathal (Vallabhnagar, Udaipur) was excavated by V.N. Misra from 1993.
  • Balathal has the oldest skeletal evidence of leprosy (c. 2000 BCE).
  • Ojiyana (Bhilwara) was excavated by B.R. Meena and Alok Tripathi.

Exam traps

  • Kalibangan is on the Ghaggar; Ganeshwar on the Kantli; Ahar on the Ahar (Banas basin); Bagor on the Kothari.
  • Kalibangan is Harappan; Ahar is NOT Harappan — it is a separate Chalcolithic culture.
  • Ganeshwar is copper-age (Chalcolithic), not Harappan, though it traded with the Harappans.
  • Tambavati = Ahar, not Ganeshwar.
  • Kalibangan (Lal and Thapar) vs Ganeshwar (R.C. Agrawal) vs Balathal (V.N. Misra) vs Ojiyana (Meena and Tripathi) — match excavator to site.
  • Rice WAS known at Ahar (RPSC key, 2021); a statement saying it was unknown is wrong.
  • At Kalibangan both the citadel and the lower town were walled; in most Harappan cities only the citadel was.

7Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words · RAS Mains 2018 Give a brief description of the archaeological sites Bagor, Kalibangan and Ahar.

How to frame it

  • One compact paragraph per site: location, period, excavator, key finds
  • One line comparing them
Model answer (84 words)

Bagor (Bhilwara, Kothari river), excavated by V.N. Misra, is a large Mesolithic site with microliths and early animal domestication (c. 5000 BCE), later copper and iron. Kalibangan (Hanumangarh, Ghaggar), excavated by Lal and Thapar, has early-Harappan and Harappan phases: ploughed field, earthquake evidence, fire altars, fortified citadel and lower town. Ahar (Udaipur), ancient Tambavati, is the type-site of the Ahar–Banas Chalcolithic culture: stone-founded houses, copper smelting, black-and-red ware with white designs. Together they trace Rajasthan from herding to Harappan towns and regional farming cultures.

10 marks · 100 words Compare the Harappan culture of Kalibangan with the Ahar–Banas culture.

How to frame it

  • Setting
  • Settlement and building
  • Economy and technology
  • Pottery
  • Significance of each
Model answer (96 words)

Kalibangan, on the Ghaggar, was an urban Harappan town with a grid plan and walled citadel and lower town; Ahar–Banas sites in the Banas valley were farming villages. Kalibangan used mud and baked brick, seals and Harappan weights; Ahar used stone foundations and had no writing. Both used copper, but Ahar smelted local Aravalli ore, while Harappan towns drew copper from centres like Ganeshwar. Kalibangan had red ware with black painting; Ahar had black-and-red ware with white designs. Kalibangan shows how local villages grew into Harappan towns; Ahar shows an independent regional culture that outlasted them.

5 marks · 50 words What was the importance of Ganeshwar?

How to frame it

  • Location
  • Finds
  • Link to Harappans
Model answer (48 words)

Ganeshwar (Neem ka Thana, Kantli river) lies in the Khetri copper belt. Its excavation (R.C. Agrawal, 1977) yielded over a thousand nearly pure copper objects, such as arrowheads, fish-hooks and axes. Similar objects at Harappan sites suggest it supplied copper to them, hence "mother of the copper-age cultures".

2 marks · 15 words What is Tambavati?

How to frame it

  • Identification
  • Meaning
Model answer (14 words)

Ancient name of Ahar (Udaipur), the Chalcolithic copper-smelting site; it means "city of copper".

8One-screen revision card

  • Chalcolithic = copper + stone, village-based; Bronze Age in India = urban Harappan.
  • Kalibangan: Hanumangarh, Ghaggar; Ghosh 1952, Lal–Thapar 1961–69; ploughed field, earthquake, fire altars, both parts fortified; abandoned c. 1900 BCE as the Ghaggar dried.
  • Ganeshwar: Kantli, Khetri belt; R.C. Agrawal 1977; copper supplier; "mother of copper cultures". Copper hoards: Kurada, Jharol, Sabania.
  • Ahar: Udaipur; Tambavati, Dhulkot; black-and-red ware with white designs; stone foundations; rice known.
  • Gilund: Rajsamand, Banas; B.B. Lal; seal impressions. Balathal: Vallabhnagar; V.N. Misra; oldest leprosy evidence. Ojiyana: Bhilwara; Meena and Tripathi.

ताम्र निधिCopper hoardA cache of copper objects found together.

Sources used for this unit

Unit R04 · c. 1500 BCE – 600 CE

Ancient Rajasthan: Janapadas to the Hunas

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR1 Pre-historical sites of Rajasthan — Palaeolithic to Chalcolithic and Bronze Age; society and culture of ancient Rajasthan
  • PR2 Sources of Rajasthan history: archaeological, archival, literary and numismatic
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH1 Prehistoric cultures, ancient sites; political and cultural achievements of rulers (to 18th century)

Topics this unit covers

  • Pre-historical sites (Iron Age)
  • Society and culture of ancient Rajasthan
  • Sources: archaeological and numismatic

Bairat, Noh, Rangmahal, Nagari and the tribal republics (Malava, Yaudheya, Arjunayana) recur in Prelims; "society and culture of ancient Rajasthan" is an explicit syllabus line.

2The big picture

After the copper-using cultures, iron reached Rajasthan, and the region entered the age of janapadas — territorial states and tribal republics. Rajasthan was a meeting ground: the Matsya kingdom (around Bairat) was part of the Gangetic world; republics such as the Malavas, Yaudheyas, Arjunayanas and Shibis ruled themselves through assemblies; and foreign powers — Indo-Greeks, Shakas, Kushans and finally the Hunas — passed through or ruled parts of it. Empires (Mauryas, Guptas) left edicts, coins and temples.

By around 600 CE the old republics had disappeared, the Huna invasions had shaken the Gupta order, and new clans — from whom the Rajputs would emerge — were rising around centres such as Bhinmal.

The syllabus asks for the "society and culture of ancient Rajasthan". The evidence is scattered — sacrificial pillars, Vaishnava and Buddhist remains, coins of republics — so learn it as a set of examples you can combine in an answer (section 5).

  1. c. 1000 BCE onwardsIron and Painted Grey Ware at Noh and Jodhpura
  2. c. 6th c. BCEMatsya, one of the 16 Mahajanapadas, with capital Viratnagar (Bairat)
  3. 3rd c. BCEAshoka's edicts and a Buddhist circular shrine at Bairat
  4. 2nd c. BCEIndo-Greeks attack Madhyamika (Nagari); Ghosundi inscription
  5. c. 1st–3rd c. CEMalava, Yaudheya, Arjunayana coins; Kushan-period Rangmahal
  6. 225–238 CEYupa inscriptions at Nandsa, Barnala and Badva
  7. 4th c. CESamudragupta reduces the Rajasthan republics to tributaries
  8. c. 500–530 CEHuna invasions (Toramana, Mihirakula)
  9. 628 CEBrahmagupta writes the Brahmasphutasiddhanta at Bhinmal
  10. c. 641 CEXuanzang describes the Gurjara kingdom with capital at Bhinmal

3Core notes

R04.1 The Iron Age: Noh, Jodhpura and Sunari

  • Noh (Bharatpur, Ruparel river), excavated by R.C. Agrawal (1963–67): a long sequence of five cultures — Ochre Coloured Pottery, Black-and-Red Ware, Painted Grey Ware (with iron), Northern Black Polished Ware, and Shunga–Kushan levels. A large stone Yaksha image, locally worshipped as "Jakh Baba", belongs to the Kushan period.
  • Jodhpura (Jaipur district, Sabi river): Ochre Coloured Pottery, Painted Grey Ware and evidence of iron working.
  • Sunari (Jhunjhunu, Kantli river): iron-smelting furnaces.

Painted Grey Ware is associated with the later Vedic culture of the Ganga–Yamuna doab, so these sites tie eastern and northern Rajasthan to the Vedic heartland.

R04.2 Janapadas and republics

State / peopleAreaEvidence
Matsya (Mahajanapada)Jaipur–Alwar–Bharatpur; capital Viratnagar (Bairat)Listed among the 16 Mahajanapadas; in the Mahabharata the Pandavas spend their year in hiding here
ShurasenaBharatpur–Dholpur–Karauli edges (capital Mathura)Mahajanapada list
ShibiMadhyamika (Nagari, near Chittorgarh)Coins reading "Majhimikaya Shibi Janapadasa"
MalavaTonk–Jaipur area (Nagar, Rairh)Thousands of coins reading "Malavanam Jayah"
YaudheyaNorth Rajasthan (Ganganagar–Hanumangarh) and HaryanaCoins; a warrior republic
ArjunayanaAlwar–BharatpurCoins

The republics (gana-sangha) were ruled by assemblies of clan elders rather than kings. Samudragupta's Allahabad pillar inscription lists the Malavas, Arjunayanas and Yaudheyas among frontier peoples who paid him tribute and obeyed his orders — the beginning of their end as independent powers.

R04.3 Mauryas, Indo-Greeks, Shakas and Kushans

Bairat (Viratnagar) is Rajasthan's key Mauryan site. Excavated by Daya Ram Sahni (1936–37) and later by N.R. Banerjee and K.N. Dikshit, it has:

  • Two Ashokan inscriptions: the Bairat minor rock edict and the Bhabru edict (Ashoka's declaration of faith in the Buddha, Dhamma and Sangha), found in 1837.
  • A circular Buddhist shrine (on Bijak ki Pahari) with a monastery — among the earliest structural Buddhist shrines in India.
  • A cloth bundle of coins, including punch-marked and Indo-Greek coins (several of Menander).

Indo-Greeks. The grammarian Patanjali gives as an example "the Yavana besieged Madhyamika" — i.e. Nagari near Chittor — showing Indo-Greek raids into Rajasthan in the 2nd century BCE.

Shakas (Western Kshatrapas). Rudradaman's Junagadh inscription (150 CE) lists Maru (Marwar) among his territories, and Western Kshatrapa coins are found in south-western Rajasthan. Chandragupta II's conquest of the Shakas (c. 400) brought the region into the Gupta orbit.

Kushans. Rangmahal (Hanumangarh), excavated by a Swedish team under Hanna Rydh (1952–54), has Kushan-period pottery, terracottas and coins. Noh's Yaksha also belongs to this age.

R04.4 Guptas, Hunas and the rise of Bhinmal

Guptas. Rajasthan was largely a tributary frontier for the Guptas. Their wealth is shown by the Bayana hoard (found 1946), the largest hoard of Gupta gold coins.

Hunas. Around 500 CE the Huna leaders Toramana and his son Mihirakula overran western and central India, including parts of Rajasthan. Mihirakula was defeated by Yashodharman of Mandsaur (on Rajasthan's border, c. 530 CE). Many Huna groups settled and were absorbed; "Huna" appears among the later Rajput clans.

Bhinmal (Shrimal). By the 7th century the Gurjaras had a kingdom centred on Bhinmal (Jalore district):

  • The astronomer-mathematician Brahmagupta wrote the Brahmasphutasiddhanta (628 CE) here under the Chapa king Vyaghramukha.
  • The Sanskrit poet Magha (Shishupalavadha) came from Bhinmal.
  • Xuanzang described the Gurjara kingdom with its capital at Bhinmal (c. 641).

Bhinmal leads directly into the story of the Gurjara-Pratiharas (unit R05).

R04.5 Society and culture of ancient Rajasthan

Combine these strands in any answer on ancient society and culture:

  1. Vedic religion: yupa (sacrificial pillar) inscriptions at Nandsa (Bhilwara, 225 CE), Barnala (227 CE) and Badva (Kota, 238 CE, Maukharis) record Vedic sacrifices; Ghosundi records an Ashvamedha.
  2. Bhagavata (Vaishnava) worship: Ghosundi and Nagari (2nd c. BCE) — worship of Sankarshana and Vasudeva.
  3. Buddhism: Bairat's shrine and monastery; Ashoka's edicts.
  4. Jainism: the Barli (Ajmer) inscription, read by some scholars as referring to Mahavira, and early Jain presence, which later flowered at Osian and Abu.
  5. Polity: monarchy (Matsya) alongside republics with assemblies (Malava, Yaudheya, Arjunayana, Shibi).
  6. Economy: copper and iron working (Ganeshwar, Sunari), punch-marked and tribal coinage, trade routes linking the Ganga plains with Gujarat's ports and the north-west.
  7. Learning: Bhinmal's astronomy (Brahmagupta) and poetry (Magha).

4What historians say

Ancient Rajasthan's history must be pieced together from archaeology, coins and inscriptions, because there are almost no local texts. Coins are especially important for the republics: they show that the Malavas and Yaudheyas issued currency in the name of the gana (the people), not a king — evidence for non-monarchical government (see R.S. Sharma on ancient republics).

The Huna–Rajput link is debated. The older view (Tod, V.A. Smith) saw many Rajput clans as descendants of foreign invaders such as the Hunas and Gurjaras; others stress local origins. Unit R05 takes this up.

5Prelims fact bank

  • Noh (Bharatpur, Ruparel river) was excavated by R.C. Agrawal and has five cultural phases.
  • The Yaksha image at Noh is locally worshipped as "Jakh Baba".
  • Jodhpura (Jaipur, Sabi river) has Ochre Coloured Pottery, Painted Grey Ware and iron working.
  • Sunari (Jhunjhunu, Kantli river) has iron-smelting furnaces.
  • Matsya Mahajanapada had its capital at Viratnagar (Bairat).
  • Shibi janapada coins read "Majhimikaya Shibi Janapadasa" (Madhyamika = Nagari).
  • Malava coins read "Malavanam Jayah" (Nagar and Rairh, Tonk).
  • Samudragupta's Allahabad pillar inscription lists the Malavas, Arjunayanas and Yaudheyas as tributaries.
  • Bairat was excavated by Daya Ram Sahni (1936–37).
  • Bairat has a circular Buddhist shrine on Bijak ki Pahari and the Bhabru edict of Ashoka.
  • A cloth bundle of coins at Bairat included Indo-Greek coins of Menander.
  • Patanjali records the Yavanas (Indo-Greeks) besieging Madhyamika.
  • Rudradaman's Junagadh inscription (150 CE) lists Maru among his territories.
  • Rangmahal (Hanumangarh) was excavated by a Swedish team under Hanna Rydh (1952–54); Kushan period.
  • Huna rulers Toramana and Mihirakula invaded c. 500 CE; Yashodharman of Mandsaur defeated Mihirakula.
  • Brahmagupta wrote the Brahmasphutasiddhanta at Bhinmal in 628 CE.
  • The poet Magha (Shishupalavadha) came from Bhinmal.
  • Yupa inscriptions: Nandsa (Bhilwara, 225 CE), Barnala (227 CE), Badva (Kota, 238 CE).

Exam traps

  • Madhyamika = Nagari (Chittorgarh), the Shibi centre — not Mandsaur.
  • Bairat = Viratnagar = capital of Matsya; it is in Kotputli–Behror (formerly Jaipur) district.
  • Rangmahal is Kushan-period (Swedish excavation); Kalibangan, also in Hanumangarh, is Harappan.
  • Noh is in Bharatpur; Nagar (Malava coins) is in Tonk; Nagari (Shibi) is in Chittorgarh.
  • Brahmagupta (astronomer, Bhinmal) is not Brahmadeva or Bhaskara.

7Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words Describe the society and culture of ancient Rajasthan on the basis of archaeological evidence.

How to frame it

  • Religion: Vedic yupas, Bhagavata, Buddhism, Jainism
  • Polity: Matsya and republics
  • Economy: metals, coins, trade
  • Learning: Bhinmal
  • Conclusion
Model answer (88 words)

Yupa inscriptions at Nandsa, Barnala and Badva (3rd century CE) and Ghosundi's Ashvamedha show Vedic sacrifice, while Ghosundi and Nagari attest early Bhagavata worship. Bairat's Ashokan edicts and circular shrine reveal Buddhism; the Barli inscription, Jainism. Politically, monarchy (Matsya) coexisted with republics — Malavas, Yaudheyas, Arjunayanas, Shibis — whose coins bear the name of the gana. Iron working at Sunari and Jodhpura, punch-marked, Indo-Greek and Gupta coins indicate a monetised trading economy. Bhinmal produced Brahmagupta and Magha. Ancient Rajasthan was thus religiously plural, politically varied and well connected.

5 marks · 50 words Why is Bairat important for the history of Rajasthan?

How to frame it

  • Matsya capital
  • Mauryan and Buddhist remains
  • Coins
Model answer (47 words)

Bairat (Viratnagar) was the capital of the Matsya Mahajanapada. It has two Ashokan edicts, including the Bhabru edict, and a circular Buddhist shrine with a monastery, among India's earliest. Its coin hoard, with punch-marked and Indo-Greek (Menander) coins, shows trade links. It is Rajasthan's main Mauryan site.

2 marks · 15 words What does "Malavanam Jayah" signify?

How to frame it

  • Where found
  • Meaning
Model answer (15 words)

"Victory of the Malavas": legend on Malava republic coins (Nagar, Tonk), issued by a gana.

8One-screen revision card

  • Iron Age: Noh (Bharatpur; 5 phases; Jakh Baba Yaksha), Jodhpura (Sabi), Sunari (furnaces).
  • Matsya — Viratnagar; Shibi — Madhyamika/Nagari; Malava — Nagar (Tonk); Yaudheya — north; Arjunayana — Alwar–Bharatpur.
  • Samudragupta made the republics tributaries (Allahabad pillar).
  • Bairat: Ashokan edicts (Bhabru), circular Buddhist shrine, Menander coins.
  • Indo-Greeks besieged Madhyamika (Patanjali); Rudradaman ruled Maru; Rangmahal = Kushan.
  • Hunas: Toramana, Mihirakula; defeated by Yashodharman.
  • Bhinmal: Brahmagupta (628), Magha, Xuanzang's Gurjara capital.
  • Yupas: Nandsa 225, Barnala 227, Badva 238 CE.

9Self-test

  1. What was the capital of the Matsya Mahajanapada?

    Answer

    Viratnagar (Bairat).

  2. Which republic issued coins reading "Malavanam Jayah", and where are they found?

    Answer

    The Malavas; Nagar and Rairh in Tonk.

  3. Which inscription lists the Malavas, Arjunayanas and Yaudheyas as tributaries?

    Answer

    Samudragupta's Allahabad pillar inscription.

  4. What evidence shows Indo-Greek raids into Rajasthan?

    Answer

    Patanjali's example "the Yavana besieged Madhyamika", and Indo-Greek coins at Bairat.

  5. Who excavated Rangmahal, and to which period does it belong?

    Answer

    A Swedish team under Hanna Rydh (1952–54); the Kushan period.

  6. Name the three yupa inscription sites and their dates.

    Answer

    Nandsa (225 CE), Barnala (227 CE), Badva (238 CE).

  7. Which scholar wrote the Brahmasphutasiddhanta at Bhinmal, and when?

    Answer

    Brahmagupta, 628 CE.

  8. Who defeated the Huna ruler Mihirakula?

    Answer

    Yashodharman of Mandsaur.

  9. What is the five-phase site in Bharatpur district?

    Answer

    Noh.

Unit R05 · c. 600 – 1300 CE

Origin of the Rajputs; Gurjara-Pratiharas and Parmaras

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR3 Political and cultural achievements of prominent rulers of major dynasties
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH1 Prehistoric cultures, ancient sites; political and cultural achievements of rulers (to 18th century)

Topics this unit covers

  • Major dynasties of Rajasthan and their achievements
  • Origin of the Rajputs

The theories of Rajput origin are a standard Mains question; Pratihara rulers, the Agnikula myth, Osian and the Abu temples recur in Prelims.

2The big picture

Between the 7th and 12th centuries, new ruling clans called Rajputs came to dominate Rajasthan and much of north India. Where they came from is one of the most debated questions in Indian history (section 1).

The first great power to rise from Rajasthan was the Gurjara-Pratihara dynasty. Starting near Bhinmal and Mandore, it held back the Arabs of Sindh, captured Kannauj, and for two centuries fought the Palas of Bengal and Rashtrakutas of the Deccan for control of north India — the tripartite struggle.

The Parmaras ruled Abu, Kiradu, Jalore and Vagad (and, as a separate famous line, Malwa). Under them and the Chaulukyas of Gujarat, the marble Dilwara temples at Abu were built.

The Rajput clans set up in this period — Pratiharas, Parmaras, Chauhans (R06), Guhils (R07) — are the roots of almost every later state in Rajasthan. Understanding how they arose explains the whole medieval story.

  1. c. 725Arab governor Junaid raids western India; repulsed by Nagabhata I
  2. c. 730–760Nagabhata I founds the imperial Pratihara line
  3. c. 775–800Vatsaraja; Mahavira temple at Osian built in his reign
  4. 837Jodhpur inscription of Bauka (Mandore Pratiharas)
  5. c. 816Nagabhata II captures Kannauj
  6. 836–885Mihira Bhoja, the greatest Pratihara ruler
  7. 851Arab merchant Sulaiman describes the Pratihara kingdom
  8. 861Ghatiyala inscriptions of Kakkuka
  9. 916Rashtrakuta Indra III sacks Kannauj
  10. 1018Mahmud of Ghazni attacks Kannauj; Rajyapala flees
  11. 1031Vimal Vasahi temple at Dilwara (Abu)
  12. 1230–31Luna Vasahi temple at Dilwara

3Core notes

R05.1 Theories of Rajput origin

The word comes from rajaputra ("son of a king"). In early inscriptions it meant a prince or a landholding noble; only later did "Rajput" become the name of a caste group. Five main explanations exist:

TheoryMain proponentsArgumentWeakness
Agnikula (fire-born)Padmagupta's Navasahasankacharita (c. 1000, for the Parmaras only); Chand Bardai's Prithviraj Raso (four clans)Sage Vasishtha created heroes from a fire-pit on Mount Abu: the Parmara, Pratihara, Chaulukya (Solanki) and ChauhanA myth; earlier inscriptions of these clans do not mention it
Foreign originJames Tod, V.A. Smith, D.R. BhandarkarRajputs descend from Shakas, Kushans, Hunas, Gurjaras; the fire ritual "purified" them into the caste systemRests on resemblance of customs and on names; little direct proof
Indigenous KshatriyaG.H. Ojha, C.V. VaidyaRajputs are descendants of Vedic Kshatriyas of the solar (Suryavanshi) and lunar (Chandravanshi) linesGenealogies were composed much later to claim such descent
Brahmin originD.R. Bhandarkar, based on inscriptionsSome founders are called Brahmins: the Pratihara Harichandra; the Chauhan ancestor in the Bijolia inscription (a vipra of Vatsa gotra); the Guhils as Nagar BrahminsCovers only some clans
Social process (mixed origin)B.D. ChattopadhyayaRajputs formed through a process: local chiefs, tribal and pastoral groups, and some immigrants gained land and power, built forts, patronised Brahmins and acquired Kshatriya status ("Rajputisation")Now the most widely accepted view

No single origin fits every clan. Modern historians treat "Rajput" as a status achieved through political power and land control between the 7th and 12th centuries, not as a race. In an answer, present the theories, then conclude with the social-process view.

R05.2 The Gurjara-Pratiharas: Mandore and Bhinmal

Name. "Pratihara" means door-keeper; the dynasty claimed descent from Lakshmana, door-keeper to his brother Rama (Gwalior inscription). "Gurjara" refers to the region Gurjaratra (south-west Rajasthan) or to the Gurjara people — scholars disagree which.

The Mandore branch. The Jodhpur inscription of Bauka (837) and the Ghatiyala inscriptions of Kakkuka (861) give the genealogy: a Brahmin, Harichandra, had sons by his Kshatriya wife Bhadra, who founded the Pratihara line at Mandore (near Jodhpur). This branch stayed a regional power; Mandore later passed to the Rathores (R09).

The imperial branch began in the Bhinmal–Jalore area (Gurjaratra) and later ruled from Kannauj.

R05.3 Imperial Pratiharas: rulers and achievements

RulerMain events
Nagabhata I (c. 730–760)Repulsed the Arab armies of Sindh (Junaid's raids); the Gwalior inscription calls him the destroyer of the mlechchha armies
Vatsaraja (c. 775–800)Defeated the Pala king Dharmapala but was defeated by the Rashtrakuta Dhruva; the Mahavira temple at Osian was built in his reign
Nagabhata II (c. 800–833)Captured Kannauj (c. 816) and made it the capital; defeated Dharmapala; defeated by the Rashtrakuta Govinda III
Mihira Bhoja (836–885)Greatest ruler; empire from Punjab to Bengal's borders and from the Himalayas to the Narmada; titles Adivaraha and Prabhasa (Adivaraha coins); his Gwalior prashasti is the main source for the dynasty; the Arab merchant Sulaiman (851) called him the strongest enemy of the Arabs, with the finest cavalry
Mahendrapala I (885–910)The poet Rajashekhara (Karpuramanjari, Kavyamimamsa) was his guru
Mahipala I (c. 912–944)Visited by the Arab traveller al-Masudi (915–16); Kannauj sacked by the Rashtrakuta Indra III (916)
RajyapalaFled Kannauj when Mahmud of Ghazni attacked (1018); the dynasty faded out by the mid-11th century

The tripartite struggle (c. 750–900) was a three-way contest between the Pratiharas, the Palas of Bengal and the Rashtrakutas of the Deccan for Kannauj, the symbolic capital of north India. The Pratiharas finally held it, but the long war weakened all three.

Contribution:

  1. Shielded India from Arab expansion for nearly three centuries after the conquest of Sindh (712).
  2. Gave north India a large, stable state after Harsha.
  3. Patronised literature (Rajashekhara) and temple building; their western Indian temple style is called the Mahamaru style (M.A. Dhaky) — best seen at Osian (Harihara temples, Mahavira temple, Sachiya Mata).
  4. Their decline left room for the Chauhans, Guhils, Parmaras and Chandelas.

R05.4 The Parmaras of Abu, Kiradu, Jalore and Vagad

The Parmaras (Pawars) claimed the Agnikula origin from Abu. Several branches ruled in and around Rajasthan:

BranchKey points
Abu (Chandravati)Feudatories of the Chaulukyas (Solankis) of Gujarat. Dharavarsha (c. 1163–1219), the most famous ruler, fought alongside the Chaulukyas against Muhammad Ghori (1178) and Qutbuddin Aibak (1197). His brother Prahladana wrote the play Parthaparakrama-vyayoga and founded Prahladanapur (Palanpur). Abu was taken by the Deora Chauhans c. 1311 (see R10).
Kiradu (Barmer)The Kiradu inscription (1161) gives Parmara genealogy; the Someshvara and other temples are called the "Khajuraho of Rajasthan"
JaloreParmara rule before the Chauhans took the fort (R06)
Vagad (Banswara–Dungarpur)Capital Utthunaka (Arthuna, Banswara); the Mandaleshvara temple at Arthuna (1080) and a group of Shaiva and Jain temples
Malwa (Dhar) — outside RajasthanThe famous line of Munja and Bhoja (c. 1010–1055); bordered Rajasthan and fought the Chauhans and Guhils

The Dilwara temples (Abu). Built by Jain ministers of the Chaulukya kings while the Parmaras ruled Abu:

  • Vimal Vasahi (1031), to Adinath, by Vimal Shah, minister of Bhima I.
  • Luna Vasahi (1230–31), to Neminath, by the brothers Vastupala and Tejapala, ministers of the Vaghelas, in memory of Tejapala's son Lunig; under the Parmara Somasimha, son of Dharavarsha.

(Temple architecture is treated fully in the Art and Culture notebook.)

4What historians say

Tod (1829) popularised the foreign "Scythian" origin; V.A. Smith and D.R. Bhandarkar developed it. G.H. Ojha and C.V. Vaidya argued for Vedic Kshatriya descent, reading the foreign theory as colonial prejudice. B.D. Chattopadhyaya ("Origin of the Rajputs", 1976) shifted the debate from who the Rajputs were to how they emerged: through land grants, fort building, clan networks and Brahmin legitimation in early medieval Rajasthan. Dasharath Sharma's Rajasthan Through the Ages remains the standard political narrative.

On the Pratiharas, R.C. Majumdar stressed their role as a bulwark against the Arabs; Baij Nath Puri (The History of the Gurjara-Pratiharas) is the standard monograph.

5Prelims fact bank

  • The Agnikula legend first appears for the Parmaras alone in Padmagupta's Navasahasankacharita (c. 1000).
  • Prithviraj Raso extends the Agnikula legend to four clans: Parmara, Pratihara, Chaulukya (Solanki) and Chauhan.
  • James Tod proposed the foreign (Scythian) origin of the Rajputs.
  • G.H. Ojha and C.V. Vaidya held that the Rajputs were descendants of Vedic Kshatriyas.
  • The Bijolia inscription calls the Chauhan ancestor a vipra (Brahmin) of Vatsa gotra.
  • B.D. Chattopadhyaya explained Rajput origin as a social and political process.
  • Jodhpur inscription of Bauka: 837 CE; Ghatiyala inscriptions of Kakkuka: 861 CE.
  • The Mandore Pratiharas descend from the Brahmin Harichandra and his Kshatriya wife Bhadra.
  • Nagabhata I repulsed the Arabs; Nagabhata II made Kannauj the Pratihara capital.
  • Mihira Bhoja took the titles Adivaraha and Prabhasa; his Gwalior prashasti is the main dynastic source.
  • The Arab merchant Sulaiman (851) described the Pratiharas as the strongest enemies of the Arabs.
  • Rajashekhara was the guru of Mahendrapala I.
  • Al-Masudi visited the Pratiharas under Mahipala I (915–16).
  • The Mahavira temple at Osian was built in the reign of Vatsaraja.
  • M.A. Dhaky named the early western Indian temple style "Mahamaru".
  • Dharavarsha was the most famous Parmara ruler of Abu; his brother Prahladana founded Palanpur.
  • Kiradu (Barmer) temples are called the "Khajuraho of Rajasthan".
  • Arthuna (Banswara) was the capital of the Vagad Parmaras.
  • Vimal Vasahi (1031) was built by Vimal Shah; Luna Vasahi (1230–31) by Vastupala and Tejapala.

Exam traps

  • Agnikula clans are Parmara, Pratihara, Chaulukya (Solanki) and Chauhan — not Guhil or Rathore.
  • Nagabhata II, not Nagabhata I, captured Kannauj.
  • Mihira Bhoja (Pratihara) is different from Raja Bhoja (Parmara of Malwa).
  • Sulaiman visited in 851 (Mihira Bhoja); al-Masudi in 915–16 (Mahipala I).
  • Dilwara temples were built by Chaulukya ministers in Parmara-ruled Abu — not by the Parmaras themselves.
  • Vimal Vasahi is dedicated to Adinath; Luna Vasahi to Neminath.

ExamQuestionMarks

RAS Pre 2024To whom is the Mathandev temple at Rajorgarh dedicated? — Answer (RPSC final key): Shiva—

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit found yet in the RajRAS list. The origin of the Rajputs is a standard expected question. RAS Pre questions come from the history sections of the 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers, paraphrased, with answers as per the RPSC final answer keys (2018 not used: its key was not available).

7Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words Critically examine the various theories regarding the origin of the Rajputs.

How to frame it

  • Meaning of rajaputra
  • Agnikula
  • Foreign
  • Kshatriya
  • Brahmin
  • Social process — conclusion
Model answer (89 words)

"Rajput" derives from rajaputra, a prince or landed noble. The Agnikula myth (Navasahasankacharita, Prithviraj Raso) derives four clans from Vasishtha's fire-pit at Abu, but early inscriptions ignore it. Tod, Smith and Bhandarkar proposed descent from Shakas, Hunas and Gurjaras, though on thin evidence. Ojha and Vaidya claimed Vedic Kshatriya ancestry, relying on late genealogies. Inscriptions call some founders Brahmins — Harichandra Pratihara, the Bijolia Chauhan ancestor. B.D. Chattopadhyaya shows Rajputs emerging through land control, forts and Brahmin legitimation. Rajput identity was thus an achieved status, formed from diverse groups.

5 marks · 50 words Assess the contribution of the Gurjara-Pratiharas to Indian history.

How to frame it

  • Arab resistance
  • Kannauj empire
  • Culture
Model answer (49 words)

The Pratiharas checked Arab expansion from Sindh for three centuries (Nagabhata I, Mihira Bhoja). Nagabhata II made Kannauj the centre of a large north Indian empire, contested in the tripartite struggle. They patronised Rajashekhara and the Mahamaru temple style at Osian, and paved the way for later Rajput states.

2 marks · 15 words What is the Agnikula theory?

How to frame it

  • Myth
  • Clans
Model answer (13 words)

Myth that Parmaras, Pratiharas, Chaulukyas and Chauhans arose from Vasishtha's fire-pit on Abu.

8One-screen revision card

  • Rajput origin: Agnikula (Padmagupta → Raso), foreign (Tod, Smith), Kshatriya (Ojha, Vaidya), Brahmin (Bijolia, Harichandra), process (Chattopadhyaya).
  • Mandore Pratiharas: Harichandra + Bhadra; Bauka 837, Kakkuka 861 (Ghatiyala).
  • Imperial: Nagabhata I (Arabs) → Vatsaraja (Osian) → Nagabhata II (Kannauj) → Mihira Bhoja (Adivaraha, Sulaiman) → Mahendrapala I (Rajashekhara) → Mahipala I (al-Masudi, 916 sack).
  • Tripartite struggle: Pratihara–Pala–Rashtrakuta for Kannauj.
  • Parmaras: Abu (Dharavarsha, Prahladana), Kiradu, Jalore, Vagad (Arthuna).
  • Dilwara: Vimal Vasahi 1031 (Vimal Shah, Adinath); Luna Vasahi 1230–31 (Vastupala–Tejapala, Neminath).

9Self-test

  1. Which text first records the Agnikula myth, and for which clan?

    Answer

    Padmagupta's Navasahasankacharita (c. 1000), for the Parmaras.

  2. Name the four Agnikula clans.

    Answer

    Parmara, Pratihara, Chaulukya (Solanki), Chauhan.

  3. Which historian explained Rajput origin as a social process?

    Answer

    B.D. Chattopadhyaya.

  4. Which two inscriptions give the genealogy of the Mandore Pratiharas?

    Answer

    The Jodhpur inscription of Bauka (837) and the Ghatiyala inscriptions of Kakkuka (861).

  5. Who made Kannauj the Pratihara capital?

    Answer

    Nagabhata II (c. 816).

  6. Which Arab visitor praised Mihira Bhoja's cavalry?

    Answer

    Sulaiman (851).

  7. Which poet was the guru of Mahendrapala I?

    Answer

    Rajashekhara.

  8. What was the tripartite struggle?

    Answer

    The contest among the Pratiharas, Palas and Rashtrakutas for Kannauj (c. 750–900).

  9. Who was Dharavarsha?

    Answer

    The most famous Parmara ruler of Abu (c. 1163–1219), who fought Ghori and Aibak alongside the Chaulukyas.

  10. Which temples are called the "Khajuraho of Rajasthan"?

    Answer

    The Kiradu temples (Barmer).

Unit R06 · c. 7th century – 1311 CE

The Chauhans: Shakambhari, Ajmer, Ranthambhor, Jalore

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR3 Political and cultural achievements of prominent rulers of major dynasties
  • PR4 Cooperation and resistance with central power; administrative and revenue system in medieval Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH1 Prehistoric cultures, ancient sites; political and cultural achievements of rulers (to 18th century)

Topics this unit covers

  • Major dynasties of Rajasthan and their achievements
  • Battles: Tarain, Ranthambhor, Siwana, Jalore

Prithviraj III and Tarain, Vigraharaja IV's culture, and Hammir of Ranthambhor are the most-asked Chauhan topics; Jalore and Siwana come up in Prelims.

2The big picture

The Chauhans (Chahamanas) began as feudatories of the Pratiharas around Shakambhari (Sambhar lake). As Pratihara power faded, they became independent, founded Ajmer (c. 1113) and, under Vigraharaja IV, took Delhi. Their most famous king, Prithviraj III, beat Muhammad Ghori at Tarain in 1191 and lost to him there in 1192 — opening north India to Turkish rule.

Chauhan branches survived elsewhere: at Ranthambhor (Hammir Dev, who fell to Alauddin Khalji in 1301), at Nadol and Jalore (Kanhadadeva, who fell in 1311), and later as the Hadas of Bundi–Kota and the Deoras of Sirohi.

The Chauhan story frames the central theme of medieval Rajasthan: resistance to the Delhi Sultanate, with forts, sieges, jauhar and saka. Learn the three Khalji sieges — Ranthambhor (1301), Siwana (1308), Jalore (1311) — with Chittor (1303, R07) as one set.

  1. c. 7th centuryVasudeva, traditional founder at Shakambhari (Bijolia inscription)
  2. 973Harsha inscription of Vigraharaja II (Sikar)
  3. c. 960Lakshmana founds the Nadol branch
  4. c. 1113Ajayaraja founds Ajmer (Ajayameru)
  5. c. 1135–50Arnoraja defeats the Turks; builds the Anasagar
  6. 1163Vigraharaja IV's inscription on the Delhi–Topra pillar
  7. 1170Bijolia inscription of Someshvara
  8. 1182Prithviraj III defeats the Chandela Paramardi
  9. 1191First battle of Tarain: Prithviraj defeats Ghori
  10. 1192Second battle of Tarain: Prithviraj defeated and killed
  11. 1194Govindaraja founds the Ranthambhor branch
  12. 1301Alauddin Khalji captures Ranthambhor; Hammir killed
  13. 1308Siwana falls (Sataldeva)
  14. 1311Jalore falls; Kanhadadeva killed

3Core notes

R06.1 Shakambhari: from feudatories to kings

The Bijolia inscription (1170, of Someshvara) names Vasudeva as the founder at Shakambhari and calls the clan's ancestor a vipra of Vatsa gotra from Ahichhatrapura. Early Chauhans served the Pratiharas: Guvaka I is said to have been honoured at Nagabhata II's court.

Vigraharaja II (c. 973) acted independently: he defeated the Chaulukya Mularaja of Gujarat. The Harsha inscription (973) from the Harshnath temple near Sikar is the main source for this phase.

R06.2 Ajmer: Ajayaraja to Vigraharaja IV

RulerAchievements
Ajayaraja (early 12th c.)Founded Ajmer (Ajayameru, c. 1113) and the hill fort later called Taragarh (Garh Beetli); issued silver coins, some with his queen Somalladevi
Arnoraja (Anaji, c. 1133–55)Defeated a Turkish raid near Ajmer and built the Anasagar lake on the battlefield to cleanse it; was defeated by the Chaulukya Kumarapala
Vigraharaja IV (Visaladeva, c. 1150–64)Took Delhi from the Tomaras (inscription of 1163 on the Delhi–Topra Ashokan pillar); wrote the Sanskrit play Harikeli Nataka; his court poet Somadeva wrote Lalitavigraharaja; built a Sanskrit college at Ajmer — later converted by Qutbuddin Aibak into the mosque Adhai Din ka Jhonpra; built the Visalsar lake; called Kavibandhava (friend of poets). His reign is treated as the high point of Chauhan power and culture.
Someshvara (1169–77)Father of Prithviraj III; Bijolia inscription (1170)

Pieces of Harikeli Nataka and Lalitavigraharaja survive as inscriptions on stone slabs found at the Adhai Din ka Jhonpra — direct evidence of the college.

R06.3 Prithviraj III and the battles of Tarain

Prithviraj III (Rai Pithora, r. c. 1177–92) came to the throne young, with his mother Karpuradevi as regent and the minister Kadambavasa running the state.

Campaigns:

  • Suppressed his cousin Nagarjuna and the Bhandanakas.
  • Defeated the Chandela Paramardi of Mahoba (1182) — the war remembered in the Alha–Udal ballads.
  • Fought the Chaulukyas of Gujarat; rivalry with the Gahadavala Jaichand of Kannauj. (The Sanyogita elopement story comes from the Prithviraj Raso and is not regarded as historical.)
First battle of Tarain (1191)Second battle of Tarain (1192)
CauseGhori captured Tabarhindah (Bathinda), a Chauhan fortGhori returned with a larger, reorganised army
ResultPrithviraj won; Ghori wounded (by Govindaraja of Delhi, per tradition) and fledPrithviraj defeated; captured and killed
WhyRajput charge broke the Ghurid wingsGhori used mounted archers, feigned retreat and a reserve; Prithviraj had not pursued in 1191 and had no allies

Aftermath. Ghori placed Prithviraj's son Govindaraja at Ajmer as a vassal. Prithviraj's brother Hariraja revolted; Qutbuddin Aibak crushed him, and Govindaraja moved to Ranthambhor (1194). Tarain II is taken as the start of Turkish rule in north India.

Court: Jayanaka (Prithvirajavijaya, a contemporary Sanskrit poem), Vidyapati Gauda, Vagishvara Janardana, Vishvarupa. Chand Bardai's Prithviraj Raso in its present form is much later.

The death of Prithviraj is told differently. Persian sources say he was captured and later executed (some say he was briefly allowed to rule as a vassal — coins with his name and Ghori's exist). The Raso's story of him shooting Ghori blind in Ghazni is legend.

R06.4 Ranthambhor: Hammir Dev

Hammir Dev (1282–1301) was the most famous ruler of the Ranthambhor Chauhans. He campaigned widely (performing a koti-yajna to celebrate his victories) and fought off Jalaluddin Khalji (1290–91).

Conflict with Alauddin Khalji:

  1. Cause: Hammir gave refuge to the Mongol rebels Muhammad Shah and Kehbru, who had mutinied against Alauddin; also Alauddin's wish to control the strategic fort.
  2. Alauddin's generals Ulugh Khan and Nusrat Khan attacked; Nusrat Khan was killed.
  3. Alauddin besieged the fort himself (1301). Hammir's ministers Ratipal and Ranmal defected.
  4. The women performed jauhar led by Queen Rangadevi, and Hammir died fighting (saka) — July 1301. This is often called the first jauhar of Rajasthan.

Sources: Amir Khusrau's Khazain-ul-Futuh (eyewitness, from the Sultan's side); Nayachandra Suri's Hammira Mahakavya (Sanskrit, 15th c.); Hammir Raso (Jodhraj); Ziauddin Barani.

R06.5 Nadol, Jalore and Siwana

Nadol (Pali): branch founded by Lakshmana (Rao Lakha, c. 960).

Jalore: Kirtipala (Kitu) of the Nadol line took Jalore from the Parmaras (c. 1181). The fort was called Suvarnagiri (Sonalgarh), so the branch is called Songara.

Kanhadadeva (c. 1292–1311) fought Alauddin Khalji:

  • Alauddin's army returning from Gujarat (1299) was attacked by the Jalore forces; relations stayed hostile.
  • Siwana (Barmer) was besieged in 1308; its commander Sataldeva fell; Alauddin renamed it Khairabad.
  • Jalore fell in 1311 to Kamaluddin Gurg after the betrayal of Bika Dahiya; Kanhadadeva and his son Viramadeva were killed; the fort was renamed Jalalabad.

Source: Padmanabha's Kanhadade Prabandh (1455). The story of Alauddin's daughter Firoza's love for Viramadeva is legend.

4What historians say

Dasharath Sharma's Early Chauhan Dynasties (1959) is the standard work; he rebuilt Chauhan history from inscriptions (Harsha, Bijolia, Delhi–Topra) and the Prithvirajavijaya, treating the Raso as late and unreliable for facts. Persian historians (Minhaj-us-Siraj's Tabaqat-i-Nasiri, Hasan Nizami) give the Ghurid side of Tarain.

Historians explain Prithviraj's defeat through military factors (Turkish mounted archery, unified command, reserves) and political ones (wars with neighbours such as the Chandelas, Chaulukyas and Gahadavalas left him without allies). Older explanations blaming "Rajput disunity" in general are now seen as too simple.

5Prelims fact bank

  • Vasudeva is named as the founder of the Chauhans at Shakambhari in the Bijolia inscription (1170).
  • The Harsha inscription (973) of Vigraharaja II comes from the Harshnath temple, Sikar.
  • Ajayaraja founded Ajmer (Ajayameru) c. 1113.
  • Ajayaraja issued coins with his queen Somalladevi.
  • Arnoraja built the Anasagar lake after defeating the Turks.
  • Vigraharaja IV captured Delhi from the Tomaras; inscription on the Delhi–Topra pillar (1163).
  • Vigraharaja IV wrote Harikeli Nataka; Somadeva wrote Lalitavigraharaja.
  • Vigraharaja IV's Sanskrit college at Ajmer became the Adhai Din ka Jhonpra.
  • Vigraharaja IV was called Kavibandhava.
  • Prithviraj III's mother Karpuradevi was regent; the minister was Kadambavasa.
  • Prithviraj III defeated the Chandela Paramardi in 1182.
  • Jayanaka wrote the Prithvirajavijaya.
  • First Tarain 1191: Prithviraj won; Second Tarain 1192: Ghori won.
  • Govindaraja founded the Ranthambhor Chauhan line (1194).
  • Hammir Dev sheltered the Mongol rebels Muhammad Shah and Kehbru.
  • Ranthambhor fell to Alauddin Khalji in 1301; the jauhar was led by Rangadevi.
  • Amir Khusrau's Khazain-ul-Futuh describes the Ranthambhor siege.
  • Nayachandra Suri wrote the Hammira Mahakavya.
  • Lakshmana (Rao Lakha) founded the Nadol branch c. 960.
  • Kirtipala founded the Songara Chauhan line at Jalore.
  • Siwana fell in 1308 (Sataldeva) and was renamed Khairabad.
  • Jalore fell in 1311 after Bika Dahiya's betrayal; Kanhadade Prabandh was written by Padmanabha (1455).

Exam traps

  • Ajayaraja founded Ajmer; Arnoraja built the Anasagar; Vigraharaja IV built the Visalsar and the college.
  • Adhai Din ka Jhonpra was built by Qutbuddin Aibak (on Vigraharaja IV's college) — not by Ghori himself.
  • Prithviraj won the first battle of Tarain (1191) and lost the second (1192).
  • Prithvirajavijaya (Jayanaka, contemporary) is reliable; Prithviraj Raso (Chand Bardai) is late.
  • Siwana was renamed Khairabad; Jalore became Jalalabad.
  • Ranthambhor 1301, Chittor 1303, Siwana 1308, Jalore 1311 — order of Khalji conquests.
