7Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words Evaluate the achievements of Prithviraj Chauhan III.

How to frame it

  • Accession and regency
  • Campaigns
  • Tarain I and II
  • Culture
  • Assessment
Model answer (83 words)

Prithviraj III (c. 1177–92) began under the regency of Karpuradevi and Kadambavasa. He crushed internal rivals, defeated the Chandela Paramardi (1182) and contested the Chaulukyas and Gahadavalas. In 1191 he routed Muhammad Ghori at Tarain, but failed to pursue him. In 1192 Ghori's mounted archers and reserves defeated him; he was captured and killed. He patronised Jayanaka, author of the Prithvirajavijaya. A brave ruler, he made enemies of his neighbours and misjudged the Ghurid threat; his defeat opened north India to Turkish rule.

5 marks · 50 words Describe the cultural achievements of Vigraharaja IV.

How to frame it

  • Own work
  • Patronage
  • Buildings
Model answer (47 words)

Vigraharaja IV (Visaladeva) wrote the Sanskrit play Harikeli Nataka and patronised Somadeva, author of Lalitavigraharaja. He founded a Sanskrit college at Ajmer, later turned into the Adhai Din ka Jhonpra, and built the Visalsar lake. Poets called him Kavibandhava. His reign marked the peak of Chauhan culture.

2 marks · 15 words Why did Alauddin Khalji attack Ranthambhor?

How to frame it

  • Refuge
  • Strategy
Model answer (16 words)

Hammir sheltered Mongol rebels Muhammad Shah and Kehbru; Ranthambhor also guarded routes to Malwa and Gujarat.

8One-screen revision card

  • Ajayaraja → Ajmer c. 1113, Taragarh, coins with Somalladevi.
  • Arnoraja → Anasagar. Vigraharaja IV → Delhi, Harikeli, college → Adhai Din ka Jhonpra, Kavibandhava.
  • Prithviraj III: Karpuradevi, Kadambavasa; Paramardi 1182; Tarain 1191 win, 1192 loss; Jayanaka.
  • Ranthambhor: Govindaraja 1194; Hammir — Mongol refugees, Ratipal–Ranmal betrayal, Rangadevi jauhar 1301.
  • Jalore (Songara): Kirtipala; Kanhadadeva; Siwana 1308 (Sataldeva, Khairabad); Jalore 1311 (Bika Dahiya).

9Self-test

  1. Who founded Ajmer?

    Answer

    Ajayaraja (c. 1113).

  2. Which Chauhan built the Anasagar, and why?

    Answer

    Arnoraja, to cleanse the ground where he defeated the Turks.

  3. Which pillar records Vigraharaja IV's conquests?

    Answer

    The Delhi–Topra Ashokan pillar (1163).

  4. What became of Vigraharaja IV's Sanskrit college?

    Answer

    Qutbuddin Aibak converted it into the Adhai Din ka Jhonpra mosque.

  5. Who was regent in the early years of Prithviraj III?

    Answer

    His mother Karpuradevi, with the minister Kadambavasa.

  6. Which contemporary Sanskrit poem describes Prithviraj III?

    Answer

    Jayanaka's Prithvirajavijaya.

  7. Give two reasons for Prithviraj's defeat in 1192.

    Answer

    Ghori's mounted archers, feigned retreat and reserves; Prithviraj's lack of allies and failure to pursue Ghori in 1191.

  8. Whom did Hammir shelter, angering Alauddin?

    Answer

    The Mongol rebels Muhammad Shah and Kehbru.

  9. Which eyewitness described the siege of Ranthambhor?

    Answer

    Amir Khusrau, in Khazain-ul-Futuh.

  10. What was Siwana renamed after 1308?

    Answer

    Khairabad.

  11. Who betrayed Jalore in 1311?

    Answer

    Bika Dahiya.

Unit R07 · c. 566 – 1509 CE

Mewar I: The Guhils to Rana Kumbha

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR3 Political and cultural achievements of prominent rulers of major dynasties
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH1 Prehistoric cultures, ancient sites; political and cultural achievements of rulers (to 18th century)

Topics this unit covers

  • Major dynasties of Rajasthan and their achievements
  • Rana Kumbha: wars, forts and literature

Rana Kumbha is one of the most-asked rulers in all of Rajasthan history (titles, forts, Vijay Stambh, books, Sarangpur). Bappa Rawal, Jaitra Singh, the 1303 siege of Chittor and Rana Hammir follow.

2The big picture

Mewar's ruling house, the Guhils, is one of the longest-reigning dynasties in the world — from the 6th century to 1947. In this unit it grows from a local chiefdom around Nagda and Ahar into the strongest Rajput state.

The story has three stages: (1) the early Guhils, from Guhaditya and Bappa Rawal to Jaitra Singh, who resisted Iltutmish; (2) the crisis of 1303, when Alauddin Khalji took Chittor from Ratan Singh; and (3) the recovery under the Sisodia branch — Rana Hammir retook Chittor, and Rana Kumbha (1433–68) made Mewar a great power, defeating Malwa and Gujarat and building forts, temples and the Vijay Stambh.

Kumbha combined three roles exam questions ask about: warrior (Sarangpur, the wars with Malwa and Gujarat), builder (Kumbhalgarh, Vijay Stambh) and scholar (Sangitraj). A 10-mark answer on him should touch all three.

  1. c. 566Guhaditya (Guhil), founder of the dynasty
  2. 646Samoli inscription of Shiladitya
  3. 8th centuryBappa Rawal (Kalbhoj); Chittor taken from the Mori rulers
  4. 10th centuryAllata rules from Ahar (Aghatapura)
  5. c. 1227Battle of Bhutala: Jaitra Singh resists Iltutmish
  6. 1303Alauddin Khalji captures Chittor; Ratan Singh killed; first saka of Chittor
  7. c. 1326Rana Hammir (Sisodia) recovers Chittor
  8. 1382–1421Rana Lakha; Zawar silver mines worked
  9. 1433Mokal murdered; Kumbha becomes Rana
  10. 1437Battle of Sarangpur: Kumbha defeats Mahmud Khalji of Malwa
  11. 1440–48Vijay Stambh built at Chittor
  12. 1456Treaty of Champaner: Malwa and Gujarat ally against Kumbha
  13. 1468Kumbha killed by his son Uda
  14. 1473–1509Rana Raimal

3Core notes

R07.1 The early Guhils: Guhaditya to Allata

  • Guhaditya (Guhil, c. 566) gave the dynasty its name. The Samoli inscription (646) of Shiladitya is an early record and mentions mining activity.
  • Bappa Rawal (8th century; usually identified with Kalbhoj): the most famous early ruler. Tradition says he took Chittor from the Mori (Maurya) ruler Man Mori, made Nagda his capital, and was devoted to Eklingji (Shiva), whose "diwan" the Mewar rulers called themselves. A gold coin attributed to him was published by G.H. Ojha.
  • Allata (10th century) ruled from Ahar (Aghatapura); he married a Huna princess, Hariya Devi, and set up a bureaucracy (the Ahar inscriptions list officials).

Bappa Rawal's date and achievements are uncertain. Later bardic accounts credit him with victories against the Arabs and even campaigns abroad; most historians accept only that he was an early, powerful Guhil ruler who held Chittor.

R07.2 Jaitra Singh to Ratan Singh: the 1303 siege

Jaitra Singh (c. 1213–53) defeated Iltutmish's invasion at the battle of Bhutala (c. 1227), though Nagda was sacked; the capital then moved to Chittor. The Sanskrit drama Hammira-mada-mardana (Jayasimha Suri) celebrates the victory. He also fought the Chaulukyas, Parmaras and Chauhans. Dasharath Sharma is usually credited with calling his reign the beginning of the medieval "golden age" of Mewar.

Ratan Singh (1302–03) faced Alauddin Khalji, who besieged Chittor for about eight months and took it in August 1303. Ratan Singh was killed; the women performed jauhar — the first saka of Chittor. Alauddin gave the fort to his son Khizr Khan and renamed it Khizrabad; later it was handed to Maldeva Songara of Jalore.

Why Alauddin attacked: Chittor's strategic position on the route to Malwa and Gujarat; his ambition to subdue Rajput forts; the prestige of the fort.

The story that Alauddin attacked to win Queen Padmini comes from Malik Muhammad Jayasi's Awadhi poem Padmavat (1540), written 237 years later. Amir Khusrau, who accompanied Alauddin, describes the siege but does not mention Padmini. Most historians treat the Padmini story — with Gora and Badal — as literary, not historical.

R07.3 The Sisodias: Hammir to Mokal

The Sisodia branch (from the village of Sisoda; rulers styled Rana rather than Rawal) took over.

  • Rana Hammir (c. 1326–64) recovered Chittor from the Songaras around 1326 and rebuilt Mewar's power. The Kumbhalgarh inscription calls him Vishamghati Panchanan ("lion in difficult battles"). He is said to have defeated Muhammad bin Tughlaq at Singoli.
  • Kshetra Singh (1364–82): wars with Malwa.
  • Rana Lakha (1382–1421): the Zawar silver and lead mines were worked, enriching Mewar; Lake Pichola was dug by a Banjara trader in his reign. He married Hansabai, sister of Ranmal Rathore of Mandore; his eldest son Chunda gave up his right to the throne so that Hansabai's son would rule — hence Chunda is called the "Bhishma of Mewar".
  • Mokal (1421–33), Hansabai's son: rebuilt the Samidheshvara temple at Chittor; murdered in 1433 by his relatives Chacha and Mera.

During Mokal's minority, Ranmal Rathore dominated the Mewar court; this rivalry continued into Kumbha's reign.

R07.4 Rana Kumbha (1433–68): warrior and statesman

Early problems. Kumbha punished his father's murderers. Ranmal Rathore's growing power alarmed the Sisodia nobles; Ranmal was killed in 1438, Chunda returned, and Ranmal's son Jodha fled to Marwar. Later Kumbha and Jodha settled the Mewar–Marwar border (the Aanwal–Baanwal agreement), and Jodha founded Jodhpur (R09).

Wars:

EnemyEvents
Malwa (Mahmud Khalji I)Battle of Sarangpur (1437): Kumbha defeated Mahmud, who had sheltered Mokal's killers; Mahmud was captured and later released. The Vijay Stambh commemorates the victory. Mahmud attacked Mewar repeatedly afterwards (Kumbhalgarh, Gagron, Mandalgarh, Ajmer) without lasting success.
GujaratConflict over Nagaur, where Kumbha intervened in a succession dispute; Gujarat's Sultans Qutbuddin and then Mahmud Begarha attacked Mewar
Malwa + GujaratTreaty of Champaner (1456): the two Sultans agreed to attack Mewar together and divide it. Kumbha held them off.

Kumbha also controlled Abu, Mandalgarh, Bundi, Ranthambhor (for a time) and Ajmer, making Mewar the leading power of Rajasthan.

R07.5 Rana Kumbha: builder and scholar

Forts. Tradition credits Kumbha with building or repairing 32 of Mewar's 84 forts. The greatest is Kumbhalgarh (completed c. 1458), designed by the architect Mandan, with a wall about 36 km long; Achalgarh (Abu) and the strengthening of Chittor are also his.

Monuments:

  • Vijay Stambh (Chittor, 1440–48): nine storeys, dedicated to Vishnu, built by the architect Jaita and his sons Napa and Puja. Its sculpture makes it a "dictionary of Hindu iconography". Its inscription (Kirti Stambh Prashasti), composed by Atri and completed by his son Mahesh, is a key source for Kumbha's reign. (Do not confuse it with the older Jain Kirti Stambh at Chittor.)
  • The Jain Ranakpur temple (1439, the Chaumukha temple of Adinath) was built by the merchant Dharana Shah (architect Depaka) in Kumbha's reign.
  • Kumbhashyam temple at Chittor; temples at Kumbhalgarh and Eklingji.

Learning. Kumbha wrote or is credited with Sangitraj, Sangit Mimansa, Sudaprabandh and Rasikapriya (a commentary on Jayadeva's Gita Govinda). Mandan wrote architectural texts (Rajavallabha, Prasada Mandana, Rupamandana); the Ekalinga Mahatmya was composed in his reign.

Titles: Abhinava Bharatacharya (for his mastery of music and drama), Hindu Suratrana (Hindu Sultan), Rana Raso (patron of poets), Halaguru, Danaguru.

End. Kumbha was killed at Kumbhalgarh in 1468 by his son Uda (Udai Singh I), who was soon driven out. Rana Raimal (1473–1509) restored order; the quarrel among his sons Prithviraj, Jaimal and Sangram Singh ended with Rana Sanga's accession (R08).

4What historians say

G.H. Ojha (Udaipur Rajya ka Itihas) and Kaviraj Shyamaldas (Vir Vinod) wrote the standard histories of Mewar; G.N. Sharma (Mewar and the Mughal Emperors) and Dasharath Sharma added critical use of inscriptions. The Kirti Stambh and Kumbhalgarh prashastis (1460) are the main sources for Kumbha; Persian chronicles of Malwa and Gujarat give a different account — each side claims Sarangpur and later battles as victories.

Historians see Kumbha as the ruler who first made Mewar a regional great power, and whose wars with Malwa and Gujarat prepared the ground for Sanga's bid for supremacy.

5Prelims fact bank

  • Guhaditya (c. 566) founded the Guhil dynasty.
  • The Samoli inscription (646) of Shiladitya mentions mining.
  • Bappa Rawal is identified with Kalbhoj; capital Nagda; devotee of Eklingji.
  • Allata ruled from Ahar (Aghatapura) and married the Huna princess Hariya Devi.
  • Jaitra Singh resisted Iltutmish at Bhutala (c. 1227); the capital moved from Nagda to Chittor.
  • Hammira-mada-mardana (Jayasimha Suri) celebrates Jaitra Singh's victory.
  • Alauddin Khalji took Chittor in August 1303; Ratan Singh killed; first saka of Chittor.
  • Chittor was renamed Khizrabad after Alauddin's son Khizr Khan.
  • The Padmini story comes from Jayasi's Padmavat (1540).
  • Rana Hammir (Sisodia) recovered Chittor c. 1326; called Vishamghati Panchanan.
  • Zawar silver mines were worked under Rana Lakha; Pichola was dug by a Banjara in his reign.
  • Chunda, who gave up the throne, is called the "Bhishma of Mewar".
  • Mokal was murdered in 1433 by Chacha and Mera.
  • Battle of Sarangpur (1437): Kumbha defeated Mahmud Khalji I of Malwa.
  • Treaty of Champaner (1456): Malwa and Gujarat allied against Kumbha.
  • Kumbhalgarh fort was designed by Mandan.
  • Vijay Stambh (1440–48): architect Jaita and sons Napa and Puja; dedicated to Vishnu.
  • The Kirti Stambh Prashasti was composed by Atri and his son Mahesh.
  • Ranakpur temple (1439) was built by Dharana Shah; architect Depaka.
  • Kumbha wrote Sangitraj, Sangit Mimansa, Sudaprabandh and Rasikapriya.
  • Kumbha's titles include Abhinava Bharatacharya, Hindu Suratrana and Rana Raso.
  • Kumbha was killed in 1468 by his son Uda.

Exam traps

  • Vijay Stambh (Kumbha, Vishnu, 15th c.) ≠ Kirti Stambh (Jain, 12th c.) — both at Chittor.
  • Bhutala: Jaitra Singh vs Iltutmish; Sarangpur: Kumbha vs Malwa; Khatoli: Sanga vs Ibrahim Lodi (R08).
  • Ranakpur was built by Dharana Shah in Kumbha's reign, not by Kumbha.
  • Treaty of Champaner (1456) was between Malwa and Gujarat — Mewar was its target, not a party.
  • Rana Hammir of Mewar (Sisodia) ≠ Hammir Dev Chauhan of Ranthambhor (R06).
  • Rasikapriya by Kumbha is a Gita Govinda commentary; Keshavdas's Rasikpriya is a different, later Hindi work.

7Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words Evaluate the achievements of Rana Kumbha as a ruler, builder and patron of learning.

How to frame it

  • Consolidation
  • Wars: Sarangpur, Champaner
  • Forts and monuments
  • Literature and titles
  • Conclusion
Model answer (79 words)

Kumbha (1433–68) punished Mokal's murderers and ended Rathore dominance at court. He defeated Mahmud Khalji of Malwa at Sarangpur (1437) and resisted the joint Malwa–Gujarat attack planned at Champaner (1456), making Mewar Rajasthan's leading state. He built or repaired 32 forts, chief among them Kumbhalgarh (architect Mandan), and the Vijay Stambh at Chittor. He wrote Sangitraj and Rasikapriya, earning the title Abhinava Bharatacharya, and patronised Mandan and Atri. His reign marks Mewar's political and cultural high point before Sanga.

5 marks · 50 words What is the historical significance of the Vijay Stambh?

How to frame it

  • Builder and date
  • Occasion
  • Art
  • Source value
Model answer (50 words)

Built by Rana Kumbha (1440–48) at Chittor to mark his victory at Sarangpur over Malwa, the nine-storeyed Vijay Stambh, by Jaita and his sons, is dedicated to Vishnu. Its sculpture is a "dictionary of Hindu iconography", and its prashasti by Atri and Mahesh is a major source for Kumbha's reign.

2 marks · 15 words Who was called the "Bhishma of Mewar", and why?

How to frame it

  • Name
  • Reason
Model answer (14 words)

Chunda, Rana Lakha's eldest son, who gave up the throne for Hansabai's son Mokal.

Unit R08 · c. 1509–1680 CE

Mewar II: Rana Sanga to Raj Singh (1509–1680)

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR3 Political and cultural achievements of prominent rulers of major dynasties
  • PR4 Cooperation and resistance with central power; administrative and revenue system in medieval Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH1 Prehistoric cultures, ancient sites; political and cultural achievements of rulers (to 18th century)

Topics this unit covers

  • Political and cultural achievements of prominent rulers of major dynasties
  • Cooperation and resistance with central power
  • Sources of Rajasthan history (Raj Prashasti, Mughal chronicles)

Mewar is the single most-asked dynasty in Rajasthan history. Expect 1–2 Prelims questions on dates, battles, titles and inscriptions, and Mains questions that ask you to evaluate (Pratap's resistance, the 1615 treaty, Khanwa).

2The big picture

In 1509 Mewar was the strongest Rajput state in north India. Over the next 170 years it faced three great powers in turn — the Lodi sultans and Babur, the Sultan of Gujarat, and the Mughal empire — and its story is a long argument about one question: should a Rajput state accept the overlordship of an imperial power, and on what terms?

Rana Sanga tried to answer by becoming the paramount power himself; he lost to Babur at Khanwa (1527). His successors survived a disastrous civil war and two sieges of Chittor (1535 and 1567–68). Maharana Pratap refused Akbar's terms and fought from the hills for 25 years. His son Amar Singh I finally made peace with Jahangir in 1615 on unusually honourable terms. Under Raj Singh (1652–1680) the old tension returned when Aurangzeb's policies turned Mewar and Marwar against the empire.

Every other Rajput state (Amber, Marwar, Bikaner) chose cooperation with the Mughals much earlier. Mewar's choice of prolonged resistance, and its final compromise, is the best case study for the syllabus line "cooperation and resistance with central power".

  1. 1509Sangram Singh (Rana Sanga) becomes Rana of Mewar
  2. 1517–1519Victories at Khatoli and Bari (Dholpur) over Ibrahim Lodi; at Gagron over Mahmud Khilji II of Malwa
  3. 17 Mar 1527Battle of Khanwa: Babur defeats Sanga's confederacy
  4. Jan 1528Death of Sanga at Kalpi
  5. 1535Bahadur Shah of Gujarat takes Chittor — second saka of Chittor
  6. 1536–1540Banbir's usurpation; Panna Dhai saves Udai Singh; Udai Singh recovers Chittor (1540)
  7. 1559Udai Singh II founds Udaipur
  8. 1567–68Akbar's siege of Chittor — third saka; Jaimal and Patta
  9. 28 Feb 1572Pratap crowned at Gogunda
  10. 18 Jun 1576Battle of Haldighati
  11. 1582Battle of Dewair; Mewar begins to recover its territory
  12. 19 Jan 1597Death of Pratap at Chavand
  13. 5 Feb 1615Mughal–Mewar treaty: Amar Singh I and Jahangir
  14. 1652–1680Raj Singh: Rajsamand lake, Raj Prashasti, conflict with Aurangzeb

3Core notes

R08.1 Rana Sanga (1509–1528): the last bid for Rajput paramountcy

Background. Sangram Singh, son of Rana Raimal, won the throne after a bitter fight with his brothers Prithviraj and Jaimal, in which he lost an eye. When he became Rana in 1509, north India was fragmented: the Lodis held Delhi, independent sultans ruled Gujarat and Malwa, and no single power dominated.

Expansion. Sanga used this vacuum:

  • Against Delhi: he defeated Ibrahim Lodi at Khatoli (1517) and Bari, near Dholpur (1518), pushing Mewar's influence towards Bayana and Agra.
  • Against Malwa: at Gagron (1519) he defeated and captured Sultan Mahmud Khilji II, then released him — generous by the Rajput code, but it left an enemy alive.
  • Against Gujarat: he intervened in the succession at Idar and raided Gujarat (around 1520).

By the mid-1520s Sanga was the most powerful ruler in north India; Babur himself counted him among the two great "pagan" powers of Hindustan.

Collision with Babur. After Panipat (April 1526) Babur held Delhi and Agra. Babur claimed in his memoirs that Sanga had promised to attack Ibrahim Lodi from the south and failed to do so; Rajput tradition denies any such agreement. Either way, both men now wanted the same prize. Sanga built a wide confederacy: Rajput chiefs of Marwar, Amber, Vagad (Dungarpur) and Chanderi (Medini Rai), together with Afghan leaders Hasan Khan Mewati and Mahmud Lodi, Ibrahim's brother. His force defeated a Mughal detachment near Bayana (February 1527), which badly shook Babur's army.

Khanwa, 17 March 1527 (near Fatehpur Sikri, in present Bharatpur district). Babur restored morale by declaring the war a jihad, publicly giving up wine and abolishing the tamgha (a trade tax) for Muslims. On the field he repeated his Panipat method: a line of carts chained together, with matchlockmen and artillery (under Ustad Ali Quli and Mustafa) behind them, and the tulughma flanking charge. The Rajput cavalry charges broke on this defence. Sanga was struck by an arrow and carried from the field; Jhala Ajja took up the royal insignia so the army would not see the Rana fall. Hasan Khan Mewati and many chiefs died. Babur took the title Ghazi.

Death. Sanga recovered and planned to fight again, but died at Kalpi in January 1528. Tradition says he was poisoned by nobles who did not want another war with Babur.

Panipat (1526)Khanwa (1527)
OpponentIbrahim Lodi (Afghan sultan)Rana Sanga's Rajput–Afghan confederacy
What it decidedWho ruled DelhiWhether the Mughals could stay in Hindustan at all
Babur's title—Ghazi

Khanwa, more than Panipat, made the Mughal empire permanent: it destroyed the only power capable of expelling Babur, and it ended the Rajput dream of a paramount Rajput state in north India. After Khanwa, Rajput states would deal with the Mughals one by one.

Assessment. Sanga was a great soldier (Tod describes his body as bearing some eighty wounds) and a skilled coalition-builder. His weaknesses were military conservatism — relying on massed cavalry against artillery — and a confederacy held together only by his personal authority.

R08.2 Crisis years: Bahadur Shah, Banbir and Panna Dhai (1528–1540)

Sanga's death opened a dangerous decade.

  • Ratan Singh II (1528–1531) died in a personal fight with the Hada ruler of Bundi.
  • Vikramaditya (1531–1536) was a boy, unpopular with the nobles; his mother, Rani Karnavati (Karmavati), effectively governed.
  • Bahadur Shah of Gujarat besieged Chittor in 1533 and again in 1534–35. Chittor fell in March 1535: Rani Karnavati led the women in jauhar and the men, under Bagh Singh of Deolia, died fighting. This is the second saka of Chittor. Humayun then defeated Bahadur Shah, and Chittor returned to Vikramaditya.

The famous story that Karnavati sent a rakhi to Humayun asking for help comes from much later accounts and is not found in the contemporary chronicles. What is certain is that Humayun did not relieve Chittor before it fell. Quote it as a tradition, not a fact.

Banbir's usurpation (1536). Banbir, an illegitimate son of Sanga's brother Prithviraj, murdered Vikramaditya and went to kill the infant prince Udai Singh. The nurse Panna Dhai placed her own son in the prince's bed; Banbir killed that child, while Udai Singh was smuggled out to Kumbhalgarh, where the fort-keeper Asha Shah Depura sheltered him. The nobles rallied to Udai Singh, crowned him at Kumbhalgarh (1537), and he expelled Banbir and recovered Chittor in 1540.

Panna Dhai (Mewar) and Gora Dhay (Marwar, who helped save infant Ajit Singh in 1679) are frequently confused — both are celebrated nurses who saved infant heirs.

R08.3 Udai Singh II (1537–1572): Udaipur and the fall of Chittor

Under Sher Shah. After Sher Shah's narrow victory over Maldev at Giri-Sumel (1544), Udai Singh avoided war by sending the keys of Chittor to Sher Shah, a token submission that kept Mewar intact.

A new capital. In 1559 Udai Singh founded Udaipur in the Girwa hills, building the Udaisagar lake. Chittor stood alone on open ground and had already fallen twice; the hills around Udaipur offered depth for defence and retreat. This decision shaped Pratap's whole strategy.

Akbar's siege of Chittor (October 1567 – February 1568). Akbar's reasons: Mewar had sheltered his enemies (including Baz Bahadur of Malwa), it controlled the routes from Agra to Gujarat, and its independence undermined Mughal prestige among the Rajputs. On the advice of his council Udai Singh withdrew to the hills and left the fort to Jaimal Rathore of Merta and Patta (Fatta) Sisodia of Kelwa. After a long siege with mines and batteries, Jaimal was killed by a shot from Akbar's own gun; the garrison performed the third saka of Chittor, and the women committed jauhar. Akbar ordered a massacre of the fort's defenders and inhabitants — Abul Fazl puts the number at about 30,000 — and announced the victory in the Fathnama-i-Chittor (March 1568), written in strongly religious language.

Akbar later honoured his opponents by placing statues of Jaimal and Patta on elephants at a gateway of the Agra fort.

Succession. Udai Singh died at Gogunda in February 1572, naming his favourite son Jagmal as heir. The senior nobles set this aside and installed the eldest son, Pratap. Jagmal went over to Akbar.

The three sakas of ChittorYearAttackerDefender / leader of jauhar
First1303Alauddin KhaljiRawal Ratan Singh; Rani Padmini (per tradition)
Second1535Bahadur Shah of GujaratBagh Singh; Rani Karnavati
Third1568AkbarJaimal Rathore and Patta Sisodia

R08.4 Maharana Pratap (1572–1597): resistance from the hills

Early life. Born on 9 May 1540 at Kumbhalgarh to Udai Singh II and Jaiwanta Bai; crowned at Gogunda on 28 February 1572, with a formal coronation later at Kumbhalgarh.

What Akbar wanted. Akbar's Rajput policy did not seek to abolish Rajput states. He wanted the ruler to accept Mughal suzerainty, attend the court, serve as a mansabdar and, usually, contract a marriage alliance. In return the ruler kept his homeland as a watan jagir. Between 1572 and 1573 Akbar sent four missions to persuade Pratap: Jalal Khan Qurchi, Man Singh of Amber, Bhagwant Das and Todar Mal. Pratap was courteous but would not go to court in person; at most he sent his son Amar Singh with Bhagwant Das. For Pratap, personal attendance meant ending the independence Mewar had kept since Bappa Rawal.

Haldighati, 18 June 1576. Akbar sent Man Singh with Asaf Khan. The armies met at the narrow pass of Haldighati near Khamnor, west of the Banas (Mughal chronicles call it the battle of Khamnor or Gogunda). Both armies were small by imperial standards: the eyewitness Badayuni gives Man Singh about 5,000 horse and Pratap about 3,000. Pratap's vanguard (harawal) was led by the Afghan Hakim Khan Sur; the Bhil leader Rana Punja held the hills; Ram Shah Tanwar of Gwalior and his sons died fighting. The first Rajput charge drove back the Mughal front, but the arrival of reserves turned the battle. Pratap, wounded, was forced to withdraw; Jhala Man (Jhala Bida) took the royal parasol and drew the enemy onto himself. Pratap's horse Chetak died soon after; his memorial stands at Balicha.

The result. The Mughals held the field and briefly occupied Gogunda, but they did not capture or kill Pratap, and Akbar was displeased with Man Singh and Asaf Khan. Militarily it was a Mughal tactical win; politically it achieved nothing.

The long war (1576–1585). Akbar came to Mewar himself (1576–77) and then sent repeated expeditions, notably under Shahbaz Khan (1577–79). Pratap avoided pitched battles, cut supply lines and retreated into the Aravallis. The minister Bhamashah is remembered for placing his family's wealth at Pratap's disposal at this low point. In 1582, at Dewair, Pratap and Amar Singh destroyed a major Mughal post (Amar Singh killed its commander Sultan Khan), and the chain of Mughal outposts in Mewar began to collapse. Tod called Dewair the "Marathon of Mewar". From 1585 Pratap ruled from a new capital, Chavand.

Why the Mughals eased off. After Jagannath Kachhwaha's campaign (1584–85), Akbar moved to Lahore to deal with the north-west frontier and sent no further major expedition. Pratap used these years to recover almost all of Mewar except Chittor and Mandalgarh. He died at Chavand on 19 January 1597; his cenotaph is at Bandoli.

Pratap's achievement is not that he defeated the Mughal empire — he did not — but that he kept Mewar's political independence alive for 25 years against the strongest state in India, using guerrilla warfare and the loyalty of Bhils, Afghans and ordinary Rajputs. This made the eventual 1615 settlement far better than anything Akbar had offered.

R08.5 Amar Singh I and the treaty of 1615

Amar Singh I (1597–1620) continued resistance against expeditions under Prince Salim, then (as emperor Jahangir) under Parvez, Mahabat Khan and Abdullah Khan. The decisive campaign was Prince Khurram's (1613–1615): he garrisoned Mewar heavily and devastated the countryside, and Mewar's nobles, exhausted by forty years of war, pressed for peace.

The treaty (5 February 1615). Its terms:

  1. The Maharana would not attend the Mughal court in person; the crown prince (Karan Singh) would represent him.
  2. Mewar would supply a contingent of 1,000 horse to the Mughal army.
  3. Chittor was returned, but could not be repaired or refortified.
  4. Mewar was not required to give a daughter in marriage to the Mughal family.

Karan Singh received a mansab of 5,000. Jahangir celebrated the settlement, and the Raj Prashasti inscription later recorded it from the Mewar side.

Compare with Amber (1562) and Marwar: those rulers attended court, gave marriage alliances and served as high mansabdars. Mewar accepted suzerainty but kept the two symbols that mattered most — no personal attendance and no marriage alliance. The 1615 treaty is the clearest example of the "honourable compromise" that Pratap's resistance made possible.

R08.6 Karan Singh, Jagat Singh I and Raj Singh (1620–1680)

  • Karan Singh (1620–1628) gave shelter to Prince Khurram (the future Shah Jahan) during his rebellion against Jahangir; tradition says Khurram stayed at the Jag Mandir palace on Lake Pichola.
  • Jagat Singh I (1628–1652) built the Jagdish temple in Udaipur (1651) and began repairing Chittor, contrary to the 1615 treaty.

Raj Singh (1652–1680). Shah Jahan responded to the Chittor repairs by sending Sadullah Khan (1654) to pull them down. During the Mughal war of succession (1658) Raj Singh backed Aurangzeb, but relations soon soured:

  • He married Charumati, the Kishangarh princess whom Aurangzeb had intended to marry.
  • When Aurangzeb's temple policy threatened Vaishnava shrines, Raj Singh gave refuge to the image of Shrinathji, installed at Sihad (Nathdwara) in 1672, and to Dwarkadhish at Kankroli.
  • He opposed the reimposition of jizya (1679), and when Aurangzeb tried to take over Marwar after Jaswant Singh's death (1678), he supported Durgadas Rathore and the infant Ajit Singh. Aurangzeb invaded Mewar (1679–80); the Rajputs fought from the hills. Raj Singh died in 1680; his son Jai Singh made peace in 1681.

Rajsamand and the Raj Prashasti. Raj Singh built the Rajsamand lake (begun 1662) as famine relief. On its embankment (Nauchowki) is the Raj Prashasti (c. 1676), composed in Sanskrit by Ranchhod Bhatt Tailang and carved on 25 black stone slabs — regarded as the largest inscription in India and a key source for Mewar's history, including the 1615 treaty.

Raj Singh's reign shows that the 1615 settlement depended on Mughal restraint. When Aurangzeb reimposed jizya and intervened in Marwar's succession, Mewar and Marwar together turned against the empire — one of the Rajput "breakdowns" historians cite when explaining the weakening of the Mughal empire.

4What historians say

Sources. Mughal chronicles — Babur's memoirs (Baburnama), Abul Fazl's Akbarnama, Badayuni's Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh (Badayuni fought at Haldighati) and Jahangir's memoirs — give the imperial view. Mewar sources include the Raj Prashasti, the Amarkavya, bardic literature and later court histories such as Kaviraj Shyamaldas's Vir Vinod. James Tod's Annals and Antiquities of Rajasthan (1829–32) drew on bardic tradition and fixed the romantic image of Mewar for English readers.

Three debates you can use in Mains:

  1. Who "won" Haldighati? Mughal chroniclers record a Mughal victory. Some Rajasthan historians (notably Chandrashekhar Sharma) argue that land grants Pratap issued in the area soon after the battle show he still controlled it; revised Rajasthan school textbooks (2017) adopted this view, which other historians dispute. The safest answer: a tactical Mughal victory that failed strategically.
  2. Resistance or obstinacy? The nationalist reading (from Tod onwards, and in the freedom movement) made Pratap a symbol of freedom against foreign rule. Historians such as Satish Chandra and G.N. Sharma frame it differently: Akbar wanted suzerainty, not annexation, and Pratap's refusal was about the status and honour of Mewar within that system — which is why the issue could be settled in 1615 on terms of personal attendance and marriage.
  3. Was Khanwa a "Hindu–Muslim" war? Babur framed it as jihad, but Sanga's side included Hasan Khan Mewati and Mahmud Lodi, and Babur's army included Hindustani allies. It was a struggle for political supremacy expressed partly in religious language.

5Prelims fact bank

  • Rana Sanga: reign 1509–1528; son of Rana Raimal.
  • Khatoli (1517) and Bari near Dholpur (1518): Sanga defeats Ibrahim Lodi.
  • Gagron (1519): Sanga defeats and captures Mahmud Khilji II of Malwa, then releases him.
  • Bayana (February 1527): Sanga defeats a Mughal force before Khanwa.
  • Khanwa: 17 March 1527, near Fatehpur Sikri (Bharatpur district); Babur takes the title Ghazi.
  • Before Khanwa Babur declared jihad, renounced wine and abolished tamgha for Muslims.
  • Jhala Ajja carried the royal insignia at Khanwa; Jhala Man (Bida) at Haldighati.
  • Sanga's allies at Khanwa included Medini Rai (Chanderi), Hasan Khan Mewati and Mahmud Lodi.
  • Sanga died at Kalpi, January 1528.
  • Second saka of Chittor: 1535, attack by Bahadur Shah of Gujarat; jauhar led by Rani Karnavati.
  • Banbir murdered Vikramaditya (1536); Panna Dhai saved Udai Singh by sacrificing her son.
  • Asha Shah Depura sheltered Udai Singh at Kumbhalgarh; Udai Singh recovered Chittor in 1540.
  • Udai Singh sent the keys of Chittor to Sher Shah (1544).
  • Udaipur founded by Udai Singh II in 1559; Udaisagar lake built by him.
  • Akbar's siege of Chittor: October 1567 – February 1568; third saka; defenders Jaimal Rathore and Patta Sisodia.
  • Fathnama-i-Chittor: Akbar's victory proclamation, March 1568.
  • Udai Singh II died at Gogunda in 1572; nobles set aside Jagmal and crowned Pratap.
  • Pratap: born 9 May 1540, Kumbhalgarh; mother Jaiwanta Bai; crowned at Gogunda 28 February 1572.
  • Akbar's four envoys to Pratap: Jalal Khan Qurchi, Man Singh, Bhagwant Das, Todar Mal (1572–73).
  • Haldighati: 18 June 1576; Mughal commanders Man Singh and Asaf Khan; called "battle of Khamnor" in Mughal sources.
  • Hakim Khan Sur led Pratap's vanguard (harawal); Rana Punja led the Bhils.
  • Badayuni was an eyewitness of Haldighati (Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh).
  • Chetak's memorial: Balicha, near Haldighati.
  • Dewair: 1582; Amar Singh kills Sultan Khan; Tod's "Marathon of Mewar".
  • Chavand: Pratap's capital from 1585; Pratap died there on 19 January 1597; cenotaph at Bandoli.
  • Mughal–Mewar treaty: 5 February 1615, Amar Singh I and Jahangir (negotiated through Prince Khurram).
  • 1615 terms: no personal attendance, 1,000 horse, Chittor not to be refortified, no marriage alliance.
  • Karan Singh sheltered rebel prince Khurram (Shah Jahan).
  • Jagdish temple, Udaipur: built by Jagat Singh I, 1651.
  • Raj Singh (1652–1680) married Charumati of Kishangarh.
  • Shrinathji installed at Sihad (Nathdwara) in 1672 under Raj Singh.
  • Rajsamand lake begun 1662 by Raj Singh as famine relief.
  • Raj Prashasti: Sanskrit, 25 slabs, by Ranchhod Bhatt Tailang, on Rajsamand's Nauchowki embankment.

Exam traps

  • Three sakas of Chittor: 1303 (Alauddin), 1535 (Bahadur Shah), 1568 (Akbar). Ranthambhor (1301) is a separate, earlier saka.
  • Jhala Ajja = Khanwa (Sanga). Jhala Man / Jhala Bida = Haldighati (Pratap).
  • Panna Dhai saved Udai Singh (Mewar). Gora Dhay helped save Ajit Singh (Marwar, 1679).
  • Mirza Raja Jai Singh (Amber) is not Maharana Jai Singh of Mewar (Raj Singh's son, who built Jaisamand lake).
  • Rajsamand = Raj Singh; Udaisagar = Udai Singh II; Jaisamand = Jai Singh. Do not mix lakes and builders.
  • Hakim Khan Sur, an Afghan, fought FOR Pratap at Haldighati; Man Singh, a Rajput, commanded the Mughals.
  • Haldighati is dated 18 June 1576 in most books; a few give 21 June. Use 18 June unless options force otherwise.
  • Udai Singh founded Udaipur (1559) before Akbar's siege of Chittor (1567–68), not after it.

6Previous-year questions (RAS)

I have not yet found a RAS Mains question on this unit's topics that I could verify against an official or citable paper. Related verified question: RAS Mains 2013 (10 marks) on Maldeo's relations with Humayun and Sher Shah — covered in Unit R09. The Mains questions below are exam-style practice, not PYQs. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

7Mains answer writing

2 marks · 15 words What was the Raj Prashasti?

How to frame it

  • Who, when, where, language
  • Why it matters
Model answer (18 words)

Sanskrit inscription on 25 slabs at Rajsamand, by Ranchhod Bhatt Tailang under Raj Singh; key Mewar history source.

5 marks · 50 words Why is the Battle of Khanwa considered more decisive than the First Battle of Panipat?

How to frame it

  • What Panipat decided vs what Khanwa decided
  • Effect on Rajput power
  • Effect on Mughal permanence
Model answer (47 words)

Panipat (1526) only replaced the Lodis at Delhi. Khanwa (1527) destroyed Rana Sanga's Rajput–Afghan confederacy, the one power able to expel Babur. It ended hopes of Rajput paramountcy, made Babur stay in Hindustan rather than return to Kabul, and proved the superiority of artillery and tulughma tactics.

5 marks · 50 words Discuss the terms and significance of the Mughal–Mewar treaty of 1615.

How to frame it

  • Parties and date
  • Four terms
  • Why it was "honourable"
Model answer (51 words)

Signed (1615) by Amar Singh I and Jahangir via Khurram. The Maharana need not attend court; the crown prince would; Mewar gave 1,000 horse; Chittor returned but unfortified; no marriage alliance. Mewar accepted suzerainty yet kept its two core symbols of status — a compromise made possible by Pratap's long resistance.

10 marks · 100 words Evaluate the significance of Maharana Pratap's struggle against Akbar. Was Haldighati a defeat for Pratap?

How to frame it

  • What Akbar wanted (suzerainty, attendance, marriage)
  • Haldighati: tactical vs strategic result
  • Guerrilla war, Dewair, Chavand; recovery of Mewar
  • Debate and conclusion: legacy and 1615
Model answer (93 words)

Akbar sought Mewar's submission, personal attendance and service, not annexation. Pratap refused all four embassies (1572–73). At Haldighati (1576) the Mughals held the field, but failed to capture Pratap or subdue Mewar — a tactical win and a strategic failure; some historians even claim a Rajput victory from later land grants. Pratap then waged guerrilla war with Bhil and Afghan support, won at Dewair (1582), ruled from Chavand and recovered most of Mewar except Chittor and Mandalgarh. His resistance became a lasting symbol of independence and won Mewar the honourable terms of 1615.

8One-screen revision card

  • Sanga beat Ibrahim Lodi (Khatoli 1517, Bari 1518) and Malwa (Gagron 1519), lost to Babur at Khanwa (17 Mar 1527).
  • Khanwa made the Mughals permanent and ended Rajput paramountcy; Babur became Ghazi.
  • Sakas of Chittor: 1303, 1535 (Bahadur Shah, Karnavati), 1568 (Akbar, Jaimal–Patta).
  • Panna Dhai saved Udai Singh; Udaipur founded 1559.
  • Pratap: born 1540, crowned 1572 (Gogunda); Haldighati 18 Jun 1576; Dewair 1582; Chavand 1585; died 1597.
  • Haldighati: Mughal tactical win, strategic failure; Hakim Khan Sur and Rana Punja with Pratap.
  • 1615 treaty: no attendance, 1,000 horse, Chittor unfortified, no marriage.
  • Raj Singh: Charumati, Shrinathji at Nathdwara (1672), Rajsamand and Raj Prashasti (25 slabs), anti-jizya, supported Ajit Singh.

9Self-test

  1. Name the two battles in which Rana Sanga defeated Ibrahim Lodi.

    Answer

    Khatoli (1517) and Bari, near Dholpur (1518).

  2. Which three measures did Babur take before Khanwa to raise his army's morale?

    Answer

    Declared jihad, renounced wine, and abolished tamgha for Muslims.

  3. Who carried the royal insignia at Khanwa, and who at Haldighati?

    Answer

    Jhala Ajja at Khanwa; Jhala Man (Jhala Bida) at Haldighati.

  4. Who led the jauhar during the second saka of Chittor, and who was the attacker?

    Answer

    Rani Karnavati; Bahadur Shah of Gujarat (1535).

  5. Why did Udai Singh II found Udaipur?

    Answer

    Chittor on its isolated hill was vulnerable (it had fallen twice); the Girwa hills around Udaipur offered depth for defence and retreat.

  6. Name Akbar's four envoys to Pratap.

    Answer

    Jalal Khan Qurchi, Man Singh, Bhagwant Das and Todar Mal.

  7. Who led Pratap's vanguard at Haldighati?

    Answer

    Hakim Khan Sur, an Afghan.

  8. Why is Dewair (1582) called the "Marathon of Mewar", and by whom?

    Answer

    By James Tod: it marked the turning point after which Mughal outposts in Mewar collapsed and Pratap recovered his territory.

  9. List the four terms of the 1615 treaty.

    Answer

    No personal attendance by the Maharana; 1,000 horse; Chittor returned but not to be refortified; no marriage alliance.

  10. Who composed the Raj Prashasti and on how many slabs is it carved?

    Answer

    Ranchhod Bhatt Tailang; 25 slabs.

जौहरJauharMass self-immolation of women in a besieged fort to avoid capture.
हरावलHarawalVanguard of an army.
चंदावलChandawalRearguard of an army.
थानाThanaMilitary outpost (Mughal posts in Mewar).
प्रशस्तिPrashastiEulogistic inscription recording a ruler's genealogy and deeds.
तुलुगमाTulughmaBabur's flanking tactic: wings wheel round to strike the enemy's rear.
तमगाTamghaA trade/transit tax abolished by Babur for Muslims before Khanwa.

Unit R09 · c. 1240 – 1818 CE

The Rathores of Marwar

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR3 Political and cultural achievements of prominent rulers of major dynasties
  • PR4 Cooperation and resistance with central power; administrative and revenue system in medieval Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH1 Prehistoric cultures, ancient sites; political and cultural achievements of rulers (to 18th century)

Topics this unit covers

  • Major dynasties of Rajasthan and their achievements
  • Rao Jodha, Maldeo, Chandrasen, Jaswant Singh, Durgadas

Tier B. RAS Mains 2013 asked a 10-mark question on Maldeo's relations with Humayun and Sher Shah. Giri-Sumel, Chandrasen, Durgadas and the Rathore war of 1679–1707 recur.

2The big picture

The Rathores came to Marwar (the Jodhpur region) in the 13th century as a small band and became the largest Rajput state by area. Three phases matter:

  1. State building (1240–1489): Rao Siha settles near Pali; Rao Chunda takes Mandore; Rao Jodha founds Jodhpur (1459). Rathore offshoots found Bikaner and Merta.
  2. Peak and conflict (1532–81): Rao Maldeo makes Marwar the strongest power in Rajasthan, clashes with Sher Shah at Giri-Sumel (1544); his son Chandrasen refuses to submit to Akbar.
  3. Mughal partnership and revolt (1583–1707): rulers serve the Mughals (Udai Singh, Jaswant Singh); after Jaswant's death Aurangzeb tries to annex Marwar, and Durgadas Rathore leads a 30-year resistance for the infant Ajit Singh.

Marwar shows the whole range of Rajput responses to central power: resistance (Maldeo, Chandrasen), alliance (Udai Singh, Jaswant Singh) and revolt (Durgadas). Mains questions on Rajput–Mughal relations draw heavily on it (see also R13).

  1. 1273Devli (memorial stone) of Rao Siha at Bithu, Pali
  2. c. 1395Rao Chunda takes Mandore
  3. 1438Ranmal killed at Chittor; Jodha flees
  4. 1459Rao Jodha founds Jodhpur and Mehrangarh
  5. 1488Rao Bika (son of Jodha) founds Bikaner
  6. 1532–62Rao Maldeo
  7. 1541–42Humayun seeks Maldeo's help, then flees
  8. 5 Jan 1544Battle of Giri-Sumel: Sher Shah defeats the Rathores
  9. 1562–81Rao Chandrasen resists Akbar
  10. 1570Nagaur darbar of Akbar
  11. 1583Udai Singh ("Mota Raja") accepted as ruler by Akbar
  12. 1658–59Jaswant Singh at Dharmat and Khajwa
  13. Dec 1678Jaswant Singh dies at Jamrud
  14. 1679–1707Rathore war: Durgadas and Ajit Singh
  15. 1730Khejarli: Amrita Devi and the Bishnois sacrificed to save trees
  16. 1787Battle of Tunga: Rathores and Kachhwahas check Mahadji Scindia
  17. 1818Treaty of Marwar with the East India Company

3Core notes

R09.1 Arrival and early rulers: Siha to Ranmal

The Rathores entered Marwar as a small band of warriors in a dry region of scattered chiefs. Rao Siha, said by tradition to descend from the Gahadavala Jaichand of Kannauj, settled near Pali in the mid-13th century. His memorial stone (devli) at Bithu (1273) is the earliest dated Rathore record. For over a century his successors grew slowly by alliances and raids. Rao Chunda took Mandore from the Pratiharas (c. 1395; tradition says it came as dowry), extended Rathore rule to Nagaur, and was killed in 1423. His son Ranmal (Ridmal) gained great influence in Mewar through his sister Hansabai, wife of Rana Lakha, until the Sisodia nobles, fearing a Rathore takeover, killed him at Chittor in 1438 (R07).

The descent of the Rathores from Jaichand is a bardic claim; some historians link them instead to the Rashtrakutas. Treat it as tradition.

R09.2 Rao Jodha and the founding of Jodhpur

Rao Jodha (1438–89) fled Chittor after his father's murder, recovered Mandore after years of fighting, and settled the border with Mewar through the Aanwal–Baanwal agreement with Rana Kumbha, ending a long feud. In May 1459 he founded Jodhpur and built Mehrangarh fort on the Chidiyatunk hill, a stronger and more central site than Mandore.

Jodha's sons carried Rathore power further. Bika founded Bikaner (1488; R10) and Duda founded the Merta line (the poet-saint Mirabai was Duda's granddaughter). Rao Ganga (1515–32) sent troops under his son Maldeo to fight with Rana Sanga at Khanwa (1527).

Why the Rathores spread so widely. Rathore custom gave land to every brother and son, and younger sons who received little went out to win kingdoms of their own. This is how one family came to rule Jodhpur, Bikaner, Merta, Kishangarh and smaller states. It made the clan powerful but also divided: the branches were often rivals, and Mughal and later powers could play one Rathore house against another, as Sher Shah did with Merta and Bikaner against Maldeo.

R09.3 Rao Maldeo (1532–62)

Expansion. Maldeo made Marwar the largest state in Rajasthan, taking Nagaur, Ajmer, Merta, Siwana, Jalore, Sambhar and parts of Bikaner. He killed Rao Jaitsi of Bikaner at the battle of Pahoba (Saheba) (1542). He fortified Sojat, Siwana and Merta (Malkot). Persian writers ranked him among the most powerful rulers of India. But his conquests came largely at the expense of fellow Rathores and neighbours, and the chiefs he dispossessed (Biramdev of Merta, Kalyanmal of Bikaner) became his bitter enemies.

Maldeo and Humayun. After his defeat at Kannauj (1540), the fugitive Humayun was invited by Maldeo, who offered help. Humayun delayed in Sindh; when he reached Marwar (1542) he found Maldeo hostile and fled to Amarkot, where Akbar was born.

Why did Maldeo change his mind? Mughal writers (Jauhar, Gulbadan) accuse him of treachery. Others point out that Humayun arrived over a year late, with few men, while Sher Shah was pressing Maldeo to hand him over — so Maldeo acted from caution, not betrayal.

Maldeo and Sher Shah. Sher Shah, alarmed at Maldeo's power and urged by the dispossessed chiefs, marched on Marwar. At the battle of Giri-Sumel (near Jaitaran, Pali), 5 January 1544, he planted forged letters suggesting that Maldeo's nobles would betray him. Maldeo withdrew; his commanders Jaita and Kumpa fought on and were killed. Sher Shah reportedly said he had nearly lost the empire of Hindustan "for a handful of millet (bajra)". He occupied Jodhpur, but Maldeo recovered it after Sher Shah's death (1545). Later Maldeo lost Merta and Ajmer and faced Akbar's rising power, but he remained independent until his death.

R09.4 Chandrasen, Udai Singh and the Mughal alliance

Rao Chandrasen (1562–81), Maldeo's younger son, faced his brothers (Ram and Udai Singh) backed by Akbar. Jodhpur fell to the Mughals (1564). At the Nagaur darbar (1570) several Rajput rulers submitted; Chandrasen came but left without submitting. He fought on from Bhadrajun, Siwana and the hills of Sarawan until his death (1581). He is called the "forgotten king" and the "Pratap of Marwar", since his resistance preceded Pratap's.

His brother Udai Singh ("Mota Raja", 1583–95) accepted Mughal service and was given Jodhpur. His daughter Manmati (Jagat Gosain) married Prince Salim (1586); she was the mother of Shah Jahan. Sur Singh (1595–1619) and Gaj Singh I (1619–38) served the Mughals in the Deccan and Gujarat; Jahangir gave Gaj Singh the title Dalthamban.

Why Marwar accepted the Mughal alliance. Resistance had cost Chandrasen his capital and left him a fugitive, while his brothers who submitted got Jodhpur back. Mughal service offered a secure throne backed by the emperor, high rank (mansab), jagirs and pay far beyond what Marwar's dry lands yielded, and a career in imperial wars. The price was loss of independence: the emperor could decide the succession. That price became clear in 1678.

Jaswant Singh I (1638–78) became one of the highest Mughal mansabdars. In the war of succession he led Shah Jahan's army against Aurangzeb and Murad at Dharmat (1658) and lost; at Khajwa (1659) he deserted Aurangzeb's camp and plundered it, but was later pardoned and posted to Gujarat, the Deccan and finally the north-west. He was also a scholar, author of Bhasha Bhushan (on poetics), Anand Vilas and Aparokshasiddhanta. He died at Jamrud (Khyber) in December 1678 without a living heir.

R09.5 Durgadas and the Rathore war (1679–1707)

After Jaswant's death Aurangzeb took Marwar under direct control and gave it to Indra Singh (a grand-nephew). Jaswant's posthumous son Ajit Singh, born at Lahore in 1679, was demanded by Aurangzeb to be raised at court. Durgadas Rathore, son of Jaswant's minister Askaran, escaped from Delhi with the infant and hid him (at Kalindri, Sirohi). Mewar's Rana Raj Singh supported the Rathores, and Aurangzeb fought both (R08).

In 1681 Aurangzeb's son Prince Akbar rebelled with Rathore and Sisodia backing; the revolt failed, and Durgadas escorted Akbar to the Maratha court of Sambhaji. Durgadas looked after Akbar's children, Buland Akhtar and Safiyat-un-nisa, had them taught the Quran, and returned them to Aurangzeb, an act often cited for his integrity. Guerrilla war continued until Aurangzeb's death, and Ajit Singh recovered Jodhpur in 1707.

Why the Rathore war mattered. By interfering in the succession of a loyal ally, Aurangzeb turned the Rathores, and with them Mewar, from partners into enemies. Thirty years of war tied down Mughal troops, encouraged Prince Akbar's rebellion and linked the Rajputs to the Marathas. Jadunath Sarkar and Satish Chandra see it as a political blunder that weakened the Rajput alliance on which Akbar had built the empire.

Later: Ajit Singh joined the Sayyid brothers' politics, married his daughter to Farrukhsiyar (1715) and helped depose him (1719). He exiled Durgadas, who died at Ujjain (1718). Ajit was killed by his son Bakht Singh (1724).

R09.6 The 18th century to 1818

With Mughal power gone, Marwar faced new pressures. Abhay Singh (1724–49) served as Mughal governor of Gujarat. In 1730, at Khejarli, Amrita Devi and 363 Bishnois were killed trying to stop the felling of khejri trees for the ruler's lime kilns. Under Vijay Singh (1752–93) Maratha pressure grew: at Tunga (1787) the Rathores and Kachhwahas defeated Mahadji Scindia, but he won at Patan and Merta (1790). Vijay Singh issued the Vijayshahi coins.

Man Singh (1803–43) was a patron of the Nath sect (Ayas Devnath; Mahamandir in Jodhpur). His rivalry with Jaipur over the hand of Krishnakumari of Mewar led to war (battle of Gingoli, 1807) and her poisoning (1810). Exhausted by Maratha and Pindari raids and noble revolts, he signed the treaty with the East India Company in January 1818 (R15).

How this unit connects.

  • Neighbours: Mewar and Ranmal, Kumbha, Raj Singh (R07, R08); Bikaner and other Rathore states (R10); Amber (R11).
  • Central powers: Rajput–Mughal relations, the Nagaur darbar and Aurangzeb's policy (R13); Sher Shah, Humayun and Akbar (I21).
  • Administration and sources: Marwar's hath ka kurab, rekh and Nainsi's Vigat (R14, R01).
  • After: Marathas and the 1818 treaties (R15); Marwar's Lok Parishad and integration (R20, R21); the 1857 revolt at Auwa (R16).
  • Culture: Mirabai and the bhakti movement (I20); the Bishnois of Khejarli.

4What historians say

Sources include Muhnot Nainsi's Khyat and Marwar ra Pargana ri Vigat (17th c.), the Jodhpur Rajya ki Khyat, Bankidas's writings, and Persian chronicles (Abbas Khan Sarwani's Tarikh-i-Sher Shahi for Giri-Sumel; Jauhar's and Gulbadan's memoirs for Humayun). Modern accounts: G.H. Ojha and Bisheshwar Nath Reu (Marwar ka Itihas); V.S. Bhargava, Marwar and the Mughal Emperors.

On Maldeo, Mughal and nationalist writers disagree: the former blame him for abandoning Humayun, the latter praise him for resisting Sher Shah. On the Rathore war, Jadunath Sarkar and Satish Chandra see Aurangzeb's handling of the Marwar succession as a political blunder that turned allies into enemies.

5Prelims fact bank

  • Rao Siha's devli at Bithu (Pali) is dated 1273.
  • Rao Chunda took Mandore c. 1395.
  • Rao Jodha founded Jodhpur in 1459; Mehrangarh is on the Chidiyatunk hill.
  • The Aanwal–Baanwal agreement fixed the Mewar–Marwar border between Kumbha and Jodha.
  • Rao Bika founded Bikaner (1488); Rao Duda founded the Merta line.
  • Maldeo killed Rao Jaitsi of Bikaner at Pahoba (Saheba), 1542.
  • Battle of Giri-Sumel (Jaitaran, Pali): 5 January 1544; Jaita and Kumpa killed.
  • Sher Shah's "handful of bajra" remark refers to Giri-Sumel.
  • Humayun fled from Marwar to Amarkot, where Akbar was born (1542).
  • Chandrasen walked out of the Nagaur darbar (1570) without submitting.
  • Chandrasen is called the "Pratap of Marwar" and the "forgotten king".
  • Udai Singh of Marwar was called "Mota Raja".
  • Manmati (Jagat Gosain), Udai Singh's daughter, was the mother of Shah Jahan.
  • Gaj Singh I received the title Dalthamban from Jahangir.
  • Jaswant Singh I lost at Dharmat (1658) and died at Jamrud (1678).
  • Jaswant Singh I wrote Bhasha Bhushan.
  • Durgadas Rathore protected Ajit Singh; Ajit recovered Jodhpur in 1707.
  • Prince Akbar rebelled against Aurangzeb in 1681 with Rathore support.
  • Khejarli (1730): Amrita Devi and 363 Bishnois died protecting khejri trees.
  • Battle of Tunga (1787): Rathores and Kachhwahas defeated Mahadji Scindia.
  • Man Singh of Jodhpur was a patron of the Nath sect.

Exam traps

  • Giri-Sumel (1544) was fought against Sher Shah, not Humayun or Akbar.
  • Pahoba/Saheba: Maldeo vs Jaitsi of Bikaner; Giri-Sumel: Maldeo vs Sher Shah.
  • Chandrasen (Marwar) resisted Akbar before Pratap (Mewar); both are called resisters.
  • Jagat Gosain (Marwar princess) was Shah Jahan's mother — not Jodhabai of Amber (Akbar's wife, R11).
  • Dharmat (1658): Jaswant lost to Aurangzeb; Khajwa (1659): Jaswant deserted Aurangzeb.
  • Khejarli is in Jodhpur district; the ruler was Abhay Singh.

6Previous-year questions (RAS)

ExamQuestionMarks
RAS Pre 2024Rao Chunda: (A) he got Mandore in dowry; (B) Ranmal succeeded him immediately. Which is correct? — Answer (RPSC final key): Only A—
RAS Mains 2013Describe the relations of Rao Maldeo with Humayun and Sher Shah. (paraphrased)10

Listed by RajRAS; wording paraphrased. RAS Pre questions come from the history sections of the 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers, paraphrased, with answers as per the RPSC final answer keys (2018 not used: its key was not available).

7Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words Marwar's history shows resistance, alliance and revolt towards central powers. Discuss.

How to frame it

  • Resistance: Maldeo, Chandrasen
  • Alliance: Udai Singh to Jaswant Singh
  • Revolt: Durgadas
  • Why policy changed
  • Conclusion
Model answer (107 words)

Maldeo resisted Sher Shah and nearly defeated him at Giri-Sumel (1544); Chandrasen walked out of the Nagaur darbar (1570) and fought Akbar until 1581, losing Jodhpur. His brother Udai Singh accepted Mughal service (1583), married his daughter to Salim and regained Jodhpur; Gaj Singh and Jaswant Singh rose high in Mughal service. The alliance gave security, rank and wealth but let the emperor decide succession. When Aurangzeb took Marwar after Jaswant's death (1678), Durgadas led a thirty-year war for Ajit Singh, backed Prince Akbar's revolt and won back Jodhpur (1707). Marwar's policy changed with the balance of power and with how far the emperor respected its autonomy.

10 marks · 100 words · RAS Mains 2013 Describe the relations of Rao Maldeo with Humayun and Sher Shah.

How to frame it

  • Maldeo's power
  • Humayun: invitation, delay, hostility
  • Sher Shah: causes
  • Giri-Sumel
  • Assessment
Model answer (92 words)

Maldeo (1532–62) had made Marwar Rajasthan's strongest state. After Kannauj (1540), he invited the fugitive Humayun, but Humayun arrived a year late (1542), weak, while Sher Shah demanded his surrender; Maldeo turned hostile and Humayun fled to Amarkot. Mughal writers call this treachery; others call it prudence. Sher Shah, alarmed by Maldeo's expansion and urged by dispossessed chiefs, invaded. At Giri-Sumel (January 1544) forged letters made Maldeo withdraw; Jaita and Kumpa died fighting. Sher Shah admitted near-defeat "for a handful of bajra". Maldeo regained Jodhpur in 1545. His policy was cautious self-interest.

5 marks · 50 words Assess the role of Durgadas Rathore in the history of Marwar.

How to frame it

  • Context
  • Protecting Ajit
  • Prince Akbar
  • Result
Model answer (43 words)

After Jaswant Singh's death (1678), Aurangzeb annexed Marwar. Durgadas rescued the infant Ajit Singh, led a thirty-year guerrilla war, backed Prince Akbar's revolt (1681) and honourably returned Akbar's children. His persistence restored Ajit to Jodhpur in 1707, making him a symbol of loyalty.

2 marks · 15 words What was the significance of the battle of Giri-Sumel?

How to frame it

  • Who
  • Result
Model answer (13 words)

1544: Sher Shah narrowly defeated Maldeo through forged letters, occupying Marwar until 1545.

8One-screen revision card

  • Siha (Bithu 1273) → Chunda (Mandore c. 1395) → Ranmal (killed 1438) → Jodha (Jodhpur 1459; Aanwal–Baanwal).
  • Jodha's sons: Bika → Bikaner 1488; Duda → Merta (Mirabai).
  • Maldeo: Pahoba 1542 (Jaitsi); Humayun 1542; Giri-Sumel 5 Jan 1544 (Jaita, Kumpa; "bajra").
  • Chandrasen: Nagaur darbar 1570 walkout; "Pratap of Marwar".
  • Udai Singh "Mota Raja" → Jagat Gosain (Shah Jahan's mother). Gaj Singh — Dalthamban.
  • Jaswant Singh I: Dharmat 1658, Khajwa 1659, Jamrud 1678, Bhasha Bhushan.
  • Durgadas + Ajit Singh: 1679–1707; Prince Akbar 1681.
  • Khejarli 1730 (Abhay Singh); Tunga 1787; Man Singh — Nath sect, treaty 1818.

9Self-test

  1. What is the earliest dated Rathore record?

    Answer

    Rao Siha's devli at Bithu (1273).

  2. Who founded Jodhpur, and when?

    Answer

    Rao Jodha, 1459.

  3. Which agreement settled the Mewar–Marwar border?

    Answer

    The Aanwal–Baanwal agreement between Kumbha and Jodha.

  4. Whom did Maldeo defeat at Pahoba?

    Answer

    Rao Jaitsi of Bikaner (1542).

  5. How did Sher Shah win at Giri-Sumel?

    Answer

    Forged letters made Maldeo doubt his nobles and withdraw.

  6. Which two Rathore commanders died at Giri-Sumel?

    Answer

    Jaita and Kumpa.

  7. Why is Chandrasen called the "Pratap of Marwar"?

    Answer

    He resisted Akbar to the end, before Pratap did.

  8. Who was Jagat Gosain?

    Answer

    Daughter of Udai Singh of Marwar, wife of Jahangir and mother of Shah Jahan.

  9. Where did Jaswant Singh I die?

    Answer

    Jamrud, in December 1678.

  10. Whom did Durgadas protect?

    Answer

    The infant Ajit Singh.

  11. What happened at Khejarli in 1730?

    Answer

    Amrita Devi and 363 Bishnois were killed trying to save khejri trees.

  12. Why did Udai Singh accept Mughal service when Chandrasen had resisted?

    Answer

    Resistance had cost Jodhpur; service brought a secure throne, rank, jagirs and pay.

  13. How did Aurangzeb's handling of the Marwar succession weaken the empire?

    Answer

    It turned loyal Rathore and Sisodia allies into enemies, tied down troops for 30 years and fed Prince Akbar's revolt.

  14. Why did Sher Shah invade Marwar?

    Answer

    He feared Maldeo's growing power and was urged on by chiefs Maldeo had dispossessed (Merta, Bikaner).

Unit R10 · c. 1155 – 1947 CE

Bikaner, Jaisalmer and Sirohi

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR3 Political and cultural achievements of prominent rulers of major dynasties
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH1 Prehistoric cultures, ancient sites; political and cultural achievements of rulers (to 18th century)

Topics this unit covers

  • Major dynasties of Rajasthan and their achievements

Rai Singh, Anup Singh and Ganga Singh of Bikaner; the "dhai saka" of Jaisalmer; and Rao Surtan of Sirohi (Dattani) are the usual Prelims targets.

2The big picture

Three desert and hill states of western Rajasthan, each with its own pattern:

  • Bikaner (Rathores, from 1488): a Rathore offshoot that broke from Jodhpur, allied early with the Mughals, and produced loyal generals (Rai Singh), scholars (Anup Singh) and a modernising ruler (Ganga Singh, who brought canal irrigation).
  • Jaisalmer (Bhatis, from 1155): a trade-route state on the caravan roads across the Thar, remembered for its sakas and its golden fort.
  • Sirohi (Deora Chauhans, from the 14th century): a small hill state around Abu, notable for Rao Surtan's victory over Mughal forces at Dattani (1583).

These states show that "Rajasthan history" is not just Mewar and Marwar. Prelims often asks single facts — founders, forts, titles — from them, and Ganga Singh links the medieval states to modern development.

  1. 1155Rawal Jaisal founds Jaisalmer
  2. c. 1311Lumba Deora takes Abu; Deora rule begins
  3. 1425Sahasmal founds Sirohi
  4. 1488Rao Bika founds Bikaner
  5. 1534Rao Jaitsi repulses Kamran at Ratighati
  6. 1542Jaitsi killed by Maldeo at Pahoba
  7. 1570Kalyanmal (Bikaner) and Har Rai (Jaisalmer) submit to Akbar at Nagaur
  8. 1583Battle of Dattani: Rao Surtan defeats Mughal forces
  9. 1589–94Junagarh fort, Bikaner, built under Rai Singh
  10. 1805Surat Singh takes Bhatner and renames it Hanumangarh
  11. 1818Treaties of Bikaner and Jaisalmer with the Company
  12. 1823Sirohi's treaty with the Company
  13. 1927Ganga Canal opened

3Core notes

R10.1 Bikaner: Bika to Kalyanmal

  • Rao Bika, son of Rao Jodha, left Jodhpur with his uncle Kandhal, conquered the Jangal region (with the blessing, tradition says, of Karni Mata of Deshnoke) and founded Bikaner in 1488.
  • Lunkaran (1504–26): known for generosity; called the "Karna of the Kali age" by court poets.
  • Jaitsi (1526–42): repulsed Babur's son Kamran at Ratighati (1534), celebrated in Bithu Suja's Rao Jaitsi ro Chhand; killed by Maldeo at Pahoba (1542).
  • Kalyanmal (1542–74): helped Sher Shah against Maldeo (Giri-Sumel, 1544); submitted to Akbar at the Nagaur darbar (1570). His son Prithviraj Rathore was a great Dingal poet (Veli Krisan Rukmani ri) and served at Akbar's court.

R10.2 Bikaner under the Mughals and the British

RulerKey points
Rai Singh (1574–1612)One of Akbar's most trusted generals (Gujarat, Kabul, Deccan campaigns); built Junagarh fort (1589–94) with his minister Karmachand; the Rai Singh Prashasti (1594) at the fort records Bikaner's history; wrote Rai Singh Mahotsav; later quarrelled with Karmachand
Karan Singh (1631–69)Called Jangaldhar Badshah
Anup Singh (1669–98)Served Aurangzeb in the Deccan; collected Sanskrit manuscripts there, forming the Anup Sanskrit Library; scholar and patron (Anup Vivek)
Surat Singh (1787–1828)Took Bhatner from the Bhatis (1805) and renamed it Hanumangarh; treaty with the Company (1818)
Sardar SinghHelped the British in 1857 (R16)
Ganga Singh (1887–1943)Built the Ganga Canal from the Sutlej (opened 1927), turning Ganganagar into farmland; his camel corps, the Ganga Risala, served in China (1900) and in the First World War; represented India at the Imperial War Cabinet and the Paris Peace Conference (1919); first Chancellor of the Chamber of Princes (1921); attended the Round Table Conferences

Ganga Singh is praised as a moderniser (canal, railways, courts, a legislative assembly in 1913) but his regime also repressed political activity; the Bikaner Praja Mandal faced bans and arrests (R20). Present both sides.

R10.3 Jaisalmer: the Bhatis

The Bhatis claim descent from the Yadavas (Krishna). Earlier centres were Tanot, Derawar and Lodurva. Rawal Jaisal founded Jaisalmer in 1155 and built the fort on Trikuta hill — the yellow-sandstone Sonar Qila.

The "dhai saka" (two and a half sakas):

  1. Under Rawal Mularaj, against Alauddin Khalji — full saka (jauhar and last fight).
  2. Under Rawal Duda, against Firoz Shah Tughlaq — full saka.
  3. Under Rawal Lunkaran (1550), against Amir Ali of Kandahar — the men fought to death but no jauhar was performed, so it counts as a "half" saka.

Mughal period: Rawal Har Rai submitted to Akbar at Nagaur (1570) and married a daughter to him. Jaisalmer's wealth came from the caravan trade across the Thar; it declined when sea routes and British rule diverted trade.

Later: treaty with the Company (1818, Mularaj II). The 19th-century minister Salim Singh was notorious for oppression; Maharawal Jawahar Singh's rule saw the persecution of Sagarmal Gopa (R19).

R10.4 Sirohi: the Deora Chauhans

  • A branch of the Chauhans of Jalore–Nadol, the Deoras, under Lumba took Abu and Chandravati from the Parmaras (c. 1311).
  • Sahasmal founded Sirohi in 1425. Rana Kumbha seized Abu for a time.
  • Rao Surtan (1571–1610) refused to submit to Akbar. At the battle of Dattani (1583) he defeated an imperial force; Pratap's brother Jagmal, who had been given part of Sirohi by Akbar, was killed. The poet Dursa Adha praised him.
  • Sirohi was the last Rajasthan state to sign a treaty with the Company (1823).
  • Its integration (1950, with Abu and Delwara added only in 1956) is covered in R21.

4What historians say

Bikaner's history is well documented by Dayaldas's Khyat, the Rai Singh Prashasti, and modern works by G.H. Ojha (Bikaner Rajya ka Itihas) and Karni Singh (The Relations of the House of Bikaner with the Central Powers). Jaisalmer's sakas rest largely on bardic tradition and the Jaisalmer ri Khyat; their exact dates are uncertain. For Ganga Singh, K.M. Panikkar's biography is admiring; recent historians balance his reforms against his autocracy.

5Prelims fact bank

  • Rao Bika, son of Rao Jodha, founded Bikaner in 1488.
  • Rao Jaitsi defeated Kamran at Ratighati (1534).
  • Bithu Suja wrote Rao Jaitsi ro Chhand.
  • Kalyanmal submitted to Akbar at the Nagaur darbar (1570).
  • Prithviraj Rathore wrote Veli Krisan Rukmani ri.
  • Junagarh fort (1589–94) was built under Rai Singh by his minister Karmachand.
  • The Rai Singh Prashasti (1594) is at Junagarh.
  • Karan Singh of Bikaner was called Jangaldhar Badshah.
  • Anup Singh founded the Anup Sanskrit Library.
  • Surat Singh renamed Bhatner as Hanumangarh (1805).
  • Ganga Canal (from the Sutlej) opened in 1927 under Ganga Singh.
  • Ganga Singh's camel corps was the Ganga Risala.
  • Ganga Singh was the first Chancellor of the Chamber of Princes (1921).
  • Rawal Jaisal founded Jaisalmer in 1155; the fort is the Sonar Qila on Trikuta hill.
  • Lodurva was an earlier Bhati capital.
  • Jaisalmer's "half saka" was under Rawal Lunkaran (1550): no jauhar.
  • Lumba Deora took Abu from the Parmaras c. 1311.
  • Sahasmal founded Sirohi in 1425.
  • Battle of Dattani (1583): Rao Surtan defeated Mughal forces; Jagmal killed.
  • Sirohi signed its treaty with the Company in 1823, the last in Rajasthan.

Exam traps

  • Bika founded Bikaner (1488); Jodha founded Jodhpur (1459) — father and son.
  • Prithviraj Rathore (Bikaner poet, Veli) ≠ Prithviraj Chauhan (Ajmer, R06).
  • Hanumangarh = old Bhatner; renamed by Surat Singh of Bikaner, not by the Bhatis.
  • Jaisalmer has "dhai" (2½) sakas; Chittor has three sakas (R07, R08).
  • Sirohi's treaty was 1823, not 1818.
  • Ganga Canal comes from the Sutlej; the Indira Gandhi Canal (later) from the Harike barrage.

RAS Pre questions come from the history sections of the 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers, paraphrased, with answers as per the RPSC final answer keys (2018 not used: its key was not available).

7Mains answer writing

5 marks · 50 words Evaluate the contribution of Maharaja Ganga Singh to the development of Bikaner.

How to frame it

  • Canal
  • Administration
  • Wider role
  • Limits
Model answer (49 words)

Ganga Singh (1887–1943) built the Ganga Canal (1927), turning desert into farmland. He introduced railways, courts and a legislative assembly, led the Ganga Risala abroad, and was the first Chancellor of the Chamber of Princes. Yet he suppressed political dissent, including the Praja Mandal. He was a modernising autocrat.

Unit R11 · c. 1137 – 1947 CE

Amber–Jaipur, Shekhawati and Alwar

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR3 Political and cultural achievements of prominent rulers of major dynasties
  • PR4 Cooperation and resistance with central power; administrative and revenue system in medieval Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH1 Prehistoric cultures, ancient sites; political and cultural achievements of rulers (to 18th century)

Topics this unit covers

  • Major dynasties of Rajasthan and their achievements
  • Sawai Jai Singh: Jaipur, observatories, Hurda

Bharmal's alliance (1562), Man Singh I, Mirza Raja Jai Singh (Purandar) and above all Sawai Jai Singh II (Jaipur, Jantar Mantar, Hurda) are standard Prelims and Mains topics.

2The big picture

The Kachhwahas of Dhundhar (the Jaipur region) were a modest power until 1562, when Raja Bharmal became the first major Rajput ruler to ally with Akbar. For the next 150 years Kachhwaha rulers were among the empire's leading generals and governors — Man Singh I in Kabul and Bengal, Mirza Raja Jai Singh against Shivaji. The partnership made Amber rich.

In the 18th century Sawai Jai Singh II used that wealth and Mughal decline to build a planned capital, Jaipur (1727), and astronomical observatories, while trying to unite Rajput rulers against the Marathas (Hurda, 1734). Branches of the clan created Shekhawati (Rao Shekha) and Alwar (the Narukas).

Amber is the clearest case of Rajput–Mughal cooperation; compare it with Mewar's resistance (R08) in any answer on Rajput policy (R13).

  1. c. 1137Dulherai establishes Kachhwaha rule in Dhundhar (Dausa)
  2. 1433–88Rao Shekha founds Shekhawati (Amarsar)
  3. 1562Bharmal allies with Akbar; daughter Harkha Bai marries Akbar
  4. 1576Man Singh I leads the Mughal army at Haldighati
  5. 1589–1614Man Singh I rules Amber; governor of Kabul, Bihar, Bengal
  6. 1665Treaty of Purandar: Mirza Raja Jai Singh and Shivaji
  7. 1700–43Sawai Jai Singh II
  8. 18 Nov 1727Foundation of Jaipur
  9. 17 July 1734Hurda conference of Rajput rulers
  10. 1747–48Ishwari Singh–Madho Singh succession war (Rajmahal, Bagru)
  11. 1775Pratap Singh Naruka founds Alwar state
  12. 1799Hawa Mahal built under Pratap Singh of Jaipur
  13. 1803Alwar (and Bharatpur) sign the earliest treaties in Rajasthan with the Company
  14. 1818Jaipur's treaty with the Company

3Core notes

R11.1 Origins and early rulers

  • The Kachhwahas claimed descent from Kush, son of Rama. Dulherai came from Narwar (near Gwalior) in the 12th century and won Dhundhar from the Badgujars and Meenas; early capitals were Dausa and Ramgarh.
  • Kakil Dev took Amber from the Meenas; it remained the capital until 1727.
  • Prithviraj of Amber fought for Rana Sanga at Khanwa (1527).

R11.2 The Mughal alliance: Bharmal, Bhagwant Das, Man Singh I

Raja Bharmal (1547–74), threatened by the Mughal governor of Mewat and by rival kinsmen, met Akbar in 1562 and married his daughter Harkha Bai (later titled Mariam-uz-Zamani, mother of Jahangir) to him. Akbar allowed her to keep her religion. This was the first major Rajput–Mughal marriage alliance.

Bhagwant Das (1574–89): his daughter Man Bai married Prince Salim (1585); she was the mother of Khusrau.

Man Singh I (1589–1614):

  • Commanded the Mughal army at Haldighati (1576) against Pratap.
  • Governor of Kabul, Bihar and Bengal; suppressed the Afghans of Bengal and Orissa; founded Rajmahal (Akbarnagar) in Bengal.
  • Held a mansab of 7000, the highest given to a noble outside the royal family under Akbar.
  • Built the main palace of Amber and the Jagat Shiromani temple; brought the image of Shila Devi from Jessore (Bengal) to Amber.
  • Did not join Akbar's Din-i-Ilahi. (His place among Akbar's "nine jewels" comes from a later popular list.)

Was the alliance submission or partnership? Older nationalist writing condemned it. Historians such as Satish Chandra and G.N. Sharma see a mutual bargain: the Mughals gained loyal generals; the Kachhwahas gained high mansabs, jagirs across the empire, security against rivals and freedom to run their home state (watan jagir).

R11.3 Mirza Raja Jai Singh I (1621–67)

  • Served three emperors; Shah Jahan gave him the title Mirza Raja.
  • Fought in Central Asia (Balkh), Kandahar and the Deccan; sided with Aurangzeb in the war of succession.
  • Sent against Shivaji, he besieged Purandar and forced the Treaty of Purandar (1665): Shivaji gave up 23 forts and agreed to serve the Mughals. Jai Singh persuaded Shivaji to visit Agra (1666), from where Shivaji escaped.
  • The poet Bihari (Bihari Satsai) was at his court.

R11.4 Sawai Jai Singh II (1700–43)

Aurangzeb gave him the title Sawai ("one and a quarter", i.e. above others). He served as Mughal governor of Malwa and Agra.

FieldAchievements
JaipurFounded 18 November 1727; planned by Vidyadhar Bhattacharya on a grid of wide streets and blocks (based on Vastu principles); one of India's earliest planned cities
AstronomyBuilt five Jantar Mantar observatories — Delhi, Jaipur, Ujjain, Varanasi, Mathura (the Mathura one no longer exists); prepared the astronomical tables Zij-i-Muhammad Shahi; had Euclid translated (Rekhaganit, by Jagannath Samrat). The Jaipur Jantar Mantar is a UNESCO World Heritage Site (2010).
ReligionPerformed the Ashvamedha (described as the last in India) and Vajapeya sacrifices
PoliticsCalled the Hurda conference (Hurda, Bhilwara, 17 July 1734) of Rajput rulers to resist the Marathas; it failed because of mutual rivalries. Fought Abhay Singh of Jodhpur at Gangwana (1741).
FortsCompleted Jaigarh (the Jaivana cannon) and Nahargarh

After him. His sons Ishwari Singh and Madho Singh fought for the throne: Ishwari won at Rajmahal (1747) and built the Isarlat (Sargasuli) tower; at Bagru (1748) the Marathas forced a settlement; Ishwari killed himself in 1750 and Madho Singh I became ruler. Inviting the Marathas into this dispute opened Rajasthan to Maratha intervention (R15).

Pratap Singh (1778–1803) built the Hawa Mahal (1799, architect Lalchand); wrote poetry as Brajnidhi; joined Jodhpur at Tunga (1787). Jagat Singh II signed the treaty of 1818. Ram Singh II (1835–80) started modern colleges and public works; the city was painted pink for the Prince of Wales's visit (1876).

R11.5 Shekhawati and Alwar

Shekhawati (Sikar, Jhunjhunu, Churu): Rao Shekha (1433–88), a Kachhwaha of Amarsar, built an independent chiefdom; his descendants, the Shekhawats, ruled many small thikanas. The British raised the Shekhawati Brigade (1835) to suppress raiding; the rebels Dungji and Jawaharji belong to this story (R15). Its merchant families (Marwaris) built the famous painted havelis (Art and Culture notebook).

Alwar: earlier part of Mewat under the Khanzada chiefs (Hasan Khan Mewati fought at Khanwa). Pratap Singh Naruka, of a Kachhwaha branch, broke from Jaipur and founded Alwar state (1775). Bakhtawar Singh signed a treaty with the Company in 1803 — with Bharatpur's (also 1803), the earliest in Rajasthan. Later Jai Singh (1892–1937) promoted Hindi but was deposed by the British (1933) after the Neemuchana firing (1925; R17).

4What historians say

Sources: Abul Fazl's Akbarnama for Bharmal and Man Singh; the Kachhwaha vamshavali and Amber records (the Jaipur state archives at Bikaner are a major collection). Modern works: Jadunath Sarkar, A History of Jaipur; V.S. Bhatnagar, Life and Times of Sawai Jai Singh; A.K. Roy on the founding of Jaipur.

Sarkar praised Sawai Jai Singh as a scientist-statesman but criticised his failure to stop the Marathas; later historians note that his Hurda plan was undermined by the rivalries of the same Rajput rulers he sought to unite.

5Prelims fact bank

  • Dulherai founded Kachhwaha rule in Dhundhar; early capital Dausa.
  • Kakil Dev took Amber from the Meenas.
  • Raja Bharmal allied with Akbar in 1562; his daughter Harkha Bai (Mariam-uz-Zamani) was Jahangir's mother.
  • Man Bai, daughter of Bhagwant Das, was the mother of Khusrau.
  • Man Singh I commanded the Mughal army at Haldighati (1576).
  • Man Singh I held a mansab of 7000 and governed Kabul, Bihar and Bengal.
  • Man Singh I brought the Shila Devi image from Jessore to Amber.
  • Mirza Raja Jai Singh I made the Treaty of Purandar (1665) with Shivaji.
  • Bihari (Bihari Satsai) was at the court of Mirza Raja Jai Singh.
  • Sawai Jai Singh II founded Jaipur on 18 November 1727; architect Vidyadhar Bhattacharya.
  • Sawai Jai Singh built Jantar Mantars at Delhi, Jaipur, Ujjain, Varanasi and Mathura.
  • Zij-i-Muhammad Shahi was prepared under Sawai Jai Singh.
  • The Hurda conference was held on 17 July 1734 (Bhilwara).
  • Isarlat (Sargasuli) was built by Ishwari Singh after Rajmahal (1747).
  • Hawa Mahal was built in 1799 under Pratap Singh; architect Lalchand.
  • Rao Shekha founded Shekhawati; seat Amarsar.
  • Pratap Singh Naruka founded Alwar state (1775).
  • Alwar and Bharatpur signed the earliest treaties with the Company in Rajasthan (1803).

Exam traps

  • Harkha Bai of Amber (Mariam-uz-Zamani) was Akbar's wife and Jahangir's mother; "Jodha Bai" is a later popular name.
  • Mirza Raja Jai Singh I (Purandar, 1665) ≠ Sawai Jai Singh II (Jaipur, 1727).
  • Hurda conference (1734) was against the Marathas, not the Mughals.
  • Jaipur was founded in 1727; the city was painted pink in 1876.
  • Alwar and Bharatpur signed in 1803; most others in 1817–18; Sirohi last, in 1823.
  • Rajmahal (Bengal) was founded by Man Singh; battle of Rajmahal (1747) was near Tonk/Jaipur — different places.
