7Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words Evaluate the achievements of Sawai Jai Singh II.

How to frame it

  • Mughal service
  • Jaipur
  • Astronomy
  • Hurda and Marathas
  • Assessment
Model answer (88 words)

Sawai Jai Singh II (1700–43), Mughal governor of Malwa and Agra, used imperial decline to strengthen Amber. He founded Jaipur (1727), planned by Vidyadhar on a grid — among India's first planned cities. He built five Jantar Mantar observatories, prepared the Zij-i-Muhammad Shahi and sponsored translations of Euclid. He revived Vedic rites, including the Ashvamedha. Politically, his Hurda conference (1734) to unite Rajputs against the Marathas failed, and his sons' succession war invited Maratha intervention. He was a great builder and scholar but could not secure Rajasthan's future.

5 marks · 50 words Why did Raja Bharmal ally with Akbar, and what were the results?

How to frame it

  • Reasons
  • Terms
  • Results
Model answer (44 words)

Threatened by kinsmen and the Mughal governor of Mewat, Bharmal allied with Akbar in 1562, marrying Harkha Bai to him. Amber gained security, high mansabs and autonomy; the Mughals gained loyal generals such as Man Singh. It became the model for Akbar's Rajput policy.

2 marks · 15 words What was the Hurda conference?

How to frame it

  • When, who
  • Aim
Model answer (15 words)

Meeting of Rajput rulers called by Sawai Jai Singh (1734, Bhilwara) to resist the Marathas.

  • Bharmal 1562 → Harkha Bai (Mariam-uz-Zamani). Bhagwant Das → Man Bai (Khusrau's mother).
  • Man Singh I: Haldighati 1576; Kabul, Bihar, Bengal; 7000; Shila Devi; Amber palace.
  • Mirza Raja Jai Singh I: Purandar 1665; Bihari.
  • Sawai Jai Singh II: Jaipur 18 Nov 1727 (Vidyadhar); 5 Jantar Mantars; Zij-i-Muhammad Shahi; Ashvamedha; Hurda 17 July 1734.
  • Ishwari vs Madho: Rajmahal 1747 (Isarlat), Bagru 1748. Hawa Mahal 1799 (Pratap Singh). Treaty 1818.
  • Shekhawati: Rao Shekha (Amarsar). Alwar: Pratap Singh Naruka 1775; treaty 1803.

Unit R12 · c. 1040 – 1947 CE

Hadoti, Karauli, Bharatpur, Dholpur and Tonk

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR3 Political and cultural achievements of prominent rulers of major dynasties
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH1 Prehistoric cultures, ancient sites; political and cultural achievements of rulers (to 18th century)

Topics this unit covers

  • Major dynasties of Rajasthan and their achievements

Zalim Singh Jhala and the creation of Jhalawar, Surjan Hada's treaty with Akbar, Maharaja Suraj Mal of Bharatpur, the 1805 siege of Bharatpur and Amir Khan of Tonk are the usual questions.

2The big picture

South-eastern and eastern Rajasthan had a different mix of rulers:

  • Hadoti — the land of the Hada Chauhans: Bundi (13th century), Kota (separated in 1631) and Jhalawar (carved out of Kota in 1838 for the family of the regent Zalim Singh Jhala).
  • Karauli — the Jadon (Yaduvanshi) Rajputs.
  • Bharatpur and Dholpur — Jat states. Bharatpur rose from peasant revolts against Aurangzeb to a regional power under Suraj Mal, and its fort resisted the British in 1805.
  • Tonk — Rajasthan's only Muslim-ruled princely state, created in 1817 for the Pindari leader Amir Khan.

These states show that 18th-century Rajasthan was shaped by new powers — Jats, Marathas, Pindaris and ambitious ministers — not only by the old Rajput houses.

  1. c. 1040Vijaypal (Jadon) founds Bayana
  2. 1241–42Rao Deva Hada takes Bundi from the Meenas
  3. 1348Arjunpal founds Karauli
  4. 1423Gagron: Achaldas Khichi falls to Hoshang Shah of Malwa
  5. 1569Surjan Hada surrenders Ranthambhor to Akbar
  6. 1631Kota separated from Bundi under Madho Singh
  7. 1669Gokula's Jat revolt near Mathura
  8. 1734Bundi queen invites the Marathas (Holkar)
  9. 1756–63Maharaja Suraj Mal of Bharatpur
  10. 1771–1824Zalim Singh Jhala, regent of Kota
  11. 1805Lord Lake fails to capture Bharatpur
  12. 1817Amir Khan accepts British terms; Tonk state created
  13. 1826British capture Bharatpur
  14. 1838Jhalawar state created

3Core notes

R12.1 Bundi: the Hadas

  • Rao Deva (Hada Chauhan) took Bundi from the Meenas (1241–42); the town is traditionally named after Bunda Meena. Taragarh fort was built in the 14th century (Rao Barsingh).
  • Surjan Hada held Ranthambhor for Mewar; after a siege he surrendered it to Akbar (1569) on favourable terms: Bundi's rulers were spared sending daughters to the Mughal harem (dola), exempted from jizya and from prostration at court, and allowed to keep their temples. These terms are often cited as an example of Akbar's flexible Rajput policy.
  • Rao Ratan Singh served Jahangir (titles Sarbuland Rai, Ram Raj).
  • Chhatrasal Hada died fighting for Dara Shikoh at Samugarh (1658).
  • In the time of Budh Singh, Jaipur's interference led his Kachhwaha queen to call in Malhar Rao Holkar (1734) — usually counted as the first Maratha entry into Rajasthan's politics.
  • Treaty with the Company: 1818.

R12.2 Kota, Zalim Singh Jhala and Jhalawar

Kota was separated from Bundi when Shah Jahan granted it to Madho Singh, a son of Rao Ratan Singh (1631). Mukund Singh, his successor, died fighting for Dara at Dharmat (1658).

Zalim Singh Jhala (1739–1824), regent and chief minister of Kota from 1771:

  • Controlled Kota for half a century while the ruler was a figurehead.
  • Revenue reforms: measured land, fixed cash rents, took over land directly from intermediaries, and promoted cultivation — Kota's revenue rose, though the burden on peasants was heavy.
  • Built a military cantonment (Chhawani) and kept a strong army; handled the Marathas and Pindaris skilfully.
  • Signed the treaty with the Company (December 1817); a supplementary clause (1818) gave the administration of Kota permanently to Zalim Singh and his heirs.

The arrangement caused conflict between the ruler and Zalim's family (battle of Mangrol, 1821). The British settled it by creating a new state, Jhalawar (1838), for Zalim's grandson Madan Singh, with its capital at Jhalrapatan.

Gagron (Jhalawar), a water fort of the Khichi Chauhans: Achaldas Khichi fell to Hoshang Shah of Malwa (1423) after a jauhar; the saint Pipa had earlier been a ruler of Gagron. Rana Sanga defeated Malwa here in 1519 (R08).

R12.3 Karauli and Dholpur

Karauli (Jadon Rajputs, claiming descent from Krishna): Vijaypal founded Bayana (Vijaymandirgarh) c. 1040; his successor Tahanpal built Tahangarh; Arjunpal founded Karauli (Kalyanpuri) in 1348. The goddess Kaila Devi is the family deity. Karauli was the first state to sign a treaty under Lord Hastings (November 1817).

Dholpur: the battle of Bari near Dholpur (1518) was a victory of Rana Sanga over Ibrahim Lodi. The Jat Rana family of Gohad received Dholpur from the British in 1805–06 and ruled until 1949.

R12.4 Bharatpur: the Jat state

Origins in revolt. Jat peasants of the Mathura–Agra region rose against Aurangzeb:

  • Gokula led a revolt in 1669 (battle of Tilpat) and was executed.
  • Rajaram raided Akbar's tomb at Sikandra (1688).
  • Churaman built a power base at Thun.

Badan Singh (d. 1756) turned the movement into a state, was recognised by Sawai Jai Singh, and built palaces at Deeg.

Maharaja Suraj Mal (1756–63):

  • Built the Lohagarh (Iron Fort) of Bharatpur, with mud walls and a moat that absorbed cannon fire, and the Deeg palaces.
  • Expanded over Agra, Mathura, Aligarh and Mewat; captured Agra (1761).
  • Advised the Marathas before Panipat (1761) to fight a defensive war; after their defeat he gave shelter to the fleeing Maratha soldiers.
  • Killed in battle near Delhi (1763) against Najib-ud-Daula.
  • Admired for his statesmanship; often called the "Plato of the Jats".

Jawahar Singh lost to Jaipur at Maonda–Mandholi (1767).

Ranjit Singh (1776–1805) signed a treaty with the Company (1803) but sheltered Jaswant Rao Holkar; Lord Lake besieged Bharatpur in 1805 and failed after four assaults — the Company's most famous setback in India of that time. A treaty followed. In 1826 Lord Combermere captured the fort during a succession dispute.

R12.5 Tonk: Amir Khan

  • Amir Khan, a Pathan military adventurer, led Pindari and mercenary bands that plundered Rajasthan in the early 19th century, serving Holkar and extracting tribute from Rajput states.
  • During the Pindari war he accepted British terms (November 1817): he disbanded his army and was recognised as Nawab of Tonk — the only Muslim-ruled princely state in Rajasthan.
  • Later nawabs patronised Islamic learning; the Arabic and Persian Research Institute and Sunehri Kothi are at Tonk.
  • Tonk joined the Rajasthan union in 1948 (R21).

4What historians say

The Vamsha Bhaskar of Suryamal Mishran (19th-century poet of Bundi) is the main bardic history of the Hadas. Tod's Annals gives a vivid portrait of Zalim Singh, whom Tod knew personally; later historians such as Jagat Narayan balance his administrative ability against his usurpation of power. For the Jats, K.R. Qanungo's History of the Jats and Jadunath Sarkar's Fall of the Mughal Empire are standard; Qanungo stressed the peasant roots of the Jat state.

5Prelims fact bank

  • Rao Deva Hada took Bundi from the Meenas (1241–42).
  • Surjan Hada surrendered Ranthambhor to Akbar in 1569 on favourable terms.
  • Chhatrasal Hada of Bundi died at Samugarh (1658) fighting for Dara.
  • The Marathas (Malhar Rao Holkar) were first invited into Rajasthan by Bundi in 1734.
  • Kota was separated from Bundi in 1631 under Madho Singh.
  • Zalim Singh Jhala was regent of Kota from 1771 to 1824.
  • Jhalawar was created in 1838 for Madan Singh, grandson of Zalim Singh.
  • Gagron (Jhalawar) is a water fort; Achaldas Khichi fell to Hoshang Shah in 1423.
  • Vijaypal founded Bayana; Arjunpal founded Karauli (1348).
  • Kaila Devi is the deity of the Karauli rulers.
  • Karauli signed the first treaty under Lord Hastings (November 1817).
  • Gokula led the Jat revolt of 1669 (Tilpat).
  • Badan Singh founded the Bharatpur state and built palaces at Deeg.
  • Suraj Mal built Lohagarh fort and captured Agra (1761).
  • Lord Lake failed to capture Bharatpur in 1805.
  • Bharatpur fort was captured by Lord Combermere in 1826.
  • Amir Khan became the Nawab of Tonk in 1817.
  • Tonk was the only Muslim-ruled princely state of Rajasthan.

Exam traps

  • Kota separated from Bundi in 1631; Jhalawar separated from Kota in 1838.
  • Zalim Singh Jhala never became the ruler of Kota; his grandson became ruler of Jhalawar.
  • Lake failed at Bharatpur in 1805; the British took it only in 1826.
  • Bari (1518, near Dholpur): Sanga vs Ibrahim Lodi; Khatoli (1517) was the first clash.
  • Bharatpur and Dholpur were Jat states; Karauli was Jadon Rajput.
  • Tonk was founded by a Pindari leader, Amir Khan — not by the Marathas.

7Mains answer writing

5 marks · 50 words Assess the role of Zalim Singh Jhala in the history of Kota.

How to frame it

  • Position
  • Reforms
  • British treaty
  • Jhalawar
Model answer (51 words)

Zalim Singh Jhala, Kota's regent from 1771, ran the state for fifty years. He measured land, fixed cash rents and raised revenue, built a strong army and handled Marathas and Pindaris. His 1817–18 treaty made the regency hereditary; the resulting conflict led to the creation of Jhalawar (1838) for his grandson.

5 marks · 50 words Evaluate Maharaja Suraj Mal's contribution to the Jat state of Bharatpur.

How to frame it

  • Building
  • Expansion
  • Panipat
  • Assessment
Model answer (46 words)

Suraj Mal (1756–63) built Lohagarh and the Deeg palaces, expanded the Jat state over Agra, Mathura and Mewat, and captured Agra (1761). He advised the Marathas against open battle at Panipat and sheltered them after defeat. He turned a peasant movement into a strong regional state.

2 marks · 15 words Why is the 1805 siege of Bharatpur remembered?

How to frame it

  • What
  • Result
Model answer (12 words)

Lord Lake's four assaults on Lohagarh failed — a rare Company defeat.

8One-screen revision card

  • Bundi: Rao Deva 1241–42; Surjan Hada — Ranthambhor 1569 (dola, jizya exemptions); Chhatrasal — Samugarh; 1734 Holkar invited.
  • Kota: 1631 Madho Singh; Zalim Singh Jhala 1771–1824 (revenue, army, hereditary regency 1818); Mangrol 1821; Jhalawar 1838.
  • Gagron: Khichis, Achaldas 1423, Pipa.
  • Karauli: Vijaypal (Bayana), Arjunpal 1348, Kaila Devi; treaty Nov 1817 (first under Hastings).
  • Bharatpur: Gokula 1669 → Rajaram → Churaman → Badan Singh (Deeg) → Suraj Mal (Lohagarh, Agra 1761, Panipat) → Lake 1805 fails → 1826 Combermere.
  • Dholpur: Bari 1518; Gohad Jat Ranas.
  • Tonk: Amir Khan, 1817; only Muslim state.

  1. Who founded Hada rule at Bundi?

    Answer

    Answer

    Exemption from dola (sending daughters to the harem) and from jizya; also no prostration and freedom to keep temples.

  3. When and for whom was Kota separated from Bundi?

    Answer

    1631, for Madho Singh.

  4. Why was Jhalawar created?

    Answer

    To end the conflict between the Kota ruler and the family of the regent Zalim Singh Jhala (1838).

  5. Which ruler invited the Marathas into Rajasthan in 1734?

    Answer

    The Kachhwaha queen of Budh Singh of Bundi (calling Malhar Rao Holkar).

  6. Who founded Karauli?

    Answer

    Arjunpal (1348).

  7. Who led the Jat revolt of 1669?

    Answer

    Gokula.

  8. Who built the Lohagarh fort?

    Answer

    Maharaja Suraj Mal of Bharatpur.

  9. Which British commander failed to take Bharatpur in 1805?

    Answer

    Lord Lake.

  10. Who founded Tonk state?

    Answer

    Amir Khan (1817).

Unit R13 · 1192 – 1761 CE

Cooperation and Resistance: Rajasthan and the Central Powers

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR4 Cooperation and resistance with central power; administrative and revenue system in medieval Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH1 Prehistoric cultures, ancient sites; political and cultural achievements of rulers (to 18th century)

Topics this unit covers

  • Rajput–Sultanate and Rajput–Mughal relations
  • Important battles

A synthesis unit. Akbar's Rajput policy, the effects of Rajput–Mughal relations and Aurangzeb's Rajput policy are classic 10-mark questions; battles and sieges are asked in Prelims.

2The big picture

Units R06–R12 told the story dynasty by dynasty. This unit pulls it together around one question: how did the Rajput states deal with the powers of Delhi and Agra?

  1. The Sultanate (1192–1526): mostly resistance. The Sultans raided and besieged forts but could rarely hold Rajasthan; Rajput states recovered after each wave.
  2. The early Mughals (1526–1556): Rana Sanga's bid for supremacy failed at Khanwa; Sher Shah's victory at Giri-Sumel showed the Rajputs could not stop a strong central army.
  3. Akbar to Shah Jahan (1556–1658): mostly cooperation. Through marriage alliances, high mansabs and home autonomy, Akbar made most Rajput rulers partners; Mewar alone held out until 1615.
  4. Aurangzeb and after (1658–1761): the partnership broke over the Marwar succession (1679); after 1707 Rajput rulers acted independently — and then faced the Marathas (R15).

Mains answers on this theme are judged on analysis: why each policy was adopted, what each side gained, and what it cost. Use the tables below as answer frames.

  1. 1192Second battle of Tarain
  2. c. 1227Jaitra Singh resists Iltutmish (Bhutala)
  3. 1301–1311Alauddin takes Ranthambhor, Chittor, Siwana, Jalore
  4. 1527Khanwa: Babur defeats Sanga
  5. 1544Giri-Sumel; Sher Shah takes Chittor without a fight
  6. 1562Bharmal of Amber allies with Akbar
  7. 1564Akbar abolishes jizya
  8. 1568Akbar captures Chittor (Jaimal and Patta); third saka
  9. 1569Ranthambhor surrendered by Surjan Hada
  10. 1570Nagaur darbar: Bikaner and Jaisalmer submit
  11. 1576Haldighati
  12. 1615Mewar–Mughal treaty (Amar Singh and Jahangir)
  13. 1679Aurangzeb annexes Marwar; reimposes jizya
  14. 1679–1707Rathore war; Mewar joins (1679–81)
  15. 1734Hurda conference against the Marathas

3Core notes

R13.1 Rajasthan and the Delhi Sultanate

SultanRajasthan campaignsOutcome
Muhammad Ghori / AibakTarain (1192); Ajmer; Hariraja's revolt crushedChauhan power ended; Ajmer under Turkish control
IltutmishRanthambhor (1226), Mandore, Nagda (Bhutala)Raids; Mewar held out
Jalaluddin KhaljiJhain; Ranthambhor (1291)Gave up the siege
Alauddin KhaljiRanthambhor (1301), Chittor (1303), Siwana (1308), Jalore (1311), JaisalmerAll major forts taken; but control was short-lived — Chittor recovered by c. 1326
TughlaqsClashes with Mewar (Hammir) and Jaisalmer (Duda)No lasting control
Malwa and Gujarat SultansWars with Kumbha and SangaMewar dominant (Sarangpur 1437, Gagron 1519)
LodisKhatoli (1517), Bari (1518)Sanga victorious

Why the Sultanate could not hold Rajasthan: hard terrain and strong hill forts; Sultans' priorities elsewhere (Mongols, the Deccan); Rajput clan networks that re-formed after defeat; and the high cost of garrisons.

Why the Rajputs lost battles: Turkish mounted archers and unified command; Rajput reliance on elephants and massed charges; clan rivalries that prevented joint action; and a defensive, fort-centred strategy.

R13.2 Babur and Sher Shah

  • Khanwa (17 March 1527): Rana Sanga led a confederacy of Rajput rulers and Afghans (Hasan Khan Mewati, Mahmud Lodi). Babur's artillery, tulughma flanking tactics and wagon defences won. It ended the chance of a Rajput-led north Indian empire (R08).
  • Giri-Sumel (1544): Sher Shah defeated Maldeo, then took Chittor without a fight (R09).
  • Bahadur Shah of Gujarat sacked Chittor (1535) — the second saka, with Rani Karnavati's jauhar.

R13.3 Akbar's Rajput policy

Akbar combined force against those who resisted with generous terms for those who submitted.

ElementDetails
Marriage alliancesAmber (1562), Bikaner and Jaisalmer (1570), Marwar (1586, Jagat Gosain to Salim); Rajput wives kept their religion; not forced on all (Bundi exempted)
High mansabsMan Singh 7000; Rajput rulers as governors and generals across the empire
Watan jagirRulers kept their home state as a hereditary jagir, with internal autonomy
Succession (tika)The emperor confirmed each new ruler — giving the Mughals leverage in disputes
Religious policyPilgrim tax (1563) and jizya (1564) abolished
ForceChittor (1568: Jaimal and Patta; about 30,000 killed; Akbar's Fatehnama), Ranthambhor (1569); war against Pratap (Haldighati, 1576)
Nagaur darbar (1570)Submission of Bikaner (Kalyanmal) and Jaisalmer (Har Rai); Chandrasen walked out

Why it worked: Rajput rulers gained security from rivals, wealth from distant jagirs and status; the Mughals gained a loyal military class that was not Central Asian or Afghan, and safe routes to Gujarat's ports.

Older nationalist writers saw the alliance as surrender and Pratap as the only patriot. Satish Chandra, G.N. Sharma and others see a political partnership in which both sides gained; they also note that Mewar's resistance was about autonomy and honour, not a "Hindu versus Muslim" contest — Pratap had Hakim Khan Sur on his side and Man Singh led the Mughals.

R13.4 Jahangir to Aurangzeb

  • Jahangir: ended the Mewar war through the treaty of 1615 — Amar Singh was not required to attend court in person; his son Karan attended; Chittor returned but not to be refortified (R08).
  • Shah Jahan: Rajputs remained central (Jai Singh, Jaswant Singh), though fewer held the very highest mansabs.
  • Aurangzeb:
    • On Jaswant Singh's death (1678) he took Marwar under direct rule and tried to control the infant Ajit Singh.
    • Reimposed jizya (1679).
    • Mewar's Raj Singh joined the Rathores; Prince Akbar rebelled (1681) with Rajput support.
    • Peace with Mewar (1681), but the Rathore war lasted until 1707.

Was Aurangzeb's Rajput policy religious? Jadunath Sarkar stressed religious bigotry. Satish Chandra argues it was mainly a political miscalculation over the Marwar succession, while the jizya added to Rajput alienation. Both agree that the breakdown cost the empire its most reliable allies.

After 1707: Ajit Singh and Sawai Jai Singh played kingmaker in Delhi (the Sayyid brothers), took governorships, and expanded their own states. Rajput rulers tried to unite against the Marathas at Hurda (1734) but failed.

R13.5 Effects of Rajput–Mughal relations

AreaEffects
PoliticalPeace inside Rajasthan for about a century; rulers' power grew over their own nobles (backed by Mughal recognition); loss of independent foreign policy
AdministrativeMughal offices (diwan, bakhshi), revenue methods (zabti, measurement) and records adopted in Rajput states (R14)
EconomicIncome from jagirs across the empire flowed home; safe trade routes; Marwari merchants spread across India
CulturalMughal influence on Rajput painting (naturalism, portraiture), architecture (Amber palace, gardens), dress and court ceremony; Rajput influence at the Mughal court (music, customs)
SocialPersian and Rajasthani literary exchange; a composite court culture; but also the decline of an independent Rajput military tradition

4What historians say

Tod romanticised Rajput resistance and condemned the alliances; nationalist historians followed him. G.N. Sharma (Mewar and the Mughal Emperors), Satish Chandra (Medieval India; Parties and Politics at the Mughal Court), M. Athar Ali (The Mughal Nobility under Aurangzeb) and Norman Ziegler placed the relationship within Mughal state-building and Rajput clan politics. Ziegler showed how Rajput ideas of service and loyalty adapted to Mughal service.

5Prelims fact bank

  • Alauddin Khalji took Ranthambhor (1301), Chittor (1303), Siwana (1308) and Jalore (1311).
  • Khanwa was fought on 17 March 1527.
  • Bahadur Shah of Gujarat sacked Chittor in 1535 (second saka; Rani Karnavati's jauhar).
  • Sher Shah took Chittor without a fight in 1544.
  • Akbar abolished the pilgrim tax (1563) and jizya (1564).
  • Akbar captured Chittor in February 1568; Jaimal and Patta led the defence (third saka).
  • Akbar issued a Fatehnama after taking Chittor.
  • Bikaner and Jaisalmer submitted at the Nagaur darbar (1570).
  • The Mewar–Mughal treaty was concluded in 1615 between Amar Singh I and Jahangir.
  • Aurangzeb reimposed jizya in 1679.
  • Prince Akbar rebelled in 1681 with Rajput support.
  • The Hurda conference (1734) aimed to unite Rajputs against the Marathas.

Exam traps

  • Chittor's three sakas: 1303 (Alauddin), 1535 (Bahadur Shah), 1568 (Akbar).
  • Sher Shah took Chittor in 1544 without a siege — it is not counted as a saka.
  • Akbar abolished jizya in 1564; Aurangzeb reimposed it in 1679.
  • The 1615 treaty exempted the Rana from attending court in person; his son Karan attended.
  • Hurda (1734) was against the Marathas, not the Mughals.

6Previous-year questions (RAS)

No verified RAS Mains question specific to this synthesis found yet in the RajRAS list. Related verified PYQs: Maldeo–Humayun–Sher Shah (2013, R09). The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

7Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words Analyse the main features of Akbar's Rajput policy and its results.

How to frame it

  • Aims
  • Features: marriages, mansabs, watan jagir, tolerance, force
  • Results for both sides
  • Mewar exception
Model answer (82 words)

Akbar sought a loyal non-Central Asian nobility and secure routes to Gujarat. He made marriage alliances (Amber 1562, Bikaner, Jaisalmer, Marwar) without forcing conversion, gave high mansabs (Man Singh 7000), left rulers their home states as watan jagirs, and abolished jizya. Against resistance he used force — Chittor (1568), Ranthambhor (1569), Haldighati (1576). Rajput rulers gained security, wealth and status; the empire gained generals and stability. Only Mewar held out until 1615. The policy created a century-long partnership, broken only under Aurangzeb.

10 marks · 100 words Discuss the effects of Rajput–Mughal relations on Rajasthan.

How to frame it

  • Political
  • Administrative
  • Economic
  • Cultural
  • Conclusion
Model answer (83 words)

Politically, Mughal alliance brought peace and strengthened rulers over their nobles, at the cost of independent foreign policy. Administratively, Rajput states adopted Mughal offices — diwan, bakhshi — and revenue methods such as zabti and land measurement. Economically, income from distant jagirs flowed home, routes became safe, and Marwari merchants spread across India. Culturally, Rajput painting absorbed Mughal naturalism, palaces such as Amber borrowed Mughal forms, and court culture became composite. The relationship thus reshaped Rajasthan's polity and culture far beyond the battlefield.

5 marks · 50 words Why did Aurangzeb's relations with the Rajputs break down?

How to frame it

  • Marwar succession
  • Jizya
  • Mewar and Prince Akbar
  • Result
Model answer (48 words)

After Jaswant Singh's death (1678), Aurangzeb annexed Marwar and sought to control the infant Ajit Singh. The reimposition of jizya (1679) deepened alienation. Mewar joined the Rathores, and Prince Akbar rebelled with Rajput support (1681). The empire lost its most reliable allies in a war lasting until 1707.

8One-screen revision card

  • Sultanate: raids and sieges (Alauddin 1301–11), no lasting control; Rajputs lost battles to cavalry, unity, tactics.
  • Khanwa 1527 (Sanga); Chittor sakas 1303, 1535, 1568; Giri-Sumel 1544.
  • Akbar: marriages, mansabs, watan jagir, tika, no jizya (1564), force (Chittor 1568, Ranthambhor 1569, Haldighati 1576), Nagaur 1570.
  • Jahangir: Mewar treaty 1615. Aurangzeb: Marwar 1679, jizya 1679, Prince Akbar 1681, Rathore war to 1707.
  • Effects: political peace, Mughal administration, economic flows, composite culture.

9Self-test

  1. Name the four forts Alauddin Khalji took in Rajasthan between 1301 and 1311.

    Answer

    Ranthambhor, Chittor, Siwana, Jalore.

  2. Give two reasons why the Sultanate could not hold Rajasthan for long.

    Answer

    Difficult terrain and strong forts; priorities elsewhere; resilient clan networks; costly garrisons.

  3. What were the three sakas of Chittor?

    Answer

    1303 (Alauddin), 1535 (Bahadur Shah), 1568 (Akbar).

  4. What is a watan jagir?

    Answer

    A Rajput ruler's home state, held as a hereditary jagir with internal autonomy.

  5. Who led the defence of Chittor in 1568?

    Answer

    Jaimal Rathore and Patta Sisodia.

  6. Which rulers submitted at the Nagaur darbar?

    Answer

    Kalyanmal of Bikaner and Har Rai of Jaisalmer.

  7. What were the main terms of the 1615 treaty?

    Answer

    The Rana need not attend court; his heir would; Chittor returned but not refortified.

  8. What triggered the breakdown under Aurangzeb?

    Answer

    The annexation of Marwar after Jaswant Singh's death (1678–79) and the reimposition of jizya.

  9. How did Satish Chandra interpret Aurangzeb's Rajput policy?

    Answer

    Mainly as a political miscalculation over the Marwar succession, not primarily religious.

Unit R14 · c. 1500 – 1818 CE

Administrative and Revenue System of Medieval Rajasthan

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR4 Cooperation and resistance with central power; administrative and revenue system in medieval Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH2 Revenue and administrative system and changing patterns

Topics this unit covers

  • Administrative and revenue system of the Rajput states
  • Jagirdari system; terms of land revenue

Tier A. Asked every recent year: RAS Mains 2024 (revenue system, 10 marks; "Hath ka Kurab", 2 marks) and 2023 ("Dyodhidar", 2 marks). Learn the technical terms precisely.

2The big picture

Medieval Rajput states were run on two principles:

  1. The clan as brotherhood. The ruler was the head of a clan; his kinsmen shared the land as jagirdars (also called samants or thakurs) in return for military service. This is the jagirdari (or samanti) system.
  2. Mughal-style administration. From the late 16th century, Rajput rulers serving the Mughals copied Mughal offices (diwan, bakhshi), revenue methods (measurement, cash rates) and record-keeping, and used Mughal backing to make their nobles more dependent on them.

Land revenue — the state's share of the crop — was the main income, collected by sharing the crop, estimating it, or fixing cash rates, plus many extra cesses.

Mains questions here test vocabulary: rekh, chakri, khalsa, bhom, sasan, batai, kankut, lag-baag, tazim, hath ka kurab, dyodhidar. Every term in the fact bank is a possible 2-mark question.

  1. Before c. 1570Clan-based "brotherhood" polity; land shared among kinsmen
  2. 1570s onwardsRajput rulers enter Mughal service; Mughal offices and methods adopted
  3. 17th centuryMuhnot Nainsi compiles Marwar ra Pargana ri Vigat (revenue and village data)
  4. 18th centuryIjara (revenue farming) spreads; heavier cesses; Maratha tribute
  5. 1818 onwardsBritish paramountcy; rulers reduce nobles' powers further (R15)

3Core notes

R14.1 How a Rajput state worked: from brotherhood to state

To understand the terms in this unit, start with the idea behind them. A Rajput state grew out of a clan. When a Rathore, Kachhwaha or Guhil chief conquered a region, he shared it with his brothers, sons and cousins, who settled in villages and forts across it. They saw themselves less as his servants than as his bhai-bandh (brothers), co-owners of the land their ancestors had won. The chief was the head of the family, first among equals, and his kinsmen owed him loyalty and armed support in war.

The clan "brotherhood" and its limits. In this early system, a kinsman's land was almost his by right: the ruler could not easily take it back, and a noble who felt slighted could leave and serve another ruler, or rebel. Succession disputes drew in the nobles, who often decided which prince became ruler. This made the state strong in war, since every noble had a stake in defending the land, but weak in peace, since the ruler depended on his nobles' goodwill. The central story of Rajput administration from the 16th century is the ruler's effort to turn these brothers into servants, whose land was a grant from him for service and could be withdrawn.

The Mughal effect. When Rajput rulers entered Mughal service from the 1570s, they became mansabdars, and the emperor recognised their home states as watan jagirs, hereditary lands that would not be transferred. This gave the ruler a powerful new backer: the emperor recognised his succession, and Mughal rank and pay made him richer than any of his nobles. Rulers then copied the Mughal system at home. They appointed a diwan and bakhshi, measured land and fixed cash rates, kept detailed revenue records, and assessed each noble's jagir at a recorded income (rekh) that fixed the service (chakri) he owed. Jagirs became grants that the ruler could transfer, resume or confiscate. The terms in the next sections, from rekh to tagir, are the tools of this centralisation.

Was it "feudal"? James Tod, writing in the 1820s, compared Rajput states to medieval European feudalism: land held from a lord in return for military service, ranked nobles, succession fees and court honours. The likeness is real, and it is why the word stuck. Later historians point to three differences. First, the bond was one of kinship, not a contract between strangers: most great jagirdars were the ruler's own clan. Second, from the 17th century the ruler could transfer and resume jagirs, which European lords could rarely do to their vassals. Third, the system was shaped by the Mughal mansab, a salaried imperial service rather than a feudal one. A balanced answer says the system had feudal features but was a clan-based polity moving, under Mughal influence, towards a centralised state.

R14.2 The ruler and central administration

The ruler was the source of all authority, but in theory he ruled as the servant of the clan deity — in Mewar the Rana called himself the diwan of Eklingji.

OfficeFunction
Pradhan (Mewar: also Musahib)Chief minister; usually a leading noble
DiwanFinance and revenue; often the most powerful official after Mughal contact
BakhshiArmy: recruitment, pay, muster of jagirdars' contingents
KhansamaRoyal household and stores
DyodhidarOfficer in charge of the dyodhi (palace gateway): controlled access to the ruler and the inner palace, and managed palace security and household business
KotwalPolice and order in the capital
HakimHead of a pargana (district): revenue, justice and order
FaujdarMilitary officer of a region
Kanungo, Patwari, Chaudhari, PatelRevenue records and village-level collection

Units: state → pargana (under a hakim) → village (patel, patwari, and a village panchayat for local disputes).

Many of these offices came from the Mughal model, and their spread shows the ruler's growing control. The diwan, often from a literate service caste rather than from the ruling clan, owed his position to the ruler alone, and so was a counterweight to the great nobles. The dyodhidar (RAS Mains 2023) mattered because access to the ruler was itself a form of power: whoever controlled the gateway controlled who could petition, plead or plot.

R14.3 The jagirdari system

Land categories:

TermMeaning
KhalsaCrown land, administered directly by the ruler's officials
Jagir (patta)Land assigned to a noble in return for service
BhomHereditary land held by bhomias in return for local duties such as guarding villages and roads; hard to take back
Sasan / MuafiRevenue-free grants to Brahmins, Charans and religious persons; grants to temples were called doli

Obligations of a jagirdar:

  • Rekh: the assessed annual income of a jagir, recorded by the state; dues were calculated on it.
  • Chakri: military service — a set number of horsemen in proportion to the rekh.
  • Succession fee on inheriting the jagir — called talwar bandhai (sword-tying) in Mewar and hukmnama in Marwar; plus nazrana (gifts) on occasions.
  • Penalties: kaid (temporary resumption to khalsa) or tagir (confiscation).

Ranks of nobles:

  • Mewar: the Solah (16 first-class nobles), the Battis (32 second-class), and the Gol (lesser nobles).
  • Marwar: Rajvi (the ruler's close kin, for three generations free of rekh and chakri), then the sardars, muddi and ganayat nobles.
  • Jaipur: the Barah Kotri — twelve branches of Kachhwaha nobles (traditionally organised by Prithviraj of Amber).

Court honours marked a noble's rank:

  • Tazim: the ruler rose to receive the noble — single (ikevadi) or double (dohri) tazim.
  • Hath ka kurab (Marwar): the noble touched the ruler's knee or garment, and the ruler placed his hand on the noble's shoulder and drew it to his own chest — one of the highest honours.
  • Banh pasav: the ruler taking the noble's arm — a lesser honour.

Why honours mattered. Honours such as tazim and hath ka kurab (RAS Mains 2024) may look like ceremony, but they were a tool of rule. A ruler who could not yet take land from a powerful noble could still rank him publicly: who stood where at court, whom the ruler rose to greet, whom he embraced. Because a noble's standing among his clan and peers depended on these signs, the right to grant or withhold them gave the ruler leverage. Rank by the ruler's favour slowly replaced rank by birth alone, the same shift the rekh and chakri system made in land.

R14.4 Land revenue: methods of assessment

The state's share was usually one-quarter to one-half of the produce, depending on the crop, irrigation and the cultivator's status.

MethodHow it worked
Batai (crop-sharing)The crop itself was divided: khet batai (standing crop divided in the field), lank batai (harvested sheaves divided before threshing), raas batai (grain heap divided after threshing)
KankutOfficials estimated (kut) the yield of the standing crop (kan) and fixed the share in advance
ZabtiFixed cash rate per bigha, by crop — mainly for cash crops (cotton, sugarcane, indigo, tobacco); adopted from Mughal practice
MukataA lump sum, in cash or kind, fixed for a field or a whole village
IjaraRevenue farming: the right to collect revenue sold to the highest bidder; spread in the 18th century and led to oppression

Land by irrigation: barani (rain-fed), chahi (well-irrigated), talabi (tank-irrigated); irrigated land paid more.

Cultivators: in Marwar, bapidar peasants had hereditary occupancy rights; gair-bapidar peasants were tenants-at-will who could be evicted.

Why so many methods? Each method shared risk differently between the state and the peasant. Under batai the state took a share of whatever grew, so in a bad year both lost; it suited rain-fed land where harvests swung widely, but it needed officials at every field or threshing floor. Kankut saved that labour by estimating the crop in advance, but estimates could be inflated. Zabti fixed a cash rate per bigha, which gave the state a predictable income and suited valuable cash crops on irrigated land, but put the whole risk of a bad year on the peasant. Mukata and ijara went further, handing a fixed sum to a village or a revenue farmer; the ijaradar, who had paid in advance, squeezed peasants to recover his money. That is why the spread of ijara in the 18th century, when rulers needed cash for Maratha tribute and wars, made the peasants' burden much heavier.

R14.5 Cesses, taxes and the burden on peasants

On top of land revenue, the state and jagirdars collected numerous cesses called lag-baag (lag = cess, baag = levy). Examples:

  • House tax (ghar ginti) and plough tax (hal).
  • Levies on festivals, marriages and visits of the ruler or jagirdar.
  • Begar: forced unpaid labour for the jagirdar or state.
  • Trade taxes: rahdari (transit duty on goods passing through), mapa (tax on goods sold in markets), dan (customs).

In jagir areas the jagirdar collected both revenue and cesses, often with little check. The multiplication of lag-baag and begar in the 19th and early 20th centuries became the main grievance of the peasant movements (Bijolia, Begun, Shekhawati — R17).

Sources: Muhnot Nainsi's Marwar ra Pargana ri Vigat; arhsatta (crop and revenue registers); sanad parwana bahi (Marwar orders); Jaipur's arzdashts and revenue records. These records, unusual in their detail, are why historians such as Dilbagh Singh and B.L. Bhadani can reconstruct village life in Rajasthan more fully than in many other regions.

What came after. After the 1818 treaties, British protection let rulers and jagirdars raise demands without fear of revolt, and cesses and begar multiplied. Rulers also used British support to reduce the nobles' powers further (R15). The peasant movements of the 20th century (R17) were aimed at exactly the system described here, and after integration the Rajasthan Land Reforms and Resumption of Jagirs Act (1952) and the Tenancy Act (1955) abolished it (R23).

How this unit connects.

  • Mughal models: mansab and jagir, zabt, the diwan and bakhshi (I23); the Rajputs and the Mughals (R13).
  • Dynasties: Mewar (R07, R08), Marwar (R09), Amber–Jaipur (R11) and the other states (R10, R12).
  • Sources: Nainsi's Vigat and Khyat, bahis and arhsattas (R01).
  • After: British paramountcy and the nobles (R15); peasant movements against lag-baag and begar (R17); changes in administration and revenue, and the end of jagirdari (R23).
  • India: Sultanate iqta (I18) and Mughal revenue (I23) for comparison.

4What historians say

G.D. Sharma (Rajput Polity) traced the shift from a clan "brotherhood" to a more centralised state as rulers, backed by Mughal recognition, gained control over succession and jagir assignments. Dilbagh Singh (The State, Landlords and Peasants: Rajasthan in the 18th Century) used Jaipur revenue records to show village structure, revenue rates and the spread of ijara. B.L. Bhadani (Peasants, Artisans and Entrepreneurs) did the same for Marwar using Nainsi's Vigat.

Historians debate how "feudal" the system was. Tod called it feudal, comparing it to medieval Europe; later historians point to differences — the clan bond, the ruler's right to transfer jagirs, and the influence of the Mughal mansab system.

5Prelims fact bank

  • Khalsa = crown land; jagir = land assigned for service.
  • Bhom land was held hereditarily by bhomias for local guard duties.
  • Sasan (muafi) = revenue-free grants to Brahmins and Charans; doli = grants to temples.
  • Rekh was the assessed income of a jagir, on which dues were calculated.
  • Chakri was the military service owed by a jagirdar.
  • The succession fee was talwar bandhai in Mewar and hukmnama in Marwar.
  • Kaid = temporary resumption of a jagir; tagir = confiscation.
  • Mewar nobles: Solah (16), Battis (32) and Gol.
  • Marwar: Rajvi nobles were close kin, free of rekh and chakri for three generations.
  • Jaipur nobles were organised as the Barah Kotri.
  • Tazim: the ruler rose to receive a noble (ikevadi or dohri).
  • Hath ka kurab: the ruler placed his hand on a noble's shoulder and drew it to his own chest (Marwar).
  • The Dyodhidar was in charge of the palace gateway and access to the ruler.
  • Khet, lank and raas are the three types of batai.
  • Kankut = estimation of the standing crop.
  • Zabti = cash rate per bigha, mainly for cash crops.
  • Ijara = revenue farming to the highest bidder.
  • Barani = rain-fed; chahi = well-irrigated; talabi = tank-irrigated.
  • Bapidar peasants had hereditary rights; gair-bapidar were tenants-at-will (Marwar).
  • Lag-baag = cesses; begar = forced labour; rahdari = transit duty.
  • Muhnot Nainsi wrote Marwar ra Pargana ri Vigat.

Exam traps

  • Rekh is an assessment of income, not a tax rate; chakri is service, not money.
  • Bhom (hereditary, for local service) ≠ jagir (resumable, for military service).
  • Lank batai is before threshing; raas batai is after threshing.
  • Kankut estimates the standing crop; batai divides the actual crop.
  • Talwar bandhai (Mewar) and hukmnama (Marwar) are the same kind of succession fee.
  • Hath ka kurab is an honour, not a tax.

RAS Mains 2024Describe the revenue system of medieval Rajasthan. (paraphrased)10

Listed by RajRAS; wording paraphrased. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

7Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words How did contact with the Mughals change administration in the Rajput states?

How to frame it

  • Before: clan brotherhood
  • Watan jagir and imperial backing
  • Mughal offices and records
  • Rekh–chakri and transferable jagirs
  • Limits
Model answer (123 words)

Before the Mughals, a Rajput state was a clan "brotherhood": kinsmen held land almost by right and often decided succession. Mughal service from the 1570s changed this. The emperor recognised the ruler's home state as a watan jagir and backed his succession, while imperial rank and pay made him richer than his nobles. Rulers copied Mughal institutions: the diwan and bakhshi, land measurement, zabti cash rates and detailed revenue records. Each jagir was assessed at a rekh that fixed the chakri owed, and jagirs became grants the ruler could transfer, resume (kaid) or confiscate (tagir). Yet clan loyalties survived, and when Mughal power declined in the 18th century, nobles again grew powerful. Mughal contact thus moved Rajput states towards centralisation without completing it.

10 marks · 100 words · RAS Mains 2024 Describe the revenue system of medieval Rajasthan.

How to frame it

  • Land categories
  • State share
  • Methods: batai, kankut, zabti, mukata, ijara
  • Cesses
  • Assessment
Model answer (85 words)

Land was khalsa (crown), jagir (for service), bhom (hereditary) or sasan (revenue-free). The state took one-quarter to one-half of the produce. Methods included batai — khet, lank or raas — kankut (estimating the standing crop), zabti (cash rates per bigha for cash crops, from Mughal practice), mukata (fixed lump sums) and, in the 18th century, ijara (revenue farming). Irrigated land (chahi, talabi) paid more than barani. Patwaris, kanungos and hakims kept records. Numerous cesses (lag-baag) and begar added to the burden, later fuelling peasant movements.

5 marks · 50 words Explain the main features of the jagirdari system in Rajasthan.

How to frame it

  • Basis
  • Obligations
  • Ranks
  • Control
Model answer (52 words)

Jagirdars, mostly the ruler's kinsmen, held land for service. Their jagir's assessed income (rekh) fixed their military service (chakri) and dues; heirs paid a succession fee (talwar bandhai or hukmnama). Ranks such as Mewar's Solah and Battis, and honours such as tazim, marked status. Rulers could resume (kaid) or confiscate (tagir) jagirs.

2 marks · 15 words · RAS Mains 2024 What was "Hath ka Kurab"?

How to frame it

  • Meaning
Model answer (15 words)

A high Marwar court honour: the ruler touched a noble's shoulder, then his own chest.

2 marks · 15 words · RAS Mains 2023 Who were the Dyodhidars?

How to frame it

  • Meaning
Model answer (13 words)

Officers controlling the palace gateway (dyodhi), access to the ruler and palace security.

8One-screen revision card

  • Offices: pradhan, diwan, bakhshi, khansama, dyodhidar, kotwal; pargana — hakim; village — patel, patwari.
  • Land: khalsa, jagir (patta), bhom, sasan/doli.
  • Jagirdar: rekh (assessed income), chakri (service), talwar bandhai/hukmnama (succession), kaid/tagir.
  • Ranks: Mewar Solah–Battis–Gol; Marwar Rajvi; Jaipur Barah Kotri.
  • Honours: tazim (ikevadi, dohri), hath ka kurab, banh pasav.
  • Revenue: ¼–½; batai (khet, lank, raas), kankut, zabti, mukata, ijara.
  • Land: barani, chahi, talabi. Peasants: bapidar vs gair-bapidar.
  • Cesses: lag-baag, begar, rahdari, mapa.

9Self-test

  1. What is khalsa land?

    Answer

    Crown land administered directly by the ruler's officials.

  2. What was rekh?

    Answer

    The assessed annual income of a jagir, used to calculate dues and service.

  3. What was talwar bandhai?

    Answer

    The succession fee paid in Mewar when an heir inherited a jagir.

  4. Who were the Solah and Battis?

    Answer

    The 16 first-class and 32 second-class nobles of Mewar.

  5. What was the Barah Kotri?

    Answer

    The twelve branches of Kachhwaha nobles of Jaipur.

  6. Distinguish lank batai and raas batai.

    Answer

    Lank: sheaves divided before threshing. Raas: grain divided after threshing.

  7. What was kankut?

    Answer

    Estimation of the yield of the standing crop to fix the state's share.

  8. What was ijara?

    Answer

    Revenue farming: collection rights sold to the highest bidder.

  9. Who were bapidar peasants?

    Answer

    Marwar peasants with hereditary occupancy rights.

  10. What is lag-baag?

    Answer

    Cesses and levies collected in addition to land revenue.

  11. What did a dyodhidar do?

    Answer

    Controlled the palace gateway and access to the ruler.

  12. How did Mughal service change the Rajput ruler's position over his nobles?

    Answer

    Imperial recognition of succession, Mughal rank and wealth, and Mughal-style offices and records let him treat jagirs as grants he could transfer or resume.

  13. Why did the spread of ijara increase the burden on peasants?

    Answer

    The revenue farmer paid in advance and squeezed peasants to recover his money and make a profit.

  14. Why were court honours such as tazim politically important?

    Answer

    They let the ruler rank nobles by his favour, giving him leverage over powerful kinsmen.

Unit R15 · 1734 – c. 1900

The 18th and 19th Centuries: Marathas, 1818 Treaties, Society and Reform

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR5 Political and social condition in the 18th–19th centuries; peasant and tribal movements in the 20th century; Praja Mandal movements and mass awakening; integration of Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH3 19th–20th century: Revolt of 1857, peasant and tribal movements, political awakening, mass movements, integration
  • RH2 Revenue and administrative system and changing patterns

Topics this unit covers

  • Maratha intervention in Rajasthan
  • Treaties with the East India Company
  • Social reform in Rajasthan (sati, female infanticide, dakan pratha)

RAS Mains 2018 asked who Dungji and Jawaharji were (2 marks). The 1817–18 treaties, the AGG and the dates of social-reform bans are frequent Prelims topics.

2The big picture

In 1734 the Marathas were invited into Rajasthan's succession quarrels; within a generation they were collecting heavy tribute from almost every state. Disputes among Rajput rulers, plunder by Pindari bands (Amir Khan) and the collapse of order followed.

In 1817–18 Lord Hastings ended this by signing treaties of "subordinate isolation" with nearly all the states. The British protected the rulers and took over their foreign relations; in return the rulers paid tribute and accepted a British Political Agent. Peace returned — but the rulers became dependent on the British, the nobles lost power, and British officers began to interfere in successions and administration.

The 19th century also saw social reform imposed or encouraged by the British (bans on sati, female infanticide and witch-hunting) and led by Indians (Swami Dayanand and the Arya Samaj).

This unit explains why 20th-century movements in Rajasthan had two enemies — the British and the local rulers and jagirdars whom the British protected (R17–R20).

  1. 1734Bundi queen invites Malhar Rao Holkar
  2. 1736Peshwa Bajirao I visits Mewar
  3. 1748Bagru: Marathas intervene in the Jaipur succession
  4. 1755–56Jayappa Scindia killed at Nagaur; Ajmer passes to the Marathas
  5. 1787Tunga (Lalsot): Rajputs check Mahadji Scindia
  6. 1790Patan and Merta: Mahadji's victories
  7. 1803Alwar and Bharatpur sign treaties with the Company
  8. 1817–18Hastings' treaties with the Rajasthan states
  9. 1818Ajmer ceded to the British by Scindia
  10. 1822Bundi bans sati (first in Rajasthan)
  11. 1832Office of the Agent to the Governor-General (AGG) for Rajputana, Ajmer
  12. 1835Shekhawati Brigade raised
  13. 1841Mewar Bhil Corps raised at Kherwara
  14. 1847Dungji and Jawaharji's raids, including Nasirabad
  15. 1853Mewar bans dakan pratha (witch-hunting)
  16. 1875Mayo College, Ajmer
  17. 1883Swami Dayanand in Udaipur and Ajmer; Paropkarini Sabha
  18. 1888–89Walterkrit Rajputra Hitkarini Sabha

3Core notes

R15.1 The Marathas in Rajasthan

How they came in. Rajput rulers invited the Marathas into their own quarrels:

  • Bundi (1734): the queen of Budh Singh called Malhar Rao Holkar against Jaipur.
  • Jaipur (1747–50): Madho Singh used Holkar against his brother Ishwari Singh (Bagru, 1748).
  • Marwar (1750s): Ram Singh sought Scindia's help against Bakht Singh and Vijay Singh; Jayappa Scindia was murdered at Nagaur (1755), and the settlement gave Ajmer to the Marathas.
  • Mewar: succession disputes and tribute demands under Scindia and Holkar.

Resistance: the Hurda conference (1734) failed; at Tunga (Lalsot, 1787) Jaipur and Jodhpur defeated Mahadji Scindia, but his general De Boigne won at Patan and Merta (1790).

Effects: heavy tribute (khiraj) and repeated plunder ruined state finances and peasants; rulers raised cesses and turned to revenue farming (R14); Pindari bands (Amir Khan) added to the chaos. This weakness drove the rulers to accept British protection.

R15.2 The treaties of 1817–18

Background. Under Wellesley, Alwar and Bharatpur signed treaties (1803). The next Governor-General reversed his forward policy, leaving Rajasthan unprotected. During the Pindari and Third Anglo-Maratha War, Lord Hastings sent Charles Metcalfe to sign treaties with the states.

StateTreaty
KarauliNovember 1817 (first under Hastings)
Tonk (Amir Khan)November 1817
KotaDecember 1817 (supplementary articles for Zalim Singh, 1818)
JodhpurJanuary 1818
Udaipur (Mewar)January 1818
Bundi, Bikaner, KishangarhFebruary–March 1818
JaipurApril 1818
Banswara, Pratapgarh, Dungarpur, JaisalmerLater in 1818
Sirohi1823 (last)

Main terms:

  1. The Company would protect the state; the state would accept British supremacy and have no relations with other states without British consent.
  2. The state would pay tribute (khiraj) — often the amount previously paid to the Marathas.
  3. The state would supply troops when required.
  4. The ruler would remain absolute in internal affairs.

Administration: a Political Agent or Resident was posted to each state or group of states; in 1832 the office of the Agent to the Governor-General (AGG) for Rajputana was set up at Ajmer (later with a summer seat at Mount Abu). Ajmer–Merwara became a British province.

Consequences:

  • Peace and the end of Maratha and Pindari plunder.
  • Despite the promise of internal autonomy, British officers interfered in successions, minorities and administration.
  • Rulers, backed by the British, no longer needed their nobles' military support; nobles lost power and some rebelled (1857, R16).
  • Tribute drained finances; British customs and salt policies hurt trade (e.g. salt from Sambhar).
  • Rulers became answerable to the British rather than to their people — a key grievance of the later Praja Mandal movements.

R15.3 British military units and outlaws

The British raised local forces to control unrest:

ForceBase and purpose
Mer Battalion (1822)Beawar: to control the Mer people of Merwara
Shekhawati Brigade (1835)Jhunjhunu: to suppress raids in Shekhawati
Jodhpur Legion (1835)Erinpura
Mewar Bhil Corps (1841)Kherwara: to control Bhil unrest (R18)

Dungji and Jawaharji were Shekhawat Rajput cousins (of Bathoth–Patodha, Sikar) who served in, then turned against, the Shekhawati Brigade and raided British territory and cantonments. Dungji was captured and imprisoned in Agra fort; Jawaharji, with companions such as Lotiya Jat and Karna Meena, freed him. They plundered the Nasirabad cantonment treasury (1847) and are said to have distributed the loot among the poor. Dungji later surrendered to the ruler of Jodhpur and Jawaharji to Bikaner. Folk songs remember them as anti-British heroes.

R15.4 Social reform

State bans, mostly under British pressure:

PracticeFirst ban in RajasthanNotes
SatiBundi, 1822Other states followed; Mewar among the last (1861)
Female infanticideKota, 1833Linked to dowry and the expense of marriages
Dakan pratha (witch-hunting)Mewar, 1853Common among tribal communities
Samadhi (live burial of ascetics)Jaipur, 1844
Tyag (payments to Charans and bards at weddings)Regulated from the 1840sThe heavy cost encouraged infanticide

Walterkrit Rajputra Hitkarini Sabha (1888–89), named after the AGG Colonel Walter: set limits on marriage expenses and tyag and fixed minimum marriage ages for Rajputs.

Indian reformers:

  • Swami Dayanand Saraswati visited several states; at Udaipur (guest of Maharana Sajjan Singh) he completed Satyarth Prakash and founded the Paropkarini Sabha (1883); he died at Ajmer (30 October 1883). The Arya Samaj spread education and opposed child marriage and caste rigidity.
  • Har Bilas Sarda of Ajmer introduced the Child Marriage Restraint Act (1929), known as the Sarda Act.
  • Education: Mayo College, Ajmer (1875) for princes; state colleges in Jaipur and elsewhere; missionary schools.

4What historians say

For the Maratha period, V.S. Bhatnagar and Jadunath Sarkar describe the economic devastation; Rima Hooja notes that Rajput rulers' own disputes invited the intervention. On British paramountcy, Tod (himself a Political Agent) praised the treaties as restoring order; later historians — M.S. Jain, Kalu Ram Sharma and Shyam Sundar Saxena — stress how the system froze feudal power, enlarged British interference and created the conditions for 1857 and later movements.

5Prelims fact bank

  • The Marathas first entered Rajasthan when the Bundi queen invited Malhar Rao Holkar in 1734.
  • Jayappa Scindia was murdered at Nagaur in 1755; Ajmer passed to the Marathas.
  • At Tunga (Lalsot, 1787) Jaipur and Jodhpur defeated Mahadji Scindia.
  • De Boigne won at Patan and Merta (1790) for Mahadji Scindia.
  • Charles Metcalfe negotiated the 1817–18 treaties for Lord Hastings.
  • Karauli signed first under Hastings (November 1817); Sirohi last (1823).
  • Ajmer was ceded to the British by Scindia in 1818.
  • The AGG for Rajputana was set up at Ajmer in 1832.
  • Mer Battalion — Beawar (1822); Mewar Bhil Corps — Kherwara (1841).
  • Shekhawati Brigade (1835) was raised to suppress raids in Shekhawati.
  • Dungji and Jawaharji looted the Nasirabad cantonment (1847).
  • Bundi banned sati first (1822); Kota banned female infanticide first (1833).
  • Mewar banned dakan pratha first (1853).
  • The Walterkrit Rajputra Hitkarini Sabha (1888–89) regulated Rajput marriage expenses.
  • Swami Dayanand founded the Paropkarini Sabha at Udaipur (1883) and died at Ajmer.
  • Har Bilas Sarda of Ajmer sponsored the Child Marriage Restraint Act (1929).
  • Mayo College, Ajmer, was founded in 1875.

Exam traps

  • The Mer Battalion was at Beawar; the Mewar Bhil Corps was at Kherwara — do not mix them.
  • Alwar and Bharatpur signed in 1803 (Wellesley); the rest in 1817–18 (Hastings); Sirohi in 1823.
  • Bundi first banned sati; Kota first banned female infanticide; Mewar first banned dakan pratha.
  • Dayanand wrote Satyarth Prakash partly at Udaipur and died at Ajmer.
  • Tunga and Lalsot are the same battle (1787).

7Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words Discuss the main terms of the 1818 treaties and their effects on the Rajput states.

How to frame it

  • Background
  • Terms
  • Political effects
  • Economic and social effects
Model answer (86 words)

Weakened by Maratha tribute and Pindari raids, the Rajput states accepted Hastings' treaties, negotiated by Metcalfe in 1817–18. The Company promised protection; states surrendered foreign relations, paid tribute and supplied troops, while keeping internal autonomy. Peace returned, but Political Agents and the AGG (1832) interfered in successions and administration. Rulers, secure under British protection, curbed their nobles, provoking discontent seen in 1857. Tribute and British trade policies strained finances. Rulers became answerable to the British, not their subjects — the grievance behind later Praja Mandal movements.

5 marks · 50 words Describe the social reform measures in 19th-century Rajasthan.

How to frame it

  • State bans
  • Hitkarini Sabha
  • Arya Samaj
Model answer (61 words)

States banned sati (Bundi 1822), female infanticide (Kota 1833), samadhi (Jaipur 1844) and witch-hunting (Mewar 1853). The Walterkrit Rajputra Hitkarini Sabha (1888–89) limited marriage expenses, including tyag (the gifts Charans and Bhats claimed at Rajput weddings, a burden linked to female infanticide), and fixed marriage ages. Swami Dayanand's Arya Samaj, with the Paropkarini Sabha (1883), spread education and opposed child marriage.

2 marks · 15 words · RAS Mains 2018 Who were Dungji and Jawaharji?

How to frame it

  • Identity
  • Act
Model answer (12 words)

Shekhawat cousins who rebelled against the British and looted Nasirabad cantonment (1847).

Unit R23 · 1818 – 1959

Changing Patterns of Administration and Revenue, 1818–1959

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR4 Cooperation and resistance with central power; administrative and revenue system in medieval Rajasthan
  • PR5 Political and social condition in the 18th–19th centuries; peasant and tribal movements in the 20th century; Praja Mandal movements and mass awakening; integration of Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH2 Revenue and administrative system and changing patterns

Topics this unit covers

  • British paramountcy and the political agency system (AGG Rajputana, residents, political agents)
  • Ajmer-Merwara as directly ruled British territory
  • Changes in the jagirdari system and in land revenue under British influence: cash assessment, settlements, cesses
  • Administrative modernisation in the princely states (Bikaner, Jaipur, Jodhpur)
  • After integration: abolition of jagirs and zamindari; Tenancy and Land Revenue Acts; Panchayati Raj (1959)

The 2026 Mains line asks for "changing patterns", not only the medieval system. RAS Mains 2024 asked for the revenue system of medieval Rajasthan (10 marks); a question on how that system changed under the British and after 1947 is the natural next step. Pair this unit with R14 (the medieval system) and R17 (peasant movements, which were a reaction to these changes).

2The big picture

Until 1818 the Rajput states ran on the system described in R14: the ruler's khalsa land, jagirs held in return for military service, revenue taken largely in kind (batai, kunta) and a long list of cesses (lag-bag). The treaties of 1817–18 (R15) changed the frame. The British took charge of defence and foreign relations, so the jagirdar's military role faded while his rights over the peasant remained. A British political agency system supervised the states, and slowly pushed them towards cash revenue, regular land settlements, courts and departments. Peasants paid more in cash and cesses without the old protection, which fed the movements of the 20th century. After integration (1948–56) the new state of Rajasthan dismantled the system: jagirs were resumed (Act of 1952), tenants got secure rights (Tenancy Act 1955), revenue law was unified (Land Revenue Act 1956), zamindari and biswedari were abolished (1959), and Panchayati Raj began at Nagaur on 2 October 1959.

Answer frame for "changing patterns": medieval base (R14) → British-era changes (agency control, cash revenue, settlements, decline of jagirdars' service role) → post-1947 land reforms and democratic decentralisation.

  1. 1817–18Treaties of protection with the Rajputana states (details in R15)
  2. 1818Ajmer ceded to the British by Daulat Rao Sindhia; ruled directly
  3. 1832Office of Agent to the Governor-General (AGG) for Rajputana created at Ajmer
  4. 19th century (late)Survey and settlement operations begin in several states; shift towards cash revenue
  5. 1913Bikaner sets up a Representative Assembly under Maharaja Ganga Singh
  6. 1948–56Integration of Rajasthan in seven stages (R21)
  7. 1952Rajasthan Land Reforms and Resumption of Jagirs Act
  8. 1955Rajasthan Tenancy Act
  9. 1956Rajasthan Land Revenue Act; Ajmer merged into Rajasthan (1 November)
  10. 1959Rajasthan Zamindari and Biswedari Abolition Act; Panchayati Raj inaugurated at Nagaur (2 October)

3Core notes

R23.1 The political agency system

  • Under the treaties the states kept internal autonomy but accepted British "protection" and paid tribute (khiraj) in many cases.
  • The Agent to the Governor-General for Rajputana (from 1832, at Ajmer; later with a summer headquarters at Mount Abu) supervised the states through residents and political agents posted at their courts (for example the Mewar Political Agency at Udaipur).
  • Agents intervened in successions, minority administrations and disputes between rulers and jagirdars. During a ruler's minority the agency often ran the state through a council, and introduced British-style departments.
  • Ajmer-Merwara was British territory, ruled directly, and became a model for settlement and irrigation work (for example the tanks built in Merwara by Colonel Dixon).

R23.2 What changed in the jagirdari system

AspectBefore 1818 (R14)Under British paramountcy
Military service (chakri)The core duty of a jagirdarLost its purpose once the British guaranteed defence; often commuted to cash payments
Ruler–jagirdar relationKinship and service bond (bhai-bant)Rulers used British backing to curb jagirdars; disputes went to the agency
Jagirdar–peasant relationCustom limited demandsDemands rose: cash rent, begar (forced labour) and dozens of cesses (lag-bag)
Revenue formMostly in kind: batai (crop sharing), kunta (estimate)Gradual shift to cash assessment based on survey and settlement

The result was a jagirdar class that kept its income and privileges but no longer performed the service that justified them. Peasant movements in Bijolia, Begun, Shekhawati and Marwar (R17) targeted exactly these burdens.

R23.3 Administrative modernisation in the states

  • Land settlements (bandobast) in several states in the late 19th and early 20th century surveyed land, recorded rights and fixed cash assessments, often with British settlement officers on loan.
  • Departments and courts: police, customs, public works, education and medical departments, and chief courts replaced personal rule in the larger states.
  • Bikaner under Maharaja Ganga Singh: Representative Assembly (1913), a chief court, and the Gang Canal (opened 1927) that brought Sutlej water to the north of the state.
  • Jaipur appointed Sir Mirza Ismail as prime minister (1942–46), who pushed public works and administrative reform.
  • Limits: reforms were uneven, came from the top, and left jagirdari areas largely untouched, which is why the Praja Mandals (R20) demanded responsible government.

R23.4 After integration: dismantling the old order

Law or stepYearWhat it did
Rajasthan Land Reforms and Resumption of Jagirs Act1952Jagirs resumed by the state in stages, with compensation and rehabilitation grants; the jagirdar as revenue intermediary ended
Rajasthan Tenancy Act1955Uniform tenancy law; khatedari (heritable, transferable) rights for cultivators; protection against arbitrary eviction
Rajasthan Land Revenue Act1956Single revenue code for the whole state: revenue courts, records of rights, settlement
Rajasthan Zamindari and Biswedari Abolition Act1959Abolished the remaining intermediary tenures
Panchayati Raj2 October 1959Three-tier democratic decentralisation inaugurated at Nagaur by Jawaharlal Nehru; Rajasthan was the first state

The Tenancy Act and the Land Revenue Act are now Mains Paper III law topics (LW8, LW9), so this unit is the history behind them.

4What historians say

G.N. Sharma (Social Life in Medieval Rajasthan) and Dilbagh Singh (The State, Landlords and Peasants: Rajasthan in the 18th Century) describe the pre-colonial system. K.L. Kamal and the official Rajasthan District Gazetteers trace the settlements and the post-1947 reforms. Historians broadly agree that paramountcy froze the jagirdari order: it removed the military reason for jagirs while protecting the jagirdars, so the burden on peasants grew until land reform ended the system.

5Prelims fact bank

  • Ajmer was ceded to the British by Daulat Rao Sindhia in 1818 and ruled directly.
  • The office of Agent to the Governor-General for Rajputana was created in 1832 at Ajmer.
  • Political agents and residents represented the British at individual state courts.
  • Under paramountcy the jagirdar's military service lost its purpose and was often commuted to cash.
  • Batai was revenue taken as a share of the crop; kunta was revenue fixed by estimating the standing crop.
  • Lag-bag was the collective name for the many cesses levied on peasants.
  • Bikaner set up a Representative Assembly in 1913 under Maharaja Ganga Singh.
  • The Gang Canal, bringing Sutlej water to Bikaner, opened in 1927.
  • Sir Mirza Ismail was prime minister of Jaipur from 1942 to 1946.
  • The Rajasthan Land Reforms and Resumption of Jagirs Act was passed in 1952.
  • The Rajasthan Tenancy Act, 1955 gave cultivators khatedari rights.
  • The Rajasthan Land Revenue Act was passed in 1956.
  • The Rajasthan Zamindari and Biswedari Abolition Act was passed in 1959.
  • Panchayati Raj was inaugurated at Nagaur on 2 October 1959.

Exam traps

  • Ajmer-Merwara was British territory, not a princely state; it joined Rajasthan only on 1 November 1956.
  • Jagir resumption (1952) came before zamindari and biswedari abolition (1959).
  • Tenancy Act 1955, Land Revenue Act 1956: do not swap the years.
  • The AGG was at Ajmer (1832); Mount Abu was a later summer headquarters.

6Previous-year questions (RAS)

No RAS Mains question yet on changes after 1818 specifically. Related: RAS Mains 2024, "Describe the revenue system of Mediaeval Rajasthan" (10 marks), and 2023, "Who were the Dyodhidars?" (2 marks), both in R14. The 2026 wording "changing patterns" makes a question on this unit likely.

7Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words Discuss the changes in the land revenue and jagirdari system of Rajasthan under British paramountcy.

How to frame it

  • Base system before 1818
  • Agency control
  • Jagirdars lose military role, keep privileges
  • Cash revenue, settlements, cesses
  • Effect: peasant movements
  • Conclusion
Model answer (96 words)

Before 1818 jagirs were held for military service and revenue was taken mostly in kind through batai and kunta. The treaties of 1817–18 placed the states under British protection and the AGG (1832). With defence guaranteed, the jagirdar's service lost its purpose, yet his rights over peasants stayed and were often turned into cash. Settlements gradually brought cash assessment, while cesses (lag-bag) and begar grew. Reforms such as Bikaner's assembly (1913) were top-down and spared jagir areas. The rising burden produced the Bijolia, Begun and Shekhawati movements and was ended only by land reform after 1947.

5 marks · 50 words What were the main land reform laws passed in Rajasthan between 1952 and 1959?

How to frame it

  • Four laws with years
  • One-line purpose each
Model answer (39 words)

The Land Reforms and Resumption of Jagirs Act (1952) ended jagirs; the Tenancy Act (1955) gave cultivators khatedari rights; the Land Revenue Act (1956) unified revenue law; and the Zamindari and Biswedari Abolition Act (1959) removed the remaining intermediaries.

2 marks · 15 words What is khatedari right?

How to frame it

  • Answer
Model answer (14 words)

A heritable, transferable cultivating right given to tenants by the Rajasthan Tenancy Act, 1955.

8One-screen revision card

  • 1818 treaties → AGG Rajputana at Ajmer (1832) → political agents at courts; Ajmer-Merwara direct rule.
  • Jagirdar: military role gone, privileges kept; peasants paid cash rent, lag-bag, begar → 20th-century movements.
  • Modernisation: settlements, departments, courts; Bikaner assembly 1913, Gang Canal 1927.
  • Reforms: Jagirs 1952 · Tenancy 1955 · Land Revenue 1956 · Zamindari-Biswedari 1959 · Panchayati Raj, Nagaur, 2 Oct 1959.

Unit R16 · May 1857 – April 1859

The Revolt of 1857 in Rajasthan

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR5 Political and social condition in the 18th–19th centuries; peasant and tribal movements in the 20th century; Praja Mandal movements and mass awakening; integration of Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH3 19th–20th century: Revolt of 1857, peasant and tribal movements, political awakening, mass movements, integration

Topics this unit covers

  • Revolt of 1857 in Rajasthan: centres, leaders, British officers

Nasirabad, Erinpura–Auwa, Kota and Tatya Tope's campaign are standard. Prelims likes cantonment–date and officer–state matching. For the all-India revolt see unit I32 of the Indian History notebook.

2The big picture

In 1857 Rajasthan had six cantonments with Indian troops of the Company. Soldiers rose at Nasirabad, Neemuch, Deoli and Erinpura; the rebels joined discontented nobles, most famously Thakur Kushal Singh of Auwa, and in Kota the state's own troops killed the British Political Agent and held the city for six months. In 1858 Tatya Tope crossed Rajasthan several times.

But the rulers stayed loyal to the British: they sheltered British families, sent troops and pursued rebels. The revolt here was therefore led by soldiers and some jagirdars, with popular sympathy but no princely leadership.

The key analytical point: in Rajasthan the revolt was directed as much against the rulers protected by the British as against the British themselves. Auwa shows nobles' anger at both.

  1. 28 May 1857Nasirabad: 15th Bengal Native Infantry revolts
  2. 3 June 1857Neemuch revolts; British refugees sheltered by Mewar
  3. June 1857Deoli troops revolt
  4. 21 Aug 1857Jodhpur Legion revolts at Erinpura; marches to Auwa
  5. 8 Sept 1857Bithora: rebels defeat the Jodhpur state army
  6. 18 Sept 1857Chelawas (Auwa): Lawrence defeated, Monck Mason killed
  7. 15 Oct 1857Kota: Major Burton killed; rebels hold Kota
  8. 24 Jan 1858Auwa captured by Colonel Holmes
  9. 30 Mar 1858Kota recaptured by General Roberts
  10. 1858Tatya Tope's campaigns in Rajasthan
  11. 18 Apr 1859Tatya Tope executed at Shivpuri

3Core notes

R16.1 Background: cantonments and causes

The six cantonments: Nasirabad, Neemuch, Deoli, Beawar, Erinpura and Kherwara. The units at Beawar (Mer Battalion) and Kherwara (Mewar Bhil Corps) did not revolt.

British officers:

PostOfficer
AGG, RajputanaGeorge Patrick Lawrence
Political Agent, MewarCaptain Showers
Political Agent, MarwarCaptain Monck Mason (killed at Auwa)
Political Agent, KotaMajor Burton (killed at Kota)
Political Agent, JaipurMajor Eden

Causes in Rajasthan:

  1. Soldiers' grievances and the all-India news (cartridges, Meerut, Delhi).
  2. Nobles' anger at British interference and at rulers who, backed by the British, cut their powers and privileges.
  3. Popular resentment of British interference in social customs and of the economic burden of tribute.

R16.2 Nasirabad, Neemuch and Deoli

  • Nasirabad (28 May 1857): the 15th Bengal Native Infantry, joined by the 30th, revolted, killed officers and marched to Delhi. Nasirabad was the first rising in Rajasthan.
  • Neemuch (3 June 1857): troops revolted (Neemuch is now in Madhya Pradesh). British families fled; Maharana Swaroop Singh of Mewar sheltered them at Udaipur (Jagmandir), and Doongla villagers protected some.
  • Deoli: the Kota Contingent's troops revolted and joined the march to Delhi.

Ajmer, with its arsenal and treasury, was saved for the British by moving loyal Mer troops from Beawar.

R16.3 Erinpura and Auwa: Thakur Kushal Singh

On 21 August 1857 the Jodhpur Legion at Erinpura revolted with the cry "Chalo Dilli, maro Firangi" ("To Delhi, kill the foreigners") and joined Thakur Kushal Singh Champawat of Auwa (Pali), already in conflict with the ruler of Jodhpur. Other thakurs (Asop, Gular, Alaniyawas and others) joined him.

  • Bithora (8 September 1857): the rebels defeated the Jodhpur state army.
  • Chelawas (18 September 1857): AGG Lawrence's force was beaten; Political Agent Monck Mason was killed and his head displayed at the gate of Auwa fort.
  • In January 1858 Colonel Holmes besieged and captured Auwa; the fort was destroyed and the image of Sugali Mata (the rebels' goddess) was taken away.
  • Kushal Singh escaped, later surrendered (1860), was acquitted by an inquiry and died in 1864.

Auwa is the clearest case in Rajasthan of nobles fighting both the British and their own ruler.

R16.4 Kota and Tatya Tope

Kota (15 October 1857): the state's own troops, led by Jaidayal and Mehrab Khan, killed Political Agent Major Burton and his sons. The Maharao Ram Singh II was kept under the rebels' control, and they ran Kota for about six months. General Roberts recaptured Kota on 30 March 1858; Jaidayal and Mehrab Khan were later hanged. Kota is notable because the rising came from a state's army and had wide popular support.

Tatya Tope, after the fall of Gwalior (1858), moved through Rajasthan seeking support:

  • Fought at Kotra (Bhilwara); defeated near the Banas.
  • Took Jhalrapatan and its treasury (Jhalawar).
  • Moved through Banswara, Salumber, Tonk and Sikar, helped by nobles such as the Rawat of Kothariya and the Rawat of Salumber.
  • Betrayed by Man Singh of Narwar, he was captured and executed at Shivpuri on 18 April 1859.

Other centres: Tonk (troops and the Nawab's uncle Mir Alam Khan rebelled, though the Nawab stayed loyal); Bharatpur and Dholpur (Gujar, Meo and soldier risings); Bikaner-born Amarchand Bantia, treasurer at Gwalior, gave money to the rebels and was hanged (1858).

R16.5 Role of the rulers; why the revolt failed

The rulers sided with the British:

  • Mewar sheltered British refugees.
  • Bikaner's Sardar Singh led his troops in person outside his state (towards Hisar) to help the British.
  • Jaipur, Jodhpur, Karauli, Alwar and others sent troops and arrested rebels.

Lord Canning later called the princes "breakwaters in the storm".

Why the revolt failed in Rajasthan:

  1. No princely leadership; the rulers' armies and resources backed the British.
  2. No coordination between centres (Nasirabad, Auwa, Kota) or common programme.
  3. Most soldiers left for Delhi rather than holding Rajasthan.
  4. Better British organisation and reinforcements after Delhi fell (September 1857).

Consequences: Crown rule (1858) and a new policy of preserving the princes; the rulers were rewarded; the nobles who rebelled lost estates. But the memory of Auwa, Kota and Tatya Tope fed later folk songs and nationalism.

Was it a "freedom struggle" in Rajasthan? Nationalist writers emphasise Auwa and Kota as patriotic. Others note that the rebel nobles were also defending their own privileges against their rulers. Both elements were present; in an answer, present them together.

4What historians say

Accounts include British officers' reports (Lawrence, Reminiscences of Forty-three Years in India), Persian and Rajasthani records, and folk songs (the Auwa songs). Modern studies: N.R. Khadgawat, Rajasthan's Role in the Struggle of 1857; M.L. Sharma; Rima Hooja. Khadgawat highlighted popular participation; others stress the dominance of soldiers and nobles.

5Prelims fact bank

  • The six cantonments: Nasirabad, Neemuch, Deoli, Beawar, Erinpura, Kherwara.
  • Beawar and Kherwara troops did not revolt.
  • AGG in 1857: George Patrick Lawrence.
  • Nasirabad rose first in Rajasthan (28 May 1857).
  • Neemuch revolted on 3 June 1857; Mewar's Swaroop Singh sheltered British refugees.
  • The Jodhpur Legion revolted at Erinpura on 21 August 1857.
  • The Erinpura rebels' cry was "Chalo Dilli, maro Firangi".
  • Thakur Kushal Singh Champawat of Auwa led the Marwar rising.
  • Bithora (8 Sept 1857): rebels defeated the Jodhpur army.
  • Chelawas (18 Sept 1857): Monck Mason was killed.
  • Colonel Holmes captured Auwa in January 1858; the Sugali Mata image was removed.
  • Major Burton was killed at Kota on 15 October 1857.
  • Jaidayal and Mehrab Khan led the Kota rising.
  • General Roberts recaptured Kota on 30 March 1858.
  • Tatya Tope took Jhalrapatan; he was executed at Shivpuri on 18 April 1859.
  • Amarchand Bantia of Bikaner was hanged for helping the rebels (1858).
  • Sardar Singh of Bikaner led his troops in person to help the British.

Exam traps

  • Monck Mason (Marwar) was killed at Auwa; Major Burton (Kota) at Kota.
  • Neemuch is now in Madhya Pradesh but was a Rajputana cantonment in 1857.
  • The Mer Battalion (Beawar) and Mewar Bhil Corps (Kherwara) stayed loyal.
  • Bithora (8 Sept) came before Chelawas (18 Sept).
  • Tatya Tope was executed at Shivpuri (Sipri), not in Rajasthan.

The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

7Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words Describe the course and nature of the Revolt of 1857 in Rajasthan.
