  • Auwa
  • Kota
  • Tatya Tope
  • Rulers' role and nature
Model answer (94 words)

The revolt began at Nasirabad (28 May 1857), followed by Neemuch and Deoli. On 21 August the Jodhpur Legion at Erinpura joined Thakur Kushal Singh of Auwa, defeating the Jodhpur army at Bithora and killing Monck Mason at Chelawas. At Kota, state troops under Jaidayal and Mehrab Khan killed Major Burton and held the city until March 1858. Tatya Tope later crossed Rajasthan, aided by some nobles. The rulers backed the British. The revolt was thus led by soldiers and nobles, aimed at both the British and the rulers they protected, and lacked coordination.

  • Coordination

The rulers supported the British with troops and money. Centres such as Nasirabad, Auwa and Kota acted separately, without a common plan. Most mutineers left for Delhi instead of holding Rajasthan. After Delhi fell, British reinforcements crushed scattered resistance.

Unit R17 · 1897 – 1947

Peasant Movements

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR5 Political and social condition in the 18th–19th centuries; peasant and tribal movements in the 20th century; Praja Mandal movements and mass awakening; integration of Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH3 19th–20th century: Revolt of 1857, peasant and tribal movements, political awakening, mass movements, integration

Topics this unit covers

  • Peasant movements of Rajasthan: Bijolia, Begun, Shekhawati, Alwar, Bundi, Marwar

Tier A, asked almost every year: RAS Mains 2024 (Chandawal incident of 1942, 5 marks), 2021 (Begun movement, 5 marks), 2018 (causes of peasant unrest in jagirdari areas, 5 marks); RAS Prelims 2021 and 2024 on Bijolia. Bijolia is the most-asked single movement.

2The big picture

In the princely states of Rajputana, most villages were not run directly by the ruler but by jagirdars (thakurs, raos, rawats), nobles who held estates (thikanas) in return for loyalty and military service. Under British protection after 1818, neither the jagirdars nor the rulers faced any real check. Jagirdars raised land revenue, added dozens of cesses (lag-baag) and demanded forced labour (begar), while peasants had no secure rights to their land. From the 1890s peasants organised against them: first in Bijolia (Mewar), the longest peasant movement in India, then in Begun, Bundi, Alwar, Shekhawati, Marwar and Bikaner.

These movements began as protests against particular taxes, often organised through caste panchayats of the Dhakad, Jat or Meo peasants. Under leaders such as Vijay Singh Pathik, Manikyalal Verma and Ramnarayan Chaudhary, they adopted Gandhian methods (refusal to pay, boycott, peasant panchayats) and linked up with the national movement and later the Praja Mandals (R20). They weakened jagirdari long before it was legally abolished after independence (R23).

For 5-mark answers, learn each movement as: where, who (peasant caste), against whom, leaders, key events, outcome. For causes (2018), use the five headings of section 1. Prelims tests firing dates, places and the names of those killed; the traps below separate look-alike events.

  1. 1897Bijolia peasants send Nanji and Thakri Patel to Udaipur
  2. 1903Chanwari tax imposed in Bijolia
  3. 1913Sadhu Sitaram Das leads non-cultivation in Bijolia
  4. 1916–17Vijay Singh Pathik joins; Uparmal Panch Board formed
  5. 1921Begun movement begins (Menal)
  6. 1922Bijolia settlement with AGG Holland
  7. 2 Apr 1923Dabi firing (Bundi): Nanak Bhil and Devlal Gurjar killed
  8. 13 July 1923Govindpura firing (Begun): Rupaji and Kirpaji killed
  9. 14 May 1925Neemuchana firing (Alwar)
  10. 1925Jat Mahasabha session at Pushkar
  11. 1932Meo peasant movement in Alwar and Bharatpur
  12. 1934Sikar Jat Prajapati Mahayagya; Katrathal women's conference (25 Apr)
  13. 25 Apr 1935Kudan firing (Sikar)
  14. 1941Final Bijolia settlement; Marwar Kisan Sabha
  15. 28 Mar 1942Chandawal incident (Marwar)
  16. 1944–46Dudhwa Khara movement (Bikaner)
  17. 13 Mar 1947Dabra incident (Marwar)

3Core notes

R17.1 Causes of peasant unrest in jagirdari areas

Khalsa and jagir; lag-baag and begar. In a princely state, land was of two kinds. Khalsa land was administered directly by the ruler's officials. Jagir land, often most of the state, was held by nobles who collected revenue from the peasants for themselves and owed the ruler service and tribute (R14). Within a jagir, the peasant paid the jagirdar a share of the crop as rent (often a third to a half), plus lag-baag, extra cesses on almost anything: marriages, deaths, cattle, festivals, the jagirdar's own family events. Bijolia peasants counted 84 of them. They also owed begar, unpaid labour such as carrying loads or working the jagirdar's fields. A peasant with hereditary rights (bapidar) had some security; others could be evicted at will. Because the jagirdar was landlord, tax collector, judge and police in one, the peasant had nowhere to appeal.

Five causes turned this burden into revolt (RAS Mains 2018):

  1. Economic burden: high land revenue, often half the crop; dozens of cesses; begar; and new levies, such as war funds during the First World War.
  2. Insecurity: peasants, especially those without hereditary rights, could be evicted; rent receipts and land records were often not given, so demands could be raised at will.
  3. Social humiliation: jagirdars controlled peasants' customs, marriages and dress, forbade them to ride horses or wear certain ornaments, and their men abused women.
  4. British protection of jagirdars and rulers after the 1818 treaties: the old checks (fear of rebellion or of a rival power) disappeared, and rulers sided with their nobles.
  5. New awareness: the famine of 1899–1900, war levies and returning soldiers; newspapers (Pratap, Rajasthan Kesari, Abhyudaya, Bharat Mitra); the Arya Samaj and caste associations (Dhakad, Jat); and the non-cooperation movement, which showed peasants what organised refusal could achieve.

R17.2 Bijolia (1897–1941): the longest peasant movement

Where and who. Bijolia was a thikana of Mewar (now Bhilwara district) on the Uparmal plateau. Its peasants were mainly Dhakads, a cultivating caste, and their struggle was against the Rao of Bijolia, a first-class noble of Mewar. It went through three phases.

1897–1915: local protest. At a death feast at Girdharipura (1897), the assembled peasants decided to send Nanji and Thakri Patel to complain to the Maharana at Udaipur. Nothing changed. In 1903 the Rao imposed a chanwari tax on every marriage; the Dhakads responded by refusing to hold marriages at all. A talwar bandhai (succession) levy, which the new Rao passed on to the peasants, followed in 1906. In 1913 Sadhu Sitaram Das led a boycott of cultivation: peasants left the land unsown rather than pay.

1916–1927: organised movement. The revolutionary Vijay Singh Pathik (born Bhoop Singh), who had come to Mewar after the failed Rajasthan conspiracy of 1915, took charge. He formed the Uparmal Panch Board (1917), a peasants' council that acted almost as a parallel government: it decided disputes, organised refusal to pay revenue, and enforced social boycott of those who broke ranks. He spread the story across India through newspapers, above all Ganesh Shankar Vidyarthi's Pratap of Kanpur; Abhyudaya and Bharat Mitra also reported the movement (RAS Prelims 2024). Gandhi sent Mahadev Desai to inquire. Several inquiry commissions followed, and in 1922 a settlement reached through the Agent to the Governor-General, Holland, abolished many cesses and begar. But it was poorly implemented.

1927–1941: final phase. When new revenue settlements raised rates again, peasants gave up their land in protest, and it was given to others. Leadership passed to Manikyalal Verma, Haribhau Upadhyay and Jamnalal Bajaj; women such as Rama Devi took part (RAS Prelims 2021). In 1941 Mewar's prime minister Sir T. Vijayaraghavacharya and revenue minister Mohan Singh Mehta settled the dispute and returned the peasants' lands.

Why Bijolia mattered. It lasted about 44 years, the longest non-violent peasant movement in India, and it was organised: the Panch Board, refusal of revenue, boycott and press publicity anticipated the methods Gandhi later used nationally. It turned a caste community's grievance into a political cause, gave Rajasthan its first generation of popular leaders (Pathik, Verma, Upadhyay), and inspired movements in neighbouring Begun and Bundi. The Mewar Praja Mandal (1938) grew out of this tradition (R20).

R17.3 Begun, Bundi, Alwar and the Meos

Begun (1921–25) (RAS Mains 2021). In the neighbouring Begun thikana (Mewar, now Chittorgarh), Dhakad peasants, inspired by Bijolia, rose against the Rawat of Begun's cesses and begar. The movement began at Menal in 1921 under Ramnarayan Chaudhary, and later Vijay Singh Pathik. In 1923 Rawat Anup Singh agreed to a settlement that met many demands. The Mewar government and British officials rejected it as a "Bolshevik settlement", fearing that concessions won by a peasant union would spread (the Russian revolution of 1917 was fresh in their minds), and the Rawat was put under supervision. The Trench Commission (1923) was appointed. When peasants met at Govindpura on 13 July 1923, police fired; Rupaji and Kirpaji Dhakad were killed. Pathik was arrested and imprisoned. Yet most demands, the reduction of cesses and begar, were conceded by 1925.

Bundi (1922–26). Peasants of the Barad region protested against cesses, begar and war levies under Nainuram Sharma. At Dabi (2 April 1923) police fired on a meeting; Nanak Bhil, who was singing the flag song, and Devlal Gurjar were killed. The movement shows that peasants of different castes, Bhil and Gurjar, joined together.

Alwar: Neemuchana (1925). Here the protesters were Rajput peasants, who objected to a steep rise in land revenue and new restrictions under Maharaja Jai Singh. On 14 May 1925 state troops fired on a gathering at Neemuchana village and set houses on fire; many were killed. Gandhi, in Young India, called it worse than Jallianwala Bagh, "Dyerism double distilled". The Maharaja was later deposed (1933).

The Meo movement (1932–33). Meo peasants of Alwar and Bharatpur protested against revenue increases and cesses under Chaudhary Yasin Khan. It took a communal turn and was suppressed with British help, a reminder that peasant grievances could be pulled in different directions.

R17.4 Shekhawati and Sikar: the Jat peasant movement

In the Shekhawati region of Jaipur state (Sikar and Jhunjhunu), Jat peasants struggled against the thikanedars from the 1920s. Here social and economic grievances were tightly linked: thikanedars not only took heavy rent and cesses but also denied Jats symbols of status, such as riding a horse at a wedding. The movement therefore mixed caste uplift with agrarian demands.

Its leaders included Kunwar Panne Singh Deorod, Sardar Harlal Singh and Thakur Deshraj, and it organised through the Jat Mahasabha, whose session at Pushkar (1925) spread awareness. The Sikar Jat Prajapati Mahayagya (January 1934) was a mass assembly in which Jats asserted their social status. At Katrathal on 25 April 1934, thousands of women, led by Kishori Devi, met to protest against the ill-treatment of women by the men of the Thakur of Sihot, an early example of women organising in rural Rajasthan. At Jaisinghpura (Jhunjhunu, 1934), an attack on peasants led to one of the first cases in which the guilty were punished. At Kudan (25 April 1935), police fired during revenue collection in Sikar, killing several peasants; the matter was raised in the British Parliament. The movement merged with the Jaipur Praja Mandal and won reductions of rent and cesses by 1946–47.

R17.5 Marwar and Bikaner: peasants and the Praja Mandals

In Marwar the peasant struggle merged with the demand for responsible government. Jai Narayan Vyas and the Marwar Lok Parishad (1938) took up peasants' grievances against the jagirdars, and the Marwar Kisan Sabha was formed in 1941. The jagirdars saw both as threats and answered with violence.

The Chandawal incident (28 March 1942) (RAS Mains 2024). Lok Parishad workers held a meeting at Chandawal (Sojat, now Pali district) to observe "Responsible Government Day". The Thakur of Chandawal's men attacked them and injured many. The incident showed that the jagirdars, not only the ruler, stood in the way of reform; it won the Lok Parishad wider peasant support and strengthened the demand for responsible government and for the abolition of jagirdari.

The Dabra incident (13 March 1947). At Dabra (Didwana, Nagaur), a joint meeting of the Lok Parishad and the Kisan Sabha was attacked by jagirdars' men; several people were killed. Coming on the eve of independence, it hardened opinion against jagirdari.

Bikaner: Dudhwa Khara (1944–46). In Churu, peasants protested against a jagirdar's oppression under Hanuman Singh Budania, with the support of the Bikaner Praja Parishad.

From caste protest to politics. Across Rajasthan, peasant movements followed a common path. They began within a caste community (Dhakads, Jats, Meos), using caste panchayats and social boycott, and against a particular tax. Outside leaders (Pathik, Chaudhary, Verma, Vyas) linked them to newspapers and Gandhian methods. In the 1930s and 1940s they merged into the Praja Mandals, which made the abolition of begar and cesses part of the demand for responsible government. After independence, the Rajasthan Land Reforms and Resumption of Jagirs Act (1952) and the Rajasthan Tenancy Act (1955) finished what the movements had started (R23).

How this unit connects.

  • Before: the jagirdari and revenue system (R14); the 1818 treaties and British paramountcy (R15).
  • Alongside: Bhil and Meena tribal movements (R18); newspapers, Seva Sangh and revolutionaries such as Pathik (R19).
  • After: the Praja Mandals (R20); integration (R21); land reform and the end of jagirdari (R23).
  • India: peasant movements elsewhere, such as Champaran, Kheda and Bardoli (I33); Gandhian methods (I39).
  • People: Vijay Singh Pathik, Manikyalal Verma, Ramnarayan Chaudhary, Kishori Devi, Jai Narayan Vyas (R22).

4What historians say

Standard studies: Ram Pande, Agrarian Movement in Rajasthan; B.L. Panagariya; Shankar Sahai Saxena on Bijolia; and Pema Ram, Agrarian Movement in Rajasthan, 1913–1947. Pema Ram stresses the role of caste associations (Dhakad, Jat) in organising peasants; others stress the link with Gandhian nationalism. Both agree the movements weakened jagirdari long before its legal abolition (1952 onwards).

Exam traps

  • Katrathal (25 April 1934) and Kudan (25 April 1935) share the date — different years.
  • Govindpura firing was in Begun; Dabi firing was in Bundi.
  • The board was the Uparmal Panch Board — Uparmal is the plateau.
  • Neemuchana (Alwar, 1925) was a Rajput peasants' movement, not a Jat one.
  • Chandawal (1942) and Dabra (1947) were in Marwar; Dudhwa Khara in Bikaner.
  • The RPSC key (2024) did not accept that Pathik went to Wardha to meet Gandhi for Bijolia; Gandhi sent Mahadev Desai to inquire.
  • Bijolia and Begun were both Dhakad movements in Mewar thikanas; Shekhawati was Jat; the Meo movement was in Alwar and Bharatpur.

7Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words Peasant movements in Rajasthan prepared the ground for the Praja Mandals. Discuss.

How to frame it

  • Grievances against jagirdari
  • Organisation and methods
  • Leaders trained
  • Merger with Praja Mandals
  • Outcome
Model answer (90 words)

Peasant movements against lag-baag, begar and insecure tenure began in Bijolia (1897) and spread to Begun, Bundi, Shekhawati and Marwar. Through caste panchayats and the Uparmal Panch Board they learned non-payment, boycott and publicity, the methods the Praja Mandals later used. They trained leaders such as Pathik, Manikyalal Verma and Ramnarayan Chaudhary, who founded or led Praja Mandals. Firing at Govindpura, Dabi and Kudan exposed the states. In the 1940s the Marwar Lok Parishad and Jaipur Praja Mandal absorbed peasant demands, making the end of jagirdari part of responsible government.

5 marks · 50 words · RAS Mains 2018 What were the causes of peasant unrest in the jagirdari areas of Rajasthan?

How to frame it

  • Economic
  • Insecurity
  • Social
  • British protection
  • Awareness
Model answer (44 words)

Jagirdars levied heavy revenue, dozens of cesses (lag-baag) and begar. Peasants lacked secure tenure and receipts. Jagirdars humiliated them socially. British protection after 1818 removed checks on rulers and jagirdars. Famines, war levies, newspapers, caste associations and Gandhian nationalism made peasants aware and organised.

5 marks · 50 words · RAS Mains 2021 Write a note on the Begun peasant movement.

How to frame it

  • Where, who
  • Leaders
  • Bolshevik settlement
  • Govindpura
  • Outcome
Model answer (45 words)

Begun (Mewar) Dhakad peasants rose in 1921 against cesses and begar, led by Ramnarayan Chaudhary and Vijay Singh Pathik. Mewar rejected the Rawat's 1923 "Bolshevik settlement". At Govindpura (13 July 1923) police killed Rupaji and Kirpaji. Despite Pathik's arrest, most demands were won by 1925.

5 marks · 50 words · RAS Mains 2024 Describe the Chandawal incident of 1942.

How to frame it

  • Context
  • Event
  • Significance
Model answer (44 words)

On 28 March 1942 Marwar Lok Parishad workers met at Chandawal (Sojat) to observe Responsible Government Day. The Thakur of Chandawal's men attacked them, injuring many. The incident exposed jagirdari violence, strengthened peasant support for the Lok Parishad and intensified demands for responsible government.

5 marks · 50 words Why is the Bijolia movement significant?

How to frame it

  • Duration
  • Methods
  • Leadership
  • Result
Model answer (41 words)

Bijolia (1897–1941) was India's longest non-violent peasant movement. Dhakad peasants used non-payment, boycott and panchayats under Sitaram Das, Pathik and Manikyalal Verma. It trained Rajasthan's leaders, drew national attention and won the abolition of many cesses and the return of land.

8One-screen revision card

  • System: khalsa vs jagir; rent + lag-baag + begar; bapidar vs non-bapidar.
  • Causes: revenue + lag-baag + begar; insecurity; humiliation; British protection; awareness.
  • Bijolia: Dhakads, Uparmal; 1897 Nanji–Thakri; 1903 chanwari; 1913 Sitaram Das; 1917 Pathik, Uparmal Panch Board; Pratap, Abhyudaya, Bharat Mitra; 1922 Holland; Rama Devi; 1941 settlement.
  • Begun: 1921 Menal; Ramnarayan Chaudhary; Bolshevik settlement; Trench; Govindpura 13 July 1923 (Rupaji, Kirpaji).
  • Bundi: Nainuram Sharma; Dabi 2 Apr 1923 (Nanak Bhil, Devlal Gurjar).
  • Alwar: Neemuchana 14 May 1925 (Rajput peasants). Meo 1932 (Yasin Khan).
  • Shekhawati: Jats; Pushkar 1925; Prajapati Mahayagya 1934; Katrathal 25 Apr 1934 (Kishori Devi); Kudan 25 Apr 1935.
  • Marwar: Lok Parishad, Kisan Sabha 1941; Chandawal 28 Mar 1942; Dabra 13 Mar 1947. Bikaner: Dudhwa Khara (Budania).

9Self-test

  1. What were lag-baag and begar?

    Answer

    Lag-baag were extra cesses on peasants; begar was forced, unpaid labour.

  2. Why did British protection after 1818 make peasant conditions worse?

    Answer

    It removed the old checks on rulers and jagirdars, who no longer feared rebellion or rival powers.

  3. Which peasant caste led the Bijolia movement?

    Answer

    The Dhakads.

  4. What tax did the Rao of Bijolia impose in 1903, and how did peasants respond?

    Answer

    The chanwari (marriage) tax; they stopped holding marriages.

  5. What was the Uparmal Panch Board, and how did it work?

    Answer

    Pathik's peasant council (1917), which settled disputes, organised refusal of revenue and enforced boycott, almost a parallel government.

  6. Why was the Begun settlement called "Bolshevik"?

    Answer

    The Mewar government and British officials saw concessions won by a peasant union as dangerously radical.

  7. Who were killed at Govindpura?

    Answer

    Rupaji and Kirpaji Dhakad (13 July 1923).

  8. Who were killed at Dabi?

    Answer

    Nanak Bhil and Devlal Gurjar (2 April 1923).

  9. In which state did the Neemuchana firing take place, and who were the protesters?

    Answer

    Alwar (14 May 1925); Rajput peasants.

  10. What was the Katrathal conference?

    Answer

    A women's conference (25 April 1934), led by Kishori Devi, against ill-treatment of women by the Thakur of Sihot's men.

  11. Where and when was the Chandawal incident, and why did it matter?

    Answer

    Chandawal, Sojat (Pali), 28 March 1942; it exposed jagirdari violence and strengthened the demand for responsible government.

  12. How did peasant movements connect with the Praja Mandals?

    Answer

    They trained leaders and methods, and in the 1940s merged into the Praja Mandals, whose programme included ending begar and cesses.

Unit R18 · 1818 – 1947

Tribal Movements

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR5 Political and social condition in the 18th–19th centuries; peasant and tribal movements in the 20th century; Praja Mandal movements and mass awakening; integration of Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH3 19th–20th century: Revolt of 1857, peasant and tribal movements, political awakening, mass movements, integration
  • RH6 Tribes and their traditions

Topics this unit covers

  • Tribal movements: Bhil, Mer, Meena; Govind Guru, Motilal Tejawat

RAS Mains 2016 asked about "Mewar Pukar" (2 marks). Mangarh (1913), Govind Guru, Motilal Tejawat's Eki movement and Neemda (1922) recur in Prelims.

2The big picture

Rajasthan's tribal communities — Bhils and Garasias of the southern hills (Mewar, Dungarpur, Banswara, Sirohi), Mers of Merwara, and Meenas of the east — had lived with considerable autonomy, collecting protection dues (bolai, rakhwali) and using forests freely. British paramountcy after 1818 brought new revenue demands, forest restrictions, police and military control. Tribals resisted.

The movements had two strands: social reform (giving up liquor, meat and superstition; self-respect) and resistance to begar, taxes and the states' agents. The leaders Govind Guru and Motilal Tejawat combined both; their gatherings at Mangarh (1913) and Neemda (1922) ended in massacres.

Tribal movements were the most heavily repressed in Rajasthan. In answers, link them to social reform and to the later work of constructive workers (Bhogilal Pandya, Manikyalal Verma) and the Praja Mandals.

  1. 1818–1860Repeated Bhil risings in Mewar and the south
  2. 1818–1821Mer resistance in Merwara
  3. 1822Mer Battalion raised at Beawar
  4. 1841Mewar Bhil Corps raised at Kherwara
  5. 1883Govind Guru founds the Samp Sabha
  6. 17 Nov 1913Mangarh massacre (Banswara)
  7. 1921Motilal Tejawat begins the Eki movement at Matrikundia; "Mewar Pukar"
  8. 7 Mar 1922Neemda massacre (Vijaynagar, Idar)
  9. 1924–1930Criminal Tribes laws applied to Meenas
  10. 1944Meena conference at Neem ka Thana
  11. 19 June 1947Rastapal (Dungarpur): Kalibai killed

3Core notes

R18.1 Bhil and Mer resistance in the 19th century

  • After 1818, Bhils in Mewar, Dungarpur, Banswara and Sirohi rose repeatedly against British-backed revenue demands and the loss of their traditional dues and forest rights.
  • The British raised the Mewar Bhil Corps (1841) at Kherwara, recruiting Bhils themselves, to control the region.
  • Mers of Merwara (around Beawar) resisted British control (1818–21); the British pacified them and raised the Mer Battalion (1822) at Beawar. Colonel Dixon founded Nayanagar (Beawar) and settled Mers as cultivators.

R18.2 Govind Guru and the Bhagat movement

Govind Guru (Govindgiri, born 1858, village Bansia, Dungarpur), a Banjara by birth, worked among the Bhils of Dungarpur, Banswara and neighbouring Gujarat and Malwa.

  • Founded the Samp Sabha (1883) — "samp" meaning unity.
  • Taught Bhils to give up liquor, meat and theft, to keep clean, to worship at the dhuni (sacred fire), to educate children, and to refuse begar and unjust taxes. Followers were called bhagats.
  • The movement alarmed the rulers of Banswara, Dungarpur and Santrampur and the British, who saw it as a challenge to their authority.

Mangarh massacre (17 November 1913): thousands of Bhils gathered on Mangarh hill (Banswara, on the Gujarat border) for an annual gathering. British-led troops of the Mewar Bhil Corps and the states fired on them; a large number were killed (figures vary, from hundreds to about 1,500). It is called the "Jallianwala of the tribals" — six years before Jallianwala Bagh.

Govind Guru was imprisoned, later released on condition of staying out of the area, and died in 1931 at Kamboi (Gujarat).

R18.3 Motilal Tejawat and the Eki movement

Motilal Tejawat (born at Kolyari, Udaipur; called "Bawji" by the Bhils), a former employee of the Jhadol thikana, launched the Eki ("unity") movement in 1921 from Matrikundia (Chittorgarh).

  • Mewar Pukar: a memorandum of 21 demands presented to the Maharana of Mewar (1921), asking for an end to begar, a reduction of cesses and land revenue, an end to harassment by officials and forest restrictions.
  • The movement spread among Bhils and Garasias of Mewar, Sirohi, Dungarpur, Banswara and Idar.
  • Neemda massacre (7 March 1922): at a gathering at Neemda (Pal–Dadhvav, Vijaynagar, in Idar state, now Gujarat), the Mewar Bhil Corps opened fire; many Bhils were killed (Tejawat claimed about 1,200; official figures were much lower). Tejawat escaped wounded.
  • He stayed underground for years, surrendered on Gandhi's advice (1929), was imprisoned until 1936, and later worked with the Praja Mandals.

R18.4 Meenas, Garasias and constructive work

Meenas (eastern Rajasthan, especially Jaipur state) were labelled a "criminal tribe" under the British Criminal Tribes Act (1924) and Jaipur's Jarayam Peshaa law (1930), which forced men to report daily to the police. The Meena Kshatriya Mahasabha and reformers such as Muni Magan Sagar and Lakshmi Narayan Jhariwal campaigned against it; a large conference at Neem ka Thana (1944) demanded repeal. The law was withdrawn after independence (1952).

Constructive workers spread education and reform among tribals:

  • Bhogilal Pandya founded the Vagad Seva Mandir (Dungarpur) and later the Dungarpur Praja Mandal (1944) — called the "Gandhi of Vagad".
  • Manikyalal Verma worked among the Bhils (Khandlai ashram, Dungarpur).
  • Schools for Bhil children were opened by the Seva Sangh.

Rastapal (19 June 1947, Dungarpur): police tried to close a Seva Sangh school run by Nanabhai Khant, who was beaten to death; the teacher Sengabhai was tied to a truck and dragged. A 12-year-old Bhil girl, Kalibai, cut the rope and was shot; she died soon after. She is remembered as a martyr for education.

Accounts differ on who Kalibai's teacher was and how she was related to the school — some name Sengabhai, others link her mainly to Nanabhai Khant. In an exam, state that she tried to save Sengabhai, the teacher at Nanabhai Khant's school.

4What historians say

Studies by Ram Pande, Shankar Sahai Saxena and anthropological work on Bhil society describe these movements. Historians such as David Hardiman (on Devi movements in western India) show that tribal reform movements often mixed religious revival, temperance and political protest. The size of the Mangarh and Neemda massacres is disputed; official records give low figures, oral tradition high ones.

  • The Mer Battalion was raised at Beawar (1822).

  • Govind Guru founded the Samp Sabha in 1883.
  • Followers of Govind Guru were called bhagats.
  • The Mangarh (Banswara) massacre took place on 17 November 1913.
  • Motilal Tejawat began the Eki movement in 1921 at Matrikundia (Chittorgarh).
  • Motilal Tejawat was called "Bawji" by the Bhils.
  • Mewar Pukar was a 21-point memorandum to the Maharana of Mewar (1921).

Exam traps

  • Mangarh (1913) — Govind Guru; Neemda (1922) — Motilal Tejawat.

  • The Eki movement began at Matrikundia (Chittorgarh), not Mangarh.
  • Mewar Pukar was a list of demands, not a newspaper.
  • Mer Battalion — Beawar; Mewar Bhil Corps — Kherwara.

Govind Guru founded the Samp Sabha (1883) among the Bhils of Dungarpur and Banswara. He urged them to give up liquor and meat, educate children and refuse begar and unjust taxes. Alarmed rulers and the British fired on his followers at Mangarh (17 November 1913). He combined social reform with resistance.

  5. Where did the Eki movement begin?

    Answer

    Matrikundia (Chittorgarh), 1921.

  6. What was the Mewar Pukar?

    Answer

    A 21-point memorandum of Bhil grievances presented to the Maharana (1921).

  7. Where did the Neemda massacre take place?

    Answer

    Neemda, Vijaynagar, in Idar state (now Gujarat), 7 March 1922.

  8. Which law targeted the Meenas of Jaipur?

    Answer

    The Jarayam Peshaa law (1930), under the Criminal Tribes system.

  9. Who was Kalibai?

    Answer

    A Bhil girl shot at Rastapal (1947) while trying to save her teacher Sengabhai.

Unit R19 · c. 1885 – 1947

Political Awakening: Press, Organisations, Revolutionaries

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR5 Political and social condition in the 18th–19th centuries; peasant and tribal movements in the 20th century; Praja Mandal movements and mass awakening; integration of Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH3 19th–20th century: Revolt of 1857, peasant and tribal movements, political awakening, mass movements, integration

Topics this unit covers

  • Political awakening in Rajasthan
  • Newspapers and organisations
  • Revolutionaries of Rajasthan

RAS Mains 2016 asked a 10-mark question on the causes of political awakening in early 20th-century Rajasthan. Newspapers (founder–paper–place) and revolutionaries (Barhath family, Kharwa, Sethi, Pathik, Gopa) recur in Prelims.

2The big picture

Political awakening came late to Rajasthan, because most of it was ruled by princes under British protection, with little press freedom, education or political organisation. Ajmer–Merwara, the only British-ruled area, became the hub: newspapers, schools and the national movement reached the princely states through it.

Three currents built awareness between 1900 and 1930: (1) revolutionaries such as Kesari Singh Barhath, Rao Gopal Singh Kharwa and Arjunlal Sethi; (2) newspapers and journalists such as Vijay Singh Pathik and Haribhau Upadhyay; and (3) organisations — the Rajasthan Seva Sangh, the Rajputana Madhya Bharat Sabha and later the All India States People's Conference. Together with the peasant and tribal movements (R17, R18) they prepared the ground for the Praja Mandals (R20).

For the 2016-type question, organise causes under: education and reform, press, economic distress, national and international events, local movements, and organisations.

  1. 1849Majhar-ul-Sarur, Bharatpur: early newspaper of Rajasthan
  2. 1885Rajputana Gazette, Ajmer
  3. 1899–1900Great famine ("Chhappaniya akal")
  4. 1903Kesari Singh Barhath's "Chetavani ra Chungatya" to Maharana Fateh Singh
  5. 1907Arjunlal Sethi's Vardhaman Vidyalaya, Jaipur; Sarvahitkarini Sabha, Churu
  6. 1912Hardinge bomb: Zorawar Singh and Pratap Singh Barhath involved
  7. 1915Planned armed rising: Rao Gopal Singh Kharwa and Vijay Singh Pathik
  8. 1918Pratap Singh Barhath dies in Bareilly jail
  9. 1919Rajasthan Seva Sangh founded (Wardha; moved to Ajmer)
  10. 1920Rajasthan Kesari (Pathik)
  11. 1927All India States People's Conference; Tyag Bhoomi (Ajmer)
  12. 26 Jan 1930Tricolour hoisted at Churu (Dhandhan tower)
  13. 1936Navjyoti, Ajmer
  14. 1938Haripura Congress: states' people to form their own bodies
  15. 4 Apr 1946Sagarmal Gopa dies in Jaisalmer jail

3Core notes

R19.1 Causes of political awakening

  1. Education and social reform: modern schools and colleges, the Arya Samaj (R15), and students educated outside the states.
  2. The press: newspapers from Ajmer and outside (Kanpur's Pratap, Wardha) exposed misrule in the states.
  3. Economic distress: the great famine of 1899–1900, heavy revenue and cesses, war levies during the First World War.
  4. National events: the partition of Bengal and Swadeshi, Gandhi's non-cooperation, the Civil Disobedience movement.
  5. International events: the First World War (returning soldiers), the Russian Revolution.
  6. Local movements: Bijolia and other peasant and tribal struggles.
  7. Marwari merchants and migrants in Calcutta and Bombay funded and publicised reform.
  8. Organisations and the Congress's changing policy towards the states (Haripura, 1938).

R19.2 Revolutionaries

PersonContribution
Kesari Singh Barhath (Shahpura)Wrote Chetavani ra Chungatya (1903), verses urging Maharana Fateh Singh of Mewar not to attend Curzon's Delhi Durbar; the Maharana went to Delhi but did not attend. Later imprisoned in the Kota conspiracy case.
Pratap Singh BarhathSon of Kesari Singh; worked with Rash Behari Bose; involved in the Hardinge bomb (1912) and the Benares conspiracy; died in Bareilly jail (1918) after refusing to betray comrades despite torture
Zorawar Singh BarhathBrother of Kesari Singh; threw the bomb at Viceroy Hardinge in Delhi (23 December 1912), with Pratap Singh; lived underground until his death
Arjunlal Sethi (Jaipur)Founded the Vardhaman Vidyalaya (1907), which trained revolutionaries; arrested in the Nimez (Arrah) case and held at Vellore; later a Congress leader in Ajmer
Rao Gopal Singh Kharwa (Kharwa, Ajmer)Planned, with Rash Behari Bose's network, an armed rising in Rajasthan for 21 February 1915; the plot was discovered and he was detained at Tatgarh
Vijay Singh Pathik (Bhoop Singh)Born in Bulandshahr; Kharwa's associate in 1915; escaped from Tatgarh and adopted the name "Pathik"; then led Bijolia (R17), founded the Rajasthan Seva Sangh and newspapers
Damodar Das Rathi (Beawar)Merchant who financed revolutionaries
Sagarmal Gopa (Jaisalmer)Wrote Jaisalmer ka Gundaraj exposing misrule; imprisoned and tortured; burnt to death in jail (4 April 1946)

R19.3 The press

PaperYear, placeFounder / editor
Majhar-ul-Sarur1849, BharatpurEarly newspaper of Rajasthan (Urdu–Hindi)
Rajputana Gazette1885, Ajmer—
Rajasthan Samachar1889, AjmerMunshi Samarthdan
Rajasthan Kesari1920, WardhaVijay Singh Pathik (supported by Jamnalal Bajaj)
Naveen Rajasthan (later Tarun Rajasthan)1922, AjmerVijay Singh Pathik
Tyag Bhoomi1927, AjmerHaribhau Upadhyay
Navjyoti1936, AjmerRamnarayan Chaudhary; later Durga Prasad Chaudhary
Lokvani1943, JaipurJaipur Praja Mandal circle

States often banned these papers; editors were jailed. Outside papers such as Pratap (Ganesh Shankar Vidyarthi, Kanpur) carried Rajasthan news when local ones could not.

R19.4 Organisations

  • Sarvahitkarini Sabha (Churu, 1907): Swami Gopaldas and Kanhaiyalal Dhandh; education and reform. On 26 January 1930 they hoisted the tricolour on the Dhandhan tower in Churu.
  • Rajputana Madhya Bharat Sabha (1918–19): formed at the time of the Delhi Congress session; Ganesh Shankar Vidyarthi and Jamnalal Bajaj were prominent; raised the problems of states' people.
  • Rajasthan Seva Sangh (1919, Wardha; moved to Ajmer 1920): Vijay Singh Pathik, Ramnarayan Chaudhary and Haribhau Upadhyay; coordinated Bijolia, Begun and the Bhil movements; split in the late 1920s.
  • Marwar: Marwar Seva Sangh (1920), Marwar Hitkarini Sabha (1923–24) and the Marwar Youth League (1931) under Jai Narayan Vyas.
  • All India States People's Conference (AISPC, 1927, Bombay): national body for the states; its Rajputana regional council coordinated local work; its 1945–46 session was held at Udaipur under Nehru.
  • Congress policy: at first the Congress kept out of the states. At Haripura (1938) it said states' people should organise their own bodies; at Tripuri (1939) it gave more open support. This led to the formation of Praja Mandals in almost every state (R20).

4What historians say

Standard works: Shankar Sahai Saxena and Pema Ram on Rajasthan's freedom movement; R.K. Saxena; Rima Hooja. Historians note that awakening in the states followed a different path from British India: it began with social reform, peasant grievances and press campaigns, and only later took the form of demands for responsible government. Ajmer's role as a channel for nationalist ideas is widely stressed.

  • Arjunlal Sethi founded the Vardhaman Vidyalaya in Jaipur (1907).
  • Rao Gopal Singh Kharwa planned an armed rising for 21 February 1915.
  • Vijay Singh Pathik's real name was Bhoop Singh.
  • Sagarmal Gopa wrote Jaisalmer ka Gundaraj and died in jail on 4 April 1946.

Exam traps

  • Kesari Singh (father) wrote Chetavani ra Chungatya; Pratap Singh (son) died in Bareilly jail; Zorawar Singh (brother) threw the Hardinge bomb.
  • Rajasthan Kesari was started at Wardha, not Ajmer.
  • Pathik was from Bulandshahr (UP); Bhoop Singh was his real name.
  • Haripura (1938) — states' people to organise separately; Tripuri (1939) — fuller support.
  • Sagarmal Gopa was from Jaisalmer, not Jodhpur.

7Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words · RAS Mains 2016 What were the causes of political awakening in Rajasthan in the early 20th century?

How to frame it

  • Education and reform
  • Press
  • Economic distress
  • National and international events
  • Local movements and organisations
Model answer (87 words)

Modern education and the Arya Samaj spread new ideas. Newspapers — Rajasthan Kesari, Tyag Bhoomi, Navjyoti, and Kanpur's Pratap — exposed misrule. The 1899–1900 famine, heavy cesses and war levies caused distress. Swadeshi, non-cooperation, the First World War and the Russian Revolution inspired people. Revolutionaries such as the Barhaths, Kharwa and Sethi set examples of sacrifice. Peasant and tribal movements (Bijolia, Eki) mobilised the masses. Organisations — the Rajasthan Seva Sangh and AISPC — and the Congress's Haripura resolution channelled this awareness into the Praja Mandal movements.

5 marks · 50 words Describe the contribution of the Barhath family to the freedom struggle.

How to frame it

  • Kesari Singh
  • Pratap Singh
  • Zorawar Singh
Model answer (47 words)

Kesari Singh Barhath's Chetavani ra Chungatya (1903) dissuaded Maharana Fateh Singh from attending Curzon's durbar; he was later imprisoned. His brother Zorawar Singh took part in the Hardinge bomb attack (1912). His son Pratap Singh, a revolutionary in Rash Behari Bose's circle, died in Bareilly jail (1918).

Unit R20 · 1931 – 1948

Praja Mandal Movements

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR5 Political and social condition in the 18th–19th centuries; peasant and tribal movements in the 20th century; Praja Mandal movements and mass awakening; integration of Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH3 19th–20th century: Revolt of 1857, peasant and tribal movements, political awakening, mass movements, integration

Topics this unit covers

  • Praja Mandal movements in the princely states: founders, years, events

Tier A, asked repeatedly: RAS Mains 2023 (Jaipur Praja Mandal's attitude to Quit India, 5 marks), 2013 (same theme, 2 marks), 2016 Special (features of the Praja Mandal movement, 10 marks). Prelims asks founder–state–year (Jhalawar 2023, Jodhpur 2024).

2The big picture

In British India, the national movement fought the colonial government directly. In Rajputana, most people lived in about nineteen princely states ruled by their own Maharajas and Maharanas under British protection, where they faced two masters: the ruler and his jagirdars at home, and the British behind them. There were no elected assemblies, little freedom of speech or press, and peasants paid heavy rent, cesses (lag-baag) and forced labour (begar). Praja Mandals ("people's associations") were the political organisations through which the people of these states demanded change, mostly between 1931 and 1945.

Their core demand was responsible government under the ruler, with civil liberties and relief from begar and cesses. They grew rapidly after the Congress's Haripura resolution (1938) told the people of the states to organise themselves. Rulers banned and repressed them, but by 1947 they had become the main political force in the states and pressed the rulers to join India. Their leaders (Manikyalal Verma, Hiralal Shastri, Jai Narayan Vyas, Gokulbhai Bhatt) became the first leaders of Rajasthan (R21).

Quit India (1942) is the test case examiners like: the Mewar and Marwar Praja Mandals joined it; the Jaipur Praja Mandal, under a "gentleman's agreement", did not, and a breakaway group (Azad Morcha) did. For the 10-mark "features" question, organise your answer by aims, methods, leadership, link with the Congress, limits and outcome. For Prelims, learn each state's founder, year and one event.

  1. 1927All India States People's Conference (AISPC) founded (R19)
  2. 1931Jaipur Praja Mandal founded (Kapoorchand Patni); Marwar Youth League
  3. 1934Hadoti Praja Mandal; Marwar Praja Mandal
  4. 1936Bikaner Praja Mandal (Calcutta); Dholpur Praja Mandal
  5. 1936–38Jaipur Praja Mandal reorganised under Jamnalal Bajaj
  6. Feb 1938Haripura Congress: states' people to form their own bodies
  7. 1938Mewar Praja Mandal (24 April); Marwar Lok Parishad (16 May); Alwar, Bharatpur, Shahpura
  8. 1939Tripuri Congress; Jaipur satyagraha; Kota, Kishangarh, Sirohi Praja Mandals
  9. 1942Quit India: Jaipur gentleman's agreement; Azad Morcha; Bikaner Praja Parishad; Bal Mukund Bissa dies
  10. 1943–45Banswara, Dungarpur, Pratapgarh, Jaisalmer Praja Mandals
  11. Dec 1945–Jan 1946AISPC session at Udaipur under Nehru
  12. 1946Jhalawar Praja Mandal; Sagarmal Gopa dies in Jaisalmer jail
  13. 30 June 1946Raisinghnagar: Birbal Singh shot (Bikaner)
  14. 11 Apr 1947Tasimo (Dholpur): two killed defending the flag
  15. 14 Aug 1947Shahpura gets full responsible government

3Core notes

R20.1 Why Praja Mandals arose

The Praja Mandals did not appear from nothing. Three earlier currents fed them. The peasant movements of Bijolia, Begun and Shekhawati (R17) and the tribal movements of the Bhils (R18) had shown that ordinary people could organise against begar, cesses and harsh jagirdars. Political awakening through newspapers, schools and bodies such as the Rajasthan Seva Sangh and the Rajputana Madhya Bharat Sabha (R19) had created a small class of educated, politically aware men and women. And the national movement in British India, especially the non-cooperation and civil disobedience campaigns, showed what mass action could achieve.

Yet political work in the states was hard. The rulers had absolute power within their states, were protected by the British, and treated political meetings as sedition. Activists were expelled, newspapers banned and leaders jailed. Many organisations were therefore founded outside the state concerned (the Bikaner Praja Mandal in Calcutta, the Jaisalmer one at Jodhpur) or in British-ruled Ajmer.

The Congress and the princely states. The princes ruled under British paramountcy: the British controlled their defence and foreign relations, and in return protected them. For two decades the Congress followed a policy of non-intervention in the states: it did not want to fight the princes as well as the British, and it hoped they might side with the nation. The All India States People's Conference (1927) gave the states' people a national platform, but the Congress itself held back. At Haripura (February 1938) it declared that the states were part of India and supported the demand for responsible government, but said the struggle in each state must be led by the people of that state, through their own organisations rather than in the Congress's name. This was the signal: Praja Mandals sprang up in state after state in 1938–39. At Tripuri (1939) the Congress moved closer, and in 1939 Nehru became president of the AISPC, linking the two struggles.

R20.2 Aims, methods and features

Responsible government. Responsible government means a government answerable to an elected legislature: ministers are chosen from among the people's representatives and must resign if they lose the legislature's support, as in Britain. The Praja Mandals asked for this under the ruler: the Maharaja would remain as a constitutional head, as the British king did, while elected ministers governed. This was a moderate demand, and it explains much about the movement: it was not anti-monarchy at first, so it could negotiate with rulers (as in Jaipur), and only in 1946–47, when independence was certain, did merger with India become the goal.

Aims. Responsible government under the ruler; civil liberties (freedom of speech, press and association); abolition of begar (forced labour) and lag-baag (cesses); reduction of land revenue and reform of the jagirdari system; and education and social reform.

Features (the 2016 Mains question):

  1. Constitutional and non-violent: petitions, public meetings, satyagraha and other Gandhian methods.
  2. Not anti-monarchy at first: they demanded responsible government under the ruler; only in 1946–47 did integration and merger become the aim.
  3. Middle-class leadership of lawyers, teachers, journalists and merchants, with growing peasant participation as the Praja Mandals took up the grievances of the earlier peasant movements.
  4. Linked to the national movement through the AISPC and the Congress (Haripura, Tripuri), yet separate in organisation.
  5. Women's participation: Narayani Devi Verma, Anjana Devi Chaudhary, Ratan Shastri, Kishori Devi.
  6. Uneven: strong in Mewar, Marwar, Jaipur and Kota; weak or late in small states; with internal splits (Jaipur 1942).
  7. Faced repression: bans, arrests, deaths in custody (Bal Mukund Bissa, Sagarmal Gopa) and firing (Raisinghnagar, Tasimo).
  8. Outcome: they trained the leaders and created the political base for integration and democracy in Rajasthan.

R20.3 Jaipur Praja Mandal and Quit India

The Jaipur Praja Mandal was founded in 1931 by Kapoorchand Patni, but it remained small until 1936–38, when it was reorganised. Jamnalal Bajaj, the Gandhian businessman from a Sikar family, became its president in 1938, with Hiralal Shastri as a leading figure. When the state banned Bajaj's entry (1938–39) and the Praja Mandal itself, it launched a satyagraha in 1939; hundreds were arrested, and the state finally recognised the organisation.

When the Congress launched Quit India in August 1942, the Jaipur Praja Mandal, led by Hiralal Shastri, did not join. The reason was an understanding, the "gentleman's agreement", with Jaipur's prime minister Sir Mirza Ismail and Maharaja Man Singh II. Under it the state would not help the British war effort with men and money beyond its existing obligations, would allow peaceful political activity, and would move towards responsible government.

Not everyone accepted this. Baba Harishchandra, Ramkaran Joshi, Daulatmal Bhandari and others formed the Azad Morcha and joined Quit India; they were arrested. The Azad Morcha later rejoined the Praja Mandal.

Betrayal or good sense? Critics, including the Azad Morcha, saw the decision as a failure to answer the national call at its most important moment. Its defenders argued that Jaipur's situation was different: the enemy here was not the ruler, who had made concessions, and an agreement that won civil liberties and a promise of reform without bloodshed served the people better than a struggle the state would have crushed. A good answer presents both views and notes that the split itself shows the Praja Mandals' dual position, between loyalty to the national movement and negotiation with their own rulers.

R20.4 Mewar, Marwar and Bikaner

Mewar. The Mewar Praja Mandal was founded on 24 April 1938 by Manikyalal Verma, with Balwant Singh Mehta as president, building on the Bijolia tradition (R17). The state banned it at once. Verma, arrested and expelled, published Mewar ka Vartaman Shasan, a criticism of the state's government. The ban was lifted in 1941 and the first session held that year. In Quit India, the Praja Mandal sent the Maharana an ultimatum to break with the British; its leaders were jailed, and students and women joined protests. The AISPC held its session at Udaipur (December 1945–January 1946) under Nehru, a sign of Mewar's importance in the states' movement.

Marwar. Jai Narayan Vyas led a series of bodies as each was banned: the Marwar Youth League (1931), the Marwar Praja Mandal (1934) and the Marwar Lok Parishad (16 May 1938), which demanded responsible government. In 1942 the Lok Parishad launched a responsible-government movement alongside Quit India; the young activist Bal Mukund Bissa died in custody (1942). Jagirdars' violence against Lok Parishad workers at Chandawal (1942) and Dabra (1947) showed how the struggle against the state and against jagirdari ran together (R17). The S.A. Sudhalkar report of 1945 concerned the Jodhpur state (RAS Prelims 2024).

Bikaner. Maharaja Ganga Singh ran one of the most efficient but most authoritarian states. The 1932 Bikaner conspiracy case prosecuted Swami Gopaldas and others for circulating "Bikaner: A Tragedy" during the Round Table Conference. The Bikaner Praja Mandal had to be founded in Calcutta (1936), by Maghraj Pawar and others, and its leaders were expelled. The Bikaner Rajya Praja Parishad (1942), under Raghuvar Dayal Goyal, continued the struggle. At Raisinghnagar (30 June 1946) police fired on a procession; Birbal Singh, carrying the flag, was killed, and became a symbol of the movement.

R20.5 Other states

In the smaller states the pattern repeated: a Praja Mandal formed (often late), was banned, its leaders jailed or expelled, and then, in 1946–47, it pressed for responsible government and merger. The southern Vagad states (Dungarpur, Banswara, Pratapgarh) drew on the Bhil movements of Govind Guru and Motilal Tejawat (R18).

StateBody and yearLeaders and events
KotaHadoti Praja Mandal 1934; Kota Praja Mandal 1939Nainuram Sharma, Abhinna Hari; in 1942 protesters briefly took over the city administration
Jhalawar1946Mangilal Bhavya, Kanhaiyalal Mittal, Makbul Alam
Alwar1938Harinarayan Sharma, Kunj Bihari Lal Modi
Bharatpur1938 (Praja Parishad 1940)Kishan Lal Joshi, Jugal Kishore Chaturvedi
Dholpur1936Krishnadutt Paliwal; Tasimo firing (11 April 1947): Chhatar Singh and Panchamsingh killed defending the flag
Shahpura1938Ramesh Chandra Ojha, Abhay Singh Dangi; first state with full responsible government (14 August 1947), Gokul Lal Asawa as chief minister
Sirohi1939 (reorganised)Gokulbhai Bhatt, called the "Gandhi of Rajasthan"
Kishangarh1939Kantilal Chauthani, Jamal Shah
Banswara1943Bhupendra Nath Trivedi
Dungarpur1944Bhogilal Pandya; Rastapal (1947, R18)
Pratapgarh1945Amritlal Payak, Chunnilal
Jaisalmer1945 (formed at Jodhpur)Meetha Lal Vyas; Sagarmal Gopa's death in jail (1946)

From Praja Mandal to Rajasthan. When independence came, the rulers had to decide whether to accede to India. The Praja Mandals, now large and organised, pressed them to join and demanded popular ministries. Small Shahpura led the way with full responsible government on the eve of independence; in other states interim ministries with Praja Mandal members were formed in 1947–48. When the states were merged in stages into Rajasthan (1948–1956), Praja Mandal leaders formed its governments: Gokul Lal Asawa, Manikyalal Verma, Hiralal Shastri and Jai Narayan Vyas all headed ministries in the successive unions (R21). The movement thus turned subjects of princes into citizens of a democracy.

How this unit connects.

  • Before: peasant movements (R17), tribal movements (R18), the press, Seva Sangh, Rajputana Madhya Bharat Sabha and AISPC (R19); British paramountcy and the 1818 treaties (R15); the jagirdari and revenue system (R14, R23).
  • National movement: Gandhian methods and Civil Disobedience (I39, I41); Quit India (I42).
  • After: integration of Rajasthan (R21) and of the princely states across India (I43).
  • People: Manikyalal Verma, Hiralal Shastri, Jai Narayan Vyas, Gokulbhai Bhatt and the women activists (R22).

4What historians say

Studies by Shankar Sahai Saxena, R.K. Saxena, Pema Ram and Rima Hooja. Historians broadly agree that the Praja Mandals were moderate and constitutional, shaped by the Congress's cautious policy towards the states. On Jaipur in 1942, opinions differ: some see Hiralal Shastri's agreement as pragmatic, since it won concessions and avoided repression; others see it as a failure to follow the national call — the Azad Morcha's view.

Exam traps

7Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words · RAS Mains 2016 (Special) Discuss the main features of the Praja Mandal movement in Rajasthan.

How to frame it

  • Aims
  • Methods
  • Leadership and participation
  • Link to national movement
  • Limits
  • Outcome
Model answer (89 words)

Praja Mandals, formed mostly after the Haripura resolution (1938), sought responsible government under the rulers, civil liberties and an end to begar and cesses. Their methods were constitutional and non-violent. Led by middle-class figures — Manikyalal Verma, Hiralal Shastri, Jai Narayan Vyas — they absorbed peasant movements and drew in women. They worked through the AISPC and with the Congress. They were uneven across states and sometimes divided, as in Jaipur in 1942, and faced bans and firing. They created the leadership and popular base for integration and democracy.

10 marks · 100 words Explain how the Congress policy towards the princely states shaped the Praja Mandal movement.

How to frame it

  • Paramountcy and non-intervention
  • AISPC
  • Haripura 1938
  • Tripuri 1939
  • Effects on Praja Mandals
Model answer (96 words)

The princes ruled under British paramountcy, and for two decades the Congress kept out of the states, hoping to avoid fighting the princes too. The AISPC (1927) gave the states' people a platform, but local work stayed weak. At Haripura (1938) the Congress backed responsible government but asked the people of each state to form their own bodies; Praja Mandals then arose across Rajputana in 1938–39. Tripuri (1939) and Nehru's AISPC presidency drew them closer. This explains their moderate demand for responsible government under the ruler, their separate organisation, and their divided response to Quit India.

5 marks · 50 words · RAS Mains 2023 What was the attitude of the Jaipur Praja Mandal towards the Quit India Movement?

How to frame it

  • Gentleman's agreement
  • Terms
  • Azad Morcha
  • Assessment
Model answer (51 words)

Under Hiralal Shastri, the Jaipur Praja Mandal stayed out of Quit India after a "gentleman's agreement" with Prime Minister Mirza Ismail, who promised civil liberties, steps towards responsible government and limited war help. Dissenters led by Baba Harishchandra formed the Azad Morcha and joined the movement. It was pragmatic but criticised.

2 marks · 15 words · RAS Mains 2013 Jaipur Praja Mandal and Quit India — in brief.

How to frame it

  • Answer
Model answer (14 words)

It stayed out under a gentleman's agreement with Mirza Ismail; the Azad Morcha joined.

  • Aims: responsible government under ruler; civil liberties; end begar and lag-baag.
  • Features: constitutional, middle-class led, peasant base, AISPC and Congress link, women, uneven, repressed, led to integration.
  • Jaipur: Patni 1931; Bajaj 1938; Hiralal Shastri; 1942 gentleman's agreement (Mirza Ismail); Azad Morcha (Baba Harishchandra).
  • Mewar: 24 Apr 1938, Manikyalal Verma; Quit India ultimatum; AISPC Udaipur 1945–46.
  • Marwar: Lok Parishad 16 May 1938 (Vyas); Bissa d. 1942; Sudhalkar report 1945 (Jodhpur).
  • Bikaner: Praja Mandal (Calcutta 1936, Maghraj Pawar); Praja Parishad 1942 (Goyal); Raisinghnagar 30 June 1946 (Birbal Singh).

9Self-test

  1. What was the main demand of the Praja Mandals?

    Answer

    Responsible government under the ruler.

  2. What does "responsible government" mean?

    Answer

    A government of ministers answerable to an elected legislature, with the ruler as constitutional head.

  3. Why did the Congress keep out of the princely states before 1938?

    Answer

    It followed non-intervention, not wanting to fight the princes as well as the British under paramountcy.

  4. What did the Haripura resolution (1938) say about the states?

    Answer

    It supported responsible government but asked the states' people to form their own organisations.

  5. Why were some Praja Mandals founded outside their own state?

    Answer

    Rulers banned political activity, so Bikaner's was founded in Calcutta and Jaisalmer's at Jodhpur.

  6. Who founded the Jaipur Praja Mandal?

    Answer

    Kapoorchand Patni (1931).

  7. With whom did Hiralal Shastri make the 1942 "gentleman's agreement"?

    Answer

    Sir Mirza Ismail, Jaipur's prime minister.

  8. What was the Azad Morcha?

    Answer

    A breakaway Jaipur group (Baba Harishchandra and others) that joined Quit India.

  9. When and by whom was the Mewar Praja Mandal founded?

    Answer

    24 April 1938, by Manikyalal Verma.

  10. Who led the Marwar Lok Parishad?

    Answer

    Jai Narayan Vyas.

  11. Who was killed at Raisinghnagar in 1946?

    Answer

    Birbal Singh.

  12. Which state first got full responsible government?

    Answer

    Shahpura (14 August 1947).

Unit R21 · March 1948 – November 1956

Integration of Rajasthan (1948–1956)

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR5 Political and social condition in the 18th–19th centuries; peasant and tribal movements in the 20th century; Praja Mandal movements and mass awakening; integration of Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH3 19th–20th century: Revolt of 1857, peasant and tribal movements, political awakening, mass movements, integration

Topics this unit covers

  • Integration of Rajasthan: seven stages, dates, capitals, Rajpramukhs, prime ministers

Tier A. RAS Mains 2021 asked about Sirohi's integration (5 marks); RAS Prelims asked on Matsya (2023), the first Rajpramukh (2023) and Shobharam (2024). The seven stages are a Prelims certainty: learn dates, member states, capital, Rajpramukh, prime minister and who inaugurated each.

2The big picture

In 1947 Rajputana was not one unit but a patchwork: 19 princely states, 3 chiefships (Lawa, Kushalgarh, Neemrana) and the British-ruled province of Ajmer–Merwara. Some states, such as Jaipur, Jodhpur, Bikaner and Udaipur, were large; others were tiny. All acceded to India at independence, but accession only handed over defence, foreign affairs and communications. The real question was how to turn these monarchies into viable, democratic administrative units.

Under Sardar Patel and V.P. Menon of the States Ministry, they were merged step by step, in seven stages from March 1948 to November 1956, into the present state of Rajasthan. The rulers kept their titles and privy purses; the Praja Mandal leaders (R20), who had pressed for merger, formed the new governments. The stages were many because the larger states resisted, the smaller ones were grouped first, and disputes over the capital, posts and borders (Matsya, Sirohi) took time to settle.

Learn the stages as a table: date, members, capital, Rajpramukh, prime minister, inaugurated by. The two "special" cases are Matsya (merged later after an inquiry) and Sirohi (divided in 1950, reunited in 1956), and Sirohi has already been asked in Mains (2021). Understand why it took seven stages; that is the analytical question.

  1. Aug 1947Rajasthan states accede to India
  2. 18 Mar 1948Stage 1: Matsya Union
  3. 25 Mar 1948Stage 2: Rajasthan Union (Purva Rajasthan)
  4. 18 Apr 1948Stage 3: United State of Rajasthan (with Udaipur)
  5. 30 Mar 1949Stage 4: Greater Rajasthan (Jaipur, Jodhpur, Bikaner, Jaisalmer) — Rajasthan Day
  6. 15 May 1949Stage 5: Matsya Union merged
  7. 26 Jan 1950Stage 6: Sirohi (without Abu and Delwara) merged; Rajasthan a Part B state
  8. 1952First general election; Ajmer a Part C state under Haribhau Upadhyay
  9. 1 Nov 1956Stage 7: Ajmer–Merwara, Abu–Delwara, Sunel Tappa added; Sironj transferred to MP

3Core notes

R21.1 Background: from accession to merger

When British rule ended, British paramountcy over the princely states lapsed. In theory each state could join India or Pakistan, or try to stay independent. Most rulers of Rajputana signed the Instrument of Accession quickly; Bikaner's Sadul Singh was among the earliest in India to do so. The exception that worried Delhi was Jodhpur: its young ruler Hanwant Singh, whose state bordered Pakistan, considered offers from Jinnah. Patel and Menon persuaded him, and he acceded in August 1947.

Accession and merger. Accession (1947) meant a ruler handed only three subjects (defence, foreign affairs and communications) to India; inside his state he still ruled. Merger (1948–49) went much further: rulers signed covenants merging their states into a union, gave up all governing power, and handed over their administration to elected ministries. In return they kept their titles, private property and a tax-free annual allowance, the privy purse, guaranteed by the covenants. The senior rulers became Rajpramukh (constitutional head of the union), a post like a governor's. Privy purses and privileges were finally abolished in 1971. Merger was the step that turned subjects of princes into citizens.

Why merge at all? The States Ministry held that only a state with a population of about 10 lakh and a revenue of about ₹1 crore could run a modern administration on its own; smaller states had to combine. Rulers' own plans for a separate Rajputana union under the larger houses (such as Mewar's proposal) came to nothing. Meanwhile the Praja Mandals agitated for popular government and merger, and some rulers, faced with unrest, saw merger as the safer course.

The process went in stages because the states were unequal. Small neighbouring states could be grouped quickly (stages 1 and 2). Large states with proud dynasties (Udaipur, Jaipur, Jodhpur, Bikaner) bargained over who would be Rajpramukh, where the capital would be, and what they would receive. And border regions with ties to neighbouring provinces (Alwar and Bharatpur with Uttar Pradesh; Sirohi with Gujarat) needed inquiries before they were settled.

R21.2 The seven stages

Stage, dateName and membersCapitalRajpramukhPrime ministerInaugurated by
1. 18 Mar 1948Matsya Union: Alwar, Bharatpur, Dholpur, Karauli (name suggested by K.M. Munshi)AlwarUdaybhan Singh (Dholpur)Shobharam KumawatN.V. Gadgil
2. 25 Mar 1948Rajasthan Union (Purva Rajasthan): Kota, Bundi, Jhalawar, Banswara, Dungarpur, Pratapgarh, Kishangarh, Shahpura, Tonk + Kushalgarh chiefshipKotaBhim Singh (Kota)Gokul Lal AsawaN.V. Gadgil
3. 18 Apr 1948United State of Rajasthan: Rajasthan Union + UdaipurUdaipurBhupal Singh (Mewar)Manikyalal VermaJawaharlal Nehru
4. 30 Mar 1949Greater Rajasthan: + Jaipur, Jodhpur, Bikaner, JaisalmerJaipurMan Singh II (Jaipur); Bhupal Singh as MaharajpramukhHiralal ShastriSardar Patel
5. 15 May 1949United State of Greater Rajasthan: + Matsya UnionJaipurMan Singh IIHiralal Shastri—
6. 26 Jan 1950Rajasthan (Part B state): + Sirohi, without Abu and Delwara tehsilsJaipurMan Singh IIHiralal Shastri—
7. 1 Nov 1956States Reorganisation: + Ajmer–Merwara, Abu and Delwara, Sunel Tappa (from Madhya Bharat); Sironj (Kota) to Madhya PradeshJaipurPost abolished; Governor (Gurumukh Nihal Singh)Chief Minister Mohan Lal Sukhadia—

Stages 1–3: the smaller states. The Matsya Union of four eastern states came first (18 March 1948), named after the ancient Matsya janapada of the region on K.M. Munshi's suggestion. Its prime minister, Shobharam Kumawat of Alwar, later served in Hiralal Shastri's cabinet (the RPSC key for 2024 accepts this, but not that he left law practice during Non-Cooperation). A week later nine south-eastern states and the Kushalgarh chiefship formed the Rajasthan Union with Kota as capital and Gokul Lal Asawa, the Shahpura leader, as prime minister. Udaipur had hoped to lead its own union, but under Praja Mandal pressure Maharana Bhupal Singh joined on 18 April 1948; Nehru inaugurated the United State of Rajasthan, with Udaipur as capital, the Maharana as Rajpramukh and Manikyalal Verma as prime minister.

Stage 4: the great states. The decisive step was the entry of the four large western and northern states, Jaipur, Jodhpur, Bikaner and Jaisalmer, which Patel inaugurated on 30 March 1949, now celebrated as Rajasthan Day. To balance the dynasties, Jaipur's Man Singh II became Rajpramukh (the first Rajpramukh of Rajasthan, RAS Prelims 2023), Mewar's Bhupal Singh was honoured as Maharajpramukh, and Hiralal Shastri of the Jaipur Praja Mandal became prime minister. The capital went to Jaipur on the recommendation of a committee under P. Satyanarayan Rao, which spread major offices among other cities to satisfy them; the High Court, for example, went to Jodhpur.

Stages 5–7: settling the edges. In Matsya, opinion in Alwar and Bharatpur was divided between joining Rajasthan and joining Uttar Pradesh, since the region had close ties with its eastern neighbours. The Shankar Rao Deo committee found majority opinion in favour of Rajasthan, and Matsya merged on 15 May 1949 (RAS Prelims 2023). On 26 January 1950, the day the Constitution came into force, Sirohi (minus Abu and Delwara) joined, and Rajasthan became a Part B state headed by a Rajpramukh. Finally, the States Reorganisation Act brought the last changes on 1 November 1956: Ajmer–Merwara, which had been a separate Part C state with Haribhau Upadhyay as chief minister (1952–56), Abu and Delwara, and Sunel Tappa (from Madhya Bharat) were added, while Sironj (an enclave of Kota) went to Madhya Pradesh. The post of Rajpramukh was abolished and Rajasthan received a Governor, Gurumukh Nihal Singh, with Mohan Lal Sukhadia as chief minister.

Part A, B and C states. The Constitution of 1950 grouped India's units into three classes. Part A states were the former British provinces, headed by a Governor. Part B states were the unions of princely states, such as Rajasthan, headed by a Rajpramukh drawn from the princes. Part C states were smaller units, such as Ajmer, administered under the Centre's control. The Seventh Amendment (1956), which followed the States Reorganisation Commission, abolished this classification: there were now only states and union territories, and every state had a Governor. That is why 1956 ends both the integration of Rajasthan and the office of Rajpramukh.

R21.3 The Sirohi question

Sirohi (RAS Mains 2021) was the most disputed case. It was the home of Gokulbhai Bhatt, the "Gandhi of Rajasthan", who led its Praja Mandal. But Gujarati leaders claimed it, because of Mount Abu, the hill station and Jain pilgrimage centre (Dilwara temples), and because of the region's links with Gujarat.

In 1948 the States Ministry placed Sirohi under the administration of the Bombay government. On 26 January 1950 it was divided: the Abu Road and Delwara tehsils, including Mount Abu, went to Bombay, and the rest of Sirohi joined Rajasthan. Rajasthan's leaders, led by Gokulbhai Bhatt, protested strongly, arguing that Abu was historically, culturally and linguistically part of Rajasthan. The States Reorganisation Commission (under Fazl Ali, 1953–55) agreed and recommended returning Abu and Delwara, and they rejoined Rajasthan on 1 November 1956. Sirohi's integration was thus completed in two steps, six years apart.

After integration. The new state had to combine nineteen different administrations, laws, revenue systems and services into one, and to deal with the jagirdari system that the Praja Mandals had fought. Hiralal Shastri (1949–51) was followed by short ministries; after the first general election (1952), Tika Ram Paliwal and then Jai Narayan Vyas led the state, and from 1954 Mohan Lal Sukhadia began a long term as chief minister. The Rajasthan Land Reforms and Resumption of Jagirs Act (1952) and the Rajasthan Tenancy Act (1955) ended the jagirdari order (R23).

How this unit connects.

  • Before: the 1818 treaties and British paramountcy (R15); the Praja Mandals and their leaders (R20); peasant movements against jagirdari (R17).
  • India: integration of the princely states by Patel and Menon (I43); linguistic reorganisation and the States Reorganisation Commission (I44).
  • After: land reform and the end of jagirdari; new administration and revenue (R23).
  • People: Manikyalal Verma, Hiralal Shastri, Gokul Lal Asawa, Gokulbhai Bhatt, Jai Narayan Vyas, Mohan Lal Sukhadia (R22).
  • Earlier history of the states: Mewar (R07, R08), Marwar (R09), Bikaner, Jaisalmer and Sirohi (R10), Jaipur and Alwar (R11), Hadoti and the eastern states (R12); the ancient Matsya janapada (R04).

4What historians say

V.P. Menon's The Story of the Integration of the Indian States gives the States Ministry's account. Rajasthani historians (Rima Hooja, R.S. Darda, Shankar Sahai Saxena) emphasise the role of the Praja Mandals and popular pressure, and the difficulty of balancing the claims of the large states (Jaipur, Jodhpur, Udaipur) over the capital and posts.

Exam traps

  • Rajasthan Day (30 March) marks stage 4, not stage 1 or 7.
  • Nehru inaugurated stage 3; Patel inaugurated stage 4; N.V. Gadgil stages 1 and 2.
  • Sunel Tappa came to Rajasthan; Sironj went to Madhya Pradesh (1956).
  • Abu and Delwara went to Bombay in 1950 and came back in 1956.
  • The first prime minister of the United State of Rajasthan was Manikyalal Verma; of Greater Rajasthan, Hiralal Shastri.
  • Accession (1947) covered only defence, foreign affairs and communications; merger (1948–49) ended the rulers' power.
  • Shobharam was in Hiralal Shastri's cabinet; the RPSC key (2024) did not accept that he left law practice during Non-Cooperation.

7Mains answer writing

5 marks · 50 words · RAS Mains 2021 Write a short note on the integration of Sirohi in United Rajasthan.

How to frame it

  • Claim
  • 1948
  • 1950 division
  • 1956
Model answer (53 words)

Gujarati leaders claimed Sirohi for Mount Abu. In 1948 it was placed under Bombay's administration. On 26 January 1950 it was divided: Abu Road and Delwara went to Bombay, the rest joined Rajasthan. After protests led by Gokulbhai Bhatt, the States Reorganisation Commission restored Abu and Delwara to Rajasthan on 1 November 1956.

10 marks · 100 words Why did the integration of Rajasthan take seven stages?

How to frame it

  • Unequal states
  • Small states first
  • Bargaining by large states
  • Border disputes
  • Constitutional change 1956
Model answer (92 words)

Rajputana's 19 states were very unequal. Small neighbouring states could be grouped quickly: Matsya and the Rajasthan Union in March 1948. Large dynasties bargained over status, capital and privy purses: Udaipur joined only in April 1948, and Jaipur, Jodhpur, Bikaner and Jaisalmer in March 1949, with Man Singh II as Rajpramukh, Bhupal Singh as Maharajpramukh and Jaipur as capital. Border areas needed inquiries: Matsya after the Shankar Rao Deo committee (May 1949), Sirohi divided in 1950. Ajmer–Merwara, Abu and Sunel came only with the 1956 reorganisation, which also abolished Part B states.

10 marks · 100 words Describe the stages of the integration of Rajasthan.

How to frame it

  • Background
  • Stages 1–3
  • Stage 4
  • Stages 5–7
  • Role of Patel and Praja Mandals
Model answer (87 words)

Rajputana's 19 states, three chiefships and Ajmer–Merwara were merged in seven stages. The Matsya Union (Alwar, Bharatpur, Dholpur, Karauli) formed on 18 March 1948; the Rajasthan Union of nine south-eastern states on 25 March; Udaipur joined on 18 April. On 30 March 1949 Jaipur, Jodhpur, Bikaner and Jaisalmer formed Greater Rajasthan, with Jaipur as capital. Matsya joined on 15 May 1949, Sirohi (without Abu) on 26 January 1950, and Ajmer–Merwara, Abu–Delwara and Sunel Tappa on 1 November 1956. Patel, Menon and Praja Mandal pressure made it possible.

2 marks · 15 words What was the Matsya Union?

How to frame it

  • Members, date
Model answer (12 words)

First stage (18 March 1948): Alwar, Bharatpur, Dholpur and Karauli, capital Alwar.

8One-screen revision card

  • Accession (1947: defence, foreign affairs, communications) → merger (1948–49: covenants, privy purses, Rajpramukh).
  • 1 Matsya 18 Mar 1948 (Alwar, Bharatpur, Dholpur, Karauli; Alwar; Udaybhan Singh; Shobharam; Gadgil; name by Munshi).
  • 2 Rajasthan Union 25 Mar 1948 (9 states + Kushalgarh; Kota; Bhim Singh; Gokul Lal Asawa; Gadgil).
  • 3 United State 18 Apr 1948 (+ Udaipur; Udaipur; Bhupal Singh; Manikyalal Verma; Nehru).
  • 4 Greater Rajasthan 30 Mar 1949 (+ Jaipur, Jodhpur, Bikaner, Jaisalmer; Jaipur; Man Singh II; Hiralal Shastri; Patel) — Rajasthan Day; Satyanarayan Rao committee.
  • 5 + Matsya 15 May 1949 (Shankar Rao Deo committee). 6 + Sirohi (minus Abu, Delwara) 26 Jan 1950; Part B state.
  • 7 1 Nov 1956: + Ajmer–Merwara, Abu–Delwara, Sunel Tappa; − Sironj; Rajpramukh abolished; Governor Gurumukh Nihal Singh; CM Sukhadia.

9Self-test

  1. How many princely states were there in Rajputana in 1947?

    Answer

    19, plus 3 chiefships and Ajmer–Merwara.

  2. How did accession differ from merger?

    Answer

    Accession gave India only defence, foreign affairs and communications; merger ended the rulers' governing power in return for privy purses and titles.

  3. Why did the larger states join later than the smaller ones?

    Answer

    They bargained over status, the post of Rajpramukh, the capital and privy purses.

  4. Which states formed the Matsya Union?

    Answer

    Alwar, Bharatpur, Dholpur and Karauli.

  5. What was the capital of the Rajasthan Union (stage 2)?

    Answer

    Kota.

  6. Who inaugurated the United State of Rajasthan (stage 3)?

    Answer

    Jawaharlal Nehru.

  7. Who was the first Rajpramukh of Rajasthan?

    Answer

    Sawai Man Singh II of Jaipur.

  8. Why is 30 March celebrated as Rajasthan Day?

    Answer

    Greater Rajasthan was formed on 30 March 1949.

  9. Why was the Matsya Union's merger delayed, and how was it settled?

    Answer

    Opinion in Alwar and Bharatpur was divided between Rajasthan and Uttar Pradesh; the Shankar Rao Deo committee found in favour of Rajasthan.

  10. Which parts of Sirohi went to Bombay in 1950, and when did they return?

    Answer

    Abu Road and Delwara tehsils; 1 November 1956.

  11. What was added and removed on 1 November 1956?

    Answer

    Added Ajmer–Merwara, Abu–Delwara, Sunel Tappa; removed Sironj.

  12. Why did the post of Rajpramukh end in 1956?

    Answer

    The Seventh Amendment abolished Part B states; every state now had a Governor.

Unit R22 · Whole syllabus

Leading Personalities and Master Chronology

1Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims 2026
  • PR9 Leading personalities of Rajasthan
Mains 2026 (GS Paper I)
  • RH1 Prehistoric cultures, ancient sites; political and cultural achievements of rulers (to 18th century)
  • RH3 19th–20th century: Revolt of 1857, peasant and tribal movements, political awakening, mass movements, integration

Topics this unit covers

  • Important personalities of Rajasthan history
  • Chronology

A revision unit. Mains often asks short notes on a personality (2 or 5 marks); Prelims tests person–role matching. Use the master chronology the week before the exam.

2The big picture

This last unit brings the notebook together in two tools:

  1. Personalities — one-line identities for the figures most often asked, grouped by period, with the unit where each is covered in detail.
  2. Master chronology — the whole of Rajasthan history on one timeline, from Bagor to 1956.

For a 2-mark "who was" question, write: identity (who, where, when) + the single most important contribution. That is exactly what the tables below give you.

  1. c. 5000 BCEBagor: early animal domestication
  2. c. 2600–1900 BCEKalibangan (Harappan)
  3. c. 3000–1500 BCEAhar–Banas culture; Ganeshwar copper
  4. 6th c. BCEMatsya Mahajanapada (Bairat)
  5. 3rd c. BCEAshokan edicts at Bairat
  6. c. 730–1018Gurjara-Pratiharas
  7. c. 1113Ajmer founded
  8. 1191–92Tarain I and II
  9. 1301–11Khalji conquests: Ranthambhor, Chittor, Siwana, Jalore
  10. 1433–68Rana Kumbha
  11. 1459Jodhpur founded
  12. 1488Bikaner founded
  13. 1527Khanwa
  14. 1544Giri-Sumel
  15. 1562Amber–Mughal alliance
  16. 1568Fall of Chittor to Akbar
  17. 1576Haldighati
  18. 1615Mewar–Mughal treaty
  19. 1679–1707Rathore war
  20. 1727Jaipur founded
  21. 1734Hurda conference; Marathas enter Rajasthan
  22. 1817–18Treaties with the East India Company
  23. 1857Revolt: Nasirabad, Auwa, Kota
  24. 1897–1941Bijolia peasant movement
  25. 1913Mangarh massacre
  26. 1938Mewar Praja Mandal, Marwar Lok Parishad
  27. 1942Quit India; Jaipur gentleman's agreement
  28. 30 Mar 1949Greater Rajasthan (Rajasthan Day)
  29. 1 Nov 1956Present Rajasthan formed
